Deuteronomy — Saints Bible cross-references
Completed: Deuteronomy 1:1–34:12. Book initial curated pass complete. Overall next: 1 Kings 1:1.
959 completed source verses; 1090 completed cross-reference pairs.
Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.
Reference edition: Saints Holy Bible 70-book edition, pinned Saints-Translation English records. Greek-based source numbering is retained.

Deuteronomy 1:1
These are the words which Moses spoke unto all Israel beyond the Jordan, in the wilderness toward the west, near the Red Sea, between Paran, Tophel, Laban, Aulon, and Katachrysea.
  Deuteronomy 1:5 — Same-chapter discourse setting: The opening locates Moses beyond the Jordan, and the later introductory notice specifies Moab as the place where he explained the law; the additional geographical names are not silently equated with other sites.

Deuteronomy 1:2
It is a journey of eleven days from Horeb by the way of Mount Seir unto Kadesh-barnea.
  Deuteronomy 1:19 — Same-chapter route and narrated arrival: The header states an eleven-day journey from Horeb by Mount Seir to Kadesh-barnea; the recital subsequently describes departure from Horeb and arrival at Kadesh-barnea, without saying the whole narrated interval lasted eleven days.

Deuteronomy 1:3
And it came to pass in the fortieth year, in the eleventh month, on the first day of the month, that Moses spoke unto all the sons of Israel, according to all that the Lord had commanded him concerning them.
  Numbers 33:38 — Fortieth-year chronological notices: Moses speaks on the first day of the eleventh month in the fortieth year; Aaron dies on the first day of the fifth month in that year. These are distinct dated events, not identical dates.

Deuteronomy 1:4
After he had smitten Sihon king of the Amorites, who dwelt in Heshbon, and Og king of Bashan, who dwelt in Ashtaroth and in Edrei,
  Numbers 21:23-25 — Sihon conquest recalled: The discourse follows the defeat of Sihon of Heshbon; Numbers narrates Sihon refusing passage, Israel defeating him, and Israel taking the Amorite towns including Heshbon.
  Numbers 21:33-35 — Og conquest recalled: The introductory notice names the defeat of Og of Bashan associated with Ashtaroth and Edrei; Numbers narrates Og coming to battle at Edrei and the defeat of him, his sons, and his people.

Deuteronomy 1:5
beyond the Jordan, in the land of Moab, Moses began to make this law plain, saying:
  Deuteronomy 4:44-46 — Repeated law-discourse frame: Moses explains the law beyond the Jordan in Moab; the later frame again identifies Moses setting forth the law beyond the Jordan, in the ravine near the house of Peor and Sihon’s former territory.

Deuteronomy 1:6
The Lord our God spoke unto us in Horeb, saying: Let it suffice you to have dwelt in this mountain.
  Deuteronomy 1:19 — Same-chapter departure command and execution: The command says that the stay at Horeb has been sufficient; Moses subsequently recounts the actual departure from Horeb and journey through the fearful wilderness to Kadesh-barnea.

Deuteronomy 1:7
Turn and set forth, and enter the hill country of the Amorites and all the neighbouring places of Arabah, the hill country and the plain, the southwest and the seacoast, the land of the Canaanites and Anti-Lebanon, as far as the great river Euphrates.
  Deuteronomy 1:19-21 — Same-chapter commanded destination and arrival: The command directs Israel toward the Amorite hill country among the wider named regions; the recital then reports arrival at that hill country and commands possession. It does not claim every region extending to the Euphrates was already occupied.

Deuteronomy 1:8
Behold, I have delivered the land before you. Enter and inherit the land which I sware unto your fathers, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, to give unto them and unto their seed after them.
  Exodus 6:8 — Patriarchal land oath and inheritance: Both passages name Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the Lord’s undertaking to give their land to Israel as an inheritance; Deuteronomy urges possession of the land promised in the earlier assurance.

Deuteronomy 1:9
And I spoke unto you at that time, saying: I shall not be able to bear you alone.
  Numbers 11:14 — Moses unable to bear the people alone: Moses’ recollection that he could not bear Israel alone corresponds to his explicit complaint that the people’s burden was too heavy; Numbers places that complaint in the meat-demand episode.
  Exodus 18:18 — Judicial burden requiring assistance: The recollection of inability to bear the people alone matches Jethro’s specific warning that judging the people alone would exhaust Moses and the people. The passages retain their different speakers.

Deuteronomy 1:10
The Lord your God has multiplied you, and behold, you are this day as the stars of heaven in multitude.
  Genesis 15:5 — Descendants compared with heavenly stars: Moses describes Israel’s present multiplication as the stars of heaven; the earlier promise directs Abram to count the stars and says his seed will be like them.
  Genesis 22:17 — Star-numbered descendants and blessing: Israel’s multiplication like the heavenly stars recalls the promise to bless Abraham and multiply his seed like the stars; this comparison does not claim an exact numerical census of the stars.

Deuteronomy 1:11
The Lord, the God of your fathers, make you a thousand times as many as you are, and bless you, as He has spoken unto you.
  Genesis 22:17-18 — Prayer for continued ancestral blessing: Moses asks the God of the fathers to multiply and bless Israel; the ancestral promise joins blessing with multiplication of Abraham’s seed. The requested thousandfold increase belongs to Moses’ prayer, not to an identical number in Genesis.

Deuteronomy 1:12
How shall I alone be able to bear your trouble, and your resistance, and your disputes?
  Exodus 18:18-22 — Burden of disputes shared with appointed judges: Moses asks how he can bear the people’s trouble and disputes alone; Jethro identifies the heavy judicial burden and proposes subordinate judges who will bear it with him.

Deuteronomy 1:13
Choose for yourselves wise men, men of knowledge and understanding, for your tribes, and I will appoint them to rule over you.
  Exodus 18:21 — Selection of qualified tribal leaders: Both passages require qualified men to assist government, but preserve their selection procedures: Moses asks the people to supply wise and understanding men by tribes, while Jethro tells Moses to choose able, God-fearing, righteous men.

Deuteronomy 1:14
And you answered me and said: The thing which you have spoken is good to do.
  Deuteronomy 1:13 — Same-chapter proposal and public approval: The people’s declaration that the proposed course is good answers the immediately specified proposal to supply wise and understanding tribal men for Moses to appoint as leaders.

Deuteronomy 1:15
And I took from among you wise men, men of knowledge and understanding, and appointed them to rule over you, captains of thousands, and captains of hundreds, and captains of fifties, and captains of tens, and recording officers for your judges.
  Exodus 18:25 — Matching graded leadership ranks: Both appointments specify leaders of thousands, hundreds, fifties, and tens; Deuteronomy additionally mentions recording officers for the judges, without inserting that additional office into Exodus.

Deuteronomy 1:16
And I commanded your judges at that time, saying: Hear the matters between your brethren, and judge justly between a man and his brother and the sojourner with him.
  Leviticus 24:22 — Impartial judgment for native and foreigner: The judges must hear brothers and judge righteously between a man, his brother, and his resident foreigner; Leviticus explicitly requires one judgment for resident foreigner and local inhabitant.

Deuteronomy 1:17
You shall not show partiality in judgment; you shall judge the small and the great alike. You shall not shrink before the face of man, for judgment belongs unto God. And you shall bring unto me the matter which is too hard for you, and I will hear it.
  Leviticus 19:15 — Judgment without social partiality: Hearing small and great alike corresponds to the law against favouring the poor or admiring the mighty in judgment; both address concrete impartiality in deciding cases.
  Exodus 18:22 — Difficult cases reserved for Moses: The instruction to bring a difficult matter to Moses matches Jethro’s arrangement that subordinate judges handle lesser judgments while weighty matters come to Moses.

Deuteronomy 1:18
And I commanded you at that time all the things which you should do.
  Deuteronomy 1:16-17 — Same-chapter judicial summary and its contents: The notice that Moses commanded the required conduct summarizes the preceding instructions to the judges about righteous hearings, equal attention to small and great, and referral of difficult cases.

Deuteronomy 1:19
And we departed from Horeb, and went through all that great and fearful wilderness which you saw, by the way of the hill country of the Amorite, as the Lord our God commanded us; and we came unto Kadesh-barnea.
  Deuteronomy 1:6-7 — Same-chapter departure follows stated route command: The report of leaving Horeb and approaching the Amorite hill country follows the command to leave the mountain and journey toward that country; Kadesh-barnea is the reported arrival point.

Deuteronomy 1:20
And I said unto you: You have come unto the hill country of the Amorite, which the Lord our God gives you.
  Deuteronomy 1:7 — Same-chapter destination identified on arrival: Moses identifies the reached country as the Amorite hill country given by God; that region had already been named explicitly in the departure command, alongside wider geographical destinations.

Deuteronomy 1:21
Behold, the Lord your God has delivered the land before you. Go up and inherit it, as the Lord, the God of your fathers, has spoken unto you; fear not, neither be dismayed.
  Numbers 14:8-9 — Exhortations to possess the land without fear: Moses commands Israel to inherit the land and not fear; Joshua and Caleb likewise urge confidence that the Lord will give the land and prohibit fear of its people. The exhortations retain their different speakers.

Deuteronomy 1:22
And you all came unto me, and said: Let us send men before us, and let them search out the land for us, and bring us word of the way by which we shall go up, and of the cities into which we shall enter.
  Numbers 13:1-3 — Differently framed commissioning of the scouts: Deuteronomy recalls the people proposing scouts to report the route and cities; Numbers gives the Lord’s command to Moses and his sending of tribal leaders. Neither account’s initiating speaker is replaced with the other’s.

Deuteronomy 1:23
And the saying pleased me; and I took twelve men from among you, one man from each tribe.
  Numbers 13:2 — One representative from each tribe: Moses recalls choosing twelve men, one from each tribe; the Lord’s scouting command requires one man from each tribe, each a leader. No individual name equivalence is inferred from this shared arrangement.

Deuteronomy 1:24
And they turned and went up into the hill country, and came unto the Valley of the Cluster, and searched it out.
  Numbers 13:23-24 — Scouting visit to the cluster-named valley: The scouts ascend and reach the Valley of the Cluster; Numbers describes their visit to the Ravine of the Cluster, its fruit, and its naming. The distinct valley and ravine renderings are retained.

Deuteronomy 1:25
And they took of the fruit of the land in their hands, and brought it down unto us, and said: The land which the Lord our God gives us is good.
  Numbers 13:26-27 — Fruit brought back and favourable land report: The men bring fruit and report that the land God gives is good; Numbers records the returned scouts displaying fruit and describing the land as flowing with milk and honey.

Deuteronomy 1:26
And you would not go up, but disobeyed the word of the Lord your God.
  Numbers 14:1-4 — Refusal following the scouting report: Moses recounts Israel refusing to ascend and resisting God’s command; Numbers gives the congregation’s weeping, grumbling, and proposal to appoint a leader and return to Egypt.

Deuteronomy 1:27
And you murmured in your tents, and said: Because the Lord hates us, He brought us forth out of the land of Egypt, to deliver us into the hands of the Amorites, to destroy us.
  Numbers 14:2-3 — Accusations about the purpose of the exodus: The people accuse the Lord of bringing them from Egypt through hatred to destruction by the Amorites; Numbers reports their complaint that entering the land will mean death in war. These remain the people’s accusations.

Deuteronomy 1:28
Whither do we go up? Your brethren have made your heart fail, saying: The nation is great and numerous and mightier than we; and the cities are great and walled up to heaven. Moreover, we have seen sons of giants there.
  Numbers 13:28-33 — Fear-producing report of strong inhabitants: The recital names stronger people, great walled cities, and giants as the discouraging report; Numbers similarly describes strongly fortified cities, Anak’s family, stronger inhabitants, and giants.

Deuteronomy 1:29
And I said unto you: Neither cower nor fear them.
  Numbers 14:9 — Appeals against fear after the scouts return: Moses tells the discouraged people not to fear the inhabitants; Joshua and Caleb likewise tell the congregation not to fear the people of the land because the Lord is among Israel.

Deuteronomy 1:30
The Lord your God, who goes before you, shall Himself fight against them with you, according to all that He did for you in the land of Egypt,
  Exodus 14:13-14 — Egyptian deliverance as assurance that God fights: Moses appeals to what the Lord did for Israel in Egypt as assurance that He will fight; the earlier sea-deliverance speech expressly promises that the Lord will fight for the frightened people.

Deuteronomy 1:31
and in this wilderness which you saw, how the Lord your God nurtured you as a man nurtures his son, in all the way which you went, until you came unto this place.
  Numbers 11:12 — Parental and nursing imagery with different agents: Moses says that God nurtured Israel like a father his son; his earlier complaint asks whether he gave birth to the people and must carry them as a nurse carries a child. The agents and metaphors remain different.

Deuteronomy 1:32
And in this matter you did not trust the Lord your God,
  Numbers 14:11 — Failure to believe despite witnessed divine acts: Moses charges Israel with not trusting the Lord despite the preceding deliverance and nurture; Numbers records the Lord asking why the people refuse belief despite the signs performed among them.

Deuteronomy 1:33
who goes before you in the way to choose a place for you, guiding you in fire by night, to show you the way wherein you should go, and in a cloud by day.
  Exodus 13:21-22 — Guidance by cloud and fire: The Lord’s going before Israel by fire at night and cloud by day recalls the continuing pillars described in Exodus; Deuteronomy additionally specifies searching out camping places.

Deuteronomy 1:34
And the Lord heard the voice of your words, and, being provoked to anger, sware, saying:
  Numbers 14:28-35 — Angry oath concerning the resistant generation: The Lord hears the people’s words and swears in anger; Numbers records the sworn announcement that the counted grumbling generation will die in the wilderness, with stated exceptions and provision for their children.

Deuteronomy 1:35
Surely none of these men shall see this good land which I sware unto their fathers,
  Numbers 14:22-23 — Provoking adults excluded from the sworn land: The excluded generation will not see the good land sworn to the fathers; Numbers likewise excludes the men who saw the signs yet repeatedly tested God from seeing that sworn land.

Deuteronomy 1:36
save Caleb son of Jephunneh. He shall see it, and I will give unto him and unto his sons the land whereon he trod, because he cleaved unto the things of the Lord.
  Numbers 14:24 — Caleb’s faithfulness and hereditary land grant: Both passages single out Caleb for following the Lord and promise that he and his seed will possess the land he entered; the shared reason is explicitly stated faithfulness.
  Joshua 14:8-14 — Caleb’s remembered promise and subsequent allotment: Caleb recalls faithfully following the Lord and Moses’ oath assigning the land he trod to him and his children; Joshua then grants Hebron. This supplies later reception and allotment of the particular Caleb promise.

Deuteronomy 1:37
And the Lord was angry with me also for your sakes, saying: You shall not enter there either.
  Numbers 20:12 — Moses excluded with differing stated explanations: Deuteronomy says the Lord was angry with Moses for the people’s sakes and barred his entry; Numbers addresses Moses and Aaron’s failure to believe and sanctify God at the water. The differing explanations and narrative settings are preserved.

Deuteronomy 1:38
Joshua son of Nun, who stands beside you, he shall enter there. Strengthen him, for he shall cause Israel to inherit it.
  Numbers 27:18-23 — Joshua commissioned as Moses’ successor: Moses must strengthen Joshua son of Nun for leading Israel into its inheritance; Numbers describes the Lord naming Joshua son of Nun and Moses publicly commissioning him before Eleazar and the congregation.
  Numbers 14:30 — Joshua named among the permitted entrants: Joshua son of Nun is designated to enter and lead Israel into inheritance; the wilderness judgment explicitly exempts Joshua son of Nun and Caleb son of Jephunneh from the adults’ exclusion.

Deuteronomy 1:39
And every young child who this day knows neither good nor evil shall enter there; and I will give it unto them, and they shall inherit it.
  Numbers 14:23 — Children without knowledge of good or evil inherit: Both passages explicitly distinguish the children who do not know good or evil and promise them the land denied to the provoking adults; this age-description is not replaced with an inferred numerical age.

Deuteronomy 1:40
But you, turn and encamp in the wilderness by the way of the Red Sea.
  Numbers 14:25 — Command to turn by the Red Sea wilderness route: Both commands redirect Israel away from the attempted entry and toward the wilderness by the Red Sea road; Numbers additionally names Amalek and the Canaanites in the valley.

Deuteronomy 1:41
And you answered me and said: We have sinned before the Lord our God. We will go up and fight, according to all that the Lord our God commanded us. And you took up, every man, his weapons of war, and gathered yourselves together, and went up into the hill country.
  Numbers 14:40 — Confession followed by untimely ascent: The people confess sin and decide to ascend for battle; Numbers likewise records their confession and determination to ascend after the judgment, rather than treating the renewed effort as approved obedience.

Deuteronomy 1:42
And the Lord said unto me: Say unto them, You shall not go up, neither shall you fight, for I am not with you; and you shall not be crushed before your enemies.
  Numbers 14:41-43 — Warning not to ascend without divine presence: Both prohibit ascent without the Lord's presence; Deuteronomy says not to ascend and not be crushed, while Numbers explicitly predicts falling before the enemies and names Amalek and the Canaanites.

Deuteronomy 1:43
And I spoke unto you, but you did not hearken unto me; and you transgressed the word of the Lord, and forced your way up into the hill country.
  Numbers 14:44 — Presumptuous ascent despite the warning: The people disregard the warning and force their way into the hill country; Numbers likewise describes forcing their way up while Moses and the ark remain in camp.

Deuteronomy 1:44
And the Amorites who dwelt in that hill country came out to meet you, and pursued you as bees do, and wounded you from Seir unto Hormah.
  Numbers 14:45 — Defeat recounted with differing enemies and endpoints: Both accounts describe defeat by mountain inhabitants after the unauthorized ascent, but Deuteronomy names Amorites pursuing like bees from Seir to Hormah, while Numbers names Amalek and Canaanites cutting them down to Erman. These names are not harmonized.

Deuteronomy 1:45
And you sat down and wept before the Lord; but the Lord did not hearken unto your voice, neither gave He heed unto you.
  Deuteronomy 1:42-44 — Same-chapter warning, defeat, and unanswered weeping: The unanswered weeping follows the specific warning that the Lord would not accompany the ascent and the narrated defeat; this link preserves that sequence without adding an unstated motive for the refusal to hear.

Deuteronomy 1:46
And you dwelt in Kadesh many days, as many days as you dwelt there.
  Deuteronomy 2:1 — Residence followed by the resumed journey: The notice of remaining many days at Kadesh closes the first recital; the next movement resumes with turning toward the wilderness and Red Sea and circling Mount Seir. No new duration is assigned to the Kadesh stay.

Deuteronomy 2:1
And we turned and departed into the wilderness by the way of the Red Sea, as the Lord had spoken unto me; and we compassed Mount Seir many days.
  Deuteronomy 1:40 — Red Sea redirection recalled as journey: The people turn toward the wilderness by the Red Sea road and circle Mount Seir; the preceding command had directed their return toward the wilderness by that Red Sea road, without specifying the later circuit’s duration.

Deuteronomy 2:2
And the Lord said unto me:
  Deuteronomy 2:3-6 — Same-chapter speech notice and specified instructions: The introductory notice that the Lord spoke is developed by the northward turn, the warning about Esau’s descendants in Seir, the prohibition on fighting them, and the instruction to purchase provisions.

Deuteronomy 2:3
Let it suffice you to have compassed this mountain; turn therefore northward.
  Deuteronomy 2:1 — Same-chapter Seir circuit and command to end it: The Lord says the circuit around the mountain has lasted sufficiently and commands a northward turn; the immediately preceding itinerary specifies the many-day circuit around Mount Seir.

Deuteronomy 2:4
And command the people, saying: You are passing through the borders of your brethren, the sons of Esau, who dwell in Seir; and they shall fear you, and be exceedingly wary of you.
  Genesis 36:6-8 — Esau’s kinship and residence in Seir: The command calls Seir’s inhabitants the sons of Esau and Israel’s brothers; Genesis identifies Esau as Jacob’s brother, records his move with his household, and explicitly states his residence in Mount Seir and his name Edom.

Deuteronomy 2:5
Do not join battle with them, for I will give you none of their land, no, not so much as a footstep; for I have given Mount Seir unto the sons of Esau for a possession.
  Numbers 20:20-21 — Respect for Esau’s territory and detour from Edom: The command forbids war over Esau’s assigned land; Numbers reports Edom’s armed refusal of passage and Israel turning away. This compares non-conquest with the detour, without converting Edom’s refusal into permission.

Deuteronomy 2:6
Buy food from them for silver, and eat; and you shall take water from them by measure for silver, and drink.
  Numbers 20:19 — Payment for water in differently addressed dealings: Israel must buy food and measured water with silver from Esau’s descendants; Numbers records an offer to pay Edom if Israel and its livestock drink water. An offer is not silently treated as an accepted sale.

Deuteronomy 2:7
For the Lord our God has blessed you in all the work of your hands. Consider how you passed through that great and fearful wilderness. Behold, these forty years the Lord your God has been with you; you have lacked nothing.
  Nehemiah 9:21 — Forty years of nourishment without lack: Moses recalls forty years of the Lord’s presence in the wilderness during which Israel lacked nothing; Nehemiah’s prayer likewise explicitly recalls forty years of wilderness nourishment and lack of nothing.

Deuteronomy 2:8
And we passed by our brethren, the sons of Esau who dwell in Seir, along the way of Arabah, from Ailon and from Ezion-geber; and we turned and passed along the wilderness way of Moab.
  Numbers 21:4 — Routes around Esau’s or Edom’s country: Deuteronomy recounts passing by Esau’s descendants in Seir and turning toward Moab’s wilderness; Numbers describes going around Edom by the Red Sea road. The accounts’ distinct named stations and stages are not collapsed.

Deuteronomy 2:9
And the Lord said unto me: Do not deal as enemies with the Moabites, neither join battle with them; for I will give you none of their land for a possession, for I have given Seir unto the sons of Lot to inherit.
  Genesis 19:36-38 — Moab’s stated descent from Lot: The prohibition on hostility toward Moab appeals to the sons of Lot; Genesis traces Moab to Lot’s older daughter and names him father of the Moabites. Deuteronomy’s actual land-name Seir is retained rather than changed to Ar.

Deuteronomy 2:10
The Emim dwelt there aforetime, a nation great and numerous and strong as the Anakim.
  Deuteronomy 2:11 — Same-chapter description and naming of the Emim: The former inhabitants are described as Emim, numerous and strong like the Anakim; the following explanation classifies them among the Rephaim and gives Emim as the Moabite designation, without equating all Anakim and Emim.

Deuteronomy 2:11
These also are reckoned as Rephaim, like the Anakim; and the Moabites call them Emim.
  Deuteronomy 2:20 — Same-chapter regional names associated with Rephaim: Moabites call the described former population Emim, while Ammonites call the population of their country Zamzummim; both notices use Rephaim. This compares regional designations without asserting the two populations were identical.

Deuteronomy 2:12
And the Horites dwelt in Seir aforetime; but the sons of Esau destroyed them, and blotted them out from before them, and dwelt in their stead, as Israel did in the land of its inheritance, which the Lord gave unto them.
  Deuteronomy 2:22 — Same-chapter repeated account of Horite displacement: Both notices describe the Horites as Seir’s earlier inhabitants and Esau’s descendants replacing them; the later notice uses that specific displacement as an analogy within the Ammonite account.

Deuteronomy 2:13
Now therefore arise, and set forth, and pass through the Ravine Zered. And we passed through the Ravine Zered.
  Numbers 21:12 — Zered itinerary notices with distinct actions: The command requires departure and crossing the ravine of Zered; Numbers reports departure and camping in that ravine. Crossing and camping are retained as distinct actions associated with the named itinerary feature.

Deuteronomy 2:14
And the time wherein we journeyed from Kadesh-barnea until we passed through the Ravine Zered was thirty and eight years, until the whole generation of men of war had fallen, dying out of the camp, as God had sworn unto them.
  Numbers 14:29-35 — Generation’s death and distinct stated durations: The recital measures thirty-eight years from Kadesh-barnea to crossing Zered until the warriors’ generation died; Numbers gives the judgment of wilderness death and forty years tied to the forty scouting days. The durations are not equated.

Deuteronomy 2:15
And the hand of God was upon them, to consume them out of the camp, until they had fallen.
  Deuteronomy 2:14 — Same-chapter divine agency and completion of judgment: The Lord’s hand consumes the warriors from the camp until they perish; the preceding chronological notice identifies that generation’s completion after thirty-eight years and connects it with the Lord’s sworn judgment.

Deuteronomy 2:16
And it came to pass, when all the men of war had fallen, dying out from among the people,
  Deuteronomy 2:14-15 — Same-chapter completed generation-loss as transition: The notice that every warrior had died marks the completed condition for the following instructions; the preceding verses specify the elapsed interval and the Lord’s consuming hand upon that generation.

Deuteronomy 2:17
that the Lord spoke unto me, saying:
  Deuteronomy 2:18-19 — Same-chapter speech introduction and border instructions: The statement that the Lord spoke introduces the particular instructions about passing Moab’s border at Seir and approaching Ammon’s descendants without provoking them or taking their assigned country.

Deuteronomy 2:18
You shall pass this day through the borders of Moab, even Seir,
  Deuteronomy 2:9 — Same-chapter repeated Moab and Seir notices: The border-crossing instruction names Moab and Seir; the earlier prohibition on hostility to Moab also actually says Seir. Both supplied readings are preserved without silently substituting another land-name.

Deuteronomy 2:19
and you shall draw near unto the sons of Ammon. Do not deal as enemies with them, neither join battle with them; for I will give you none of the land of the sons of Ammon for a possession, because I have given it unto the sons of Lot for a possession.
  Genesis 19:38 — Lot ancestry behind the Ammon prohibition: Ammon’s descendants are protected from attack because their land was assigned to Lot’s sons; Genesis records the younger daughter’s son Amman as ancestor of the Ammanites. These supplied name forms are retained.
  Deuteronomy 2:9 — Same-chapter protected lands of Lot’s descendants: The Lord separately prohibits attacking Ammon’s descendants and Moab, grounding each prohibition in a grant to Lot’s sons. The two peoples and the actual territorial wording remain distinct.

Deuteronomy 2:20
It is reckoned a land of Rephaim; for the Rephaim dwelt there aforetime, and the Ammonites call them Zamzummim,
  Deuteronomy 2:11 — Same-chapter Rephaim naming in different countries: The Ammonite notice uses Zamzummim for the former people counted as Rephaim; the Moabite notice uses Emim. The shared classification does not independently establish that the two regional populations were identical.

Deuteronomy 2:21
a nation great and numerous and mightier than you, like the Anakim. And the Lord destroyed them from before the Ammonites, who inherited the land and dwelt in their stead unto this day,
  Deuteronomy 2:22 — Same-chapter explicit analogy of displaced inhabitants: The Lord's destruction of the powerful earlier inhabitants before Ammon's descendants is followed by an explicit comparison with the Horites' displacement before Esau's descendants in Seir. The differing grammatical agents, peoples and territories remain distinct.

Deuteronomy 2:22
even as they did for the sons of Esau who dwell in Seir, when they blotted out the Horites from before them, and inherited the land, and dwelt in their stead unto this day.
  Deuteronomy 2:12 — Same-chapter Seir displacement repeated for comparison: The Horites’ destruction and Esau’s descendants inhabiting Seir repeat the earlier Seir notice; here that particular history supports the preceding comparison with the Ammonites’ inheritance.

Deuteronomy 2:23
And the Hivites who dwelt in settlements as far as Gaza, the Cappadocians who came forth from Cappadocia blotted them out, and dwelt in their stead.
  Deuteronomy 2:21-22 — Same-chapter sequence of population replacements: Cappadocians from Cappadocia destroy the Hivites near Gaza and settle in their stead; the surrounding examples also recount replacement of earlier inhabitants by Ammon’s and Esau’s descendants. The populations and locations are not identified with one another.

Deuteronomy 2:24
Now therefore arise, and set forth, and pass through the Ravine Arnon. Behold, I have delivered into your hands Sihon the Amorite, king of Heshbon, and his land. Begin to inherit it; join battle with him.
  Numbers 21:13 — Arnon crossing at the Amorite frontier: The command joins crossing the Arnon with beginning possession and war against Sihon; Numbers describes the Arnon as the frontier between Moab and the Amorites and records Israel camping beyond it.
  Numbers 21:23-24 — Commanded conquest and narrated defeat of Sihon: The Lord announces Sihon’s delivery and commands Israel to begin war and inheritance; Numbers narrates Sihon’s attack, his defeat, and Israel’s taking control of his territory from Arnon to Jabbok.

Deuteronomy 2:25
This day begin to put the dread of you and the fear of you before all the nations under heaven, who, when they hear your name, shall be troubled and shall suffer pangs before you.
  Joshua 2:9-11 — Promised terror and later report about the two kings: The Lord promises fear among nations hearing of Israel; Rahab later reports fear after hearing about the Red Sea and the destruction of Sihon and Og. Her testimony illustrates the announced terror without claiming every nation’s response is narrated there.

Deuteronomy 2:26
And I sent messengers from the wilderness of Kedemoth unto Sihon king of Heshbon with words of peace, saying:
  Numbers 21:21 — Peace envoys to Sihon: Moses recalls sending peace messages from Kedemoth to Sihon of Heshbon; Numbers likewise explicitly describes Moses sending envoys with words of peace to Sihon king of the Amorites.

Deuteronomy 2:27
I will pass through your land. I will pass along the way; I will turn neither to the right nor to the left.
  Numbers 21:22 — Request for road passage through Sihon’s land: Moses asks to pass by the road without turning right or left; Numbers gives the corresponding request to use the royal road without entering fields or vineyards or drinking well water. The details are preserved separately.

Deuteronomy 2:28
You shall sell me food for silver, and I will eat; and you shall sell me water for silver, and I will drink. Only let me pass through on foot,
  Numbers 20:19 — Paid-water proposals to different rulers: The request to Sihon offers silver for food and drinking water and asks passage on foot; the earlier request to Edom offers payment for water consumed by people and livestock. The recipients and the stated terms remain different.

Deuteronomy 2:29
as the sons of Esau who dwell in Seir, and the Moabites who dwell in Aroer, did for me, until I pass over the Jordan into the land which the Lord our God gives us.
  Numbers 20:20-21 — Differently phrased recollections of Esau or Edom dealings: The recital compares its request with what Esau’s sons and the Moabites did for Israel; Numbers expressly reports Edom refusing territorial passage and Israel turning away. The comparison is preserved without deciding that did for me includes every requested term.

Deuteronomy 2:30
And Sihon king of Heshbon would not let us pass through his land; for the Lord our God hardened his spirit and strengthened his heart, that he might be delivered into your hands, as at this day.
  Numbers 21:23 — Sihon’s refusal with an additional divine explanation: Both passages report Sihon refusing passage; Deuteronomy additionally attributes his hardened spirit and strengthened heart to the Lord’s purpose of delivering him. That explanation is not inserted into Numbers’ wording.

Deuteronomy 2:31
And the Lord said unto me: Behold, I have begun to deliver before you Sihon the Amorite, king of Heshbon, and his land; begin to inherit his land.
  Deuteronomy 2:33-36 — Same-chapter delivery promise and narrated conquest: The Lord announces that He has begun delivering Sihon and his land and commands possession; the ensuing report describes Sihon’s defeat, captured towns, and the territory delivered into Israel’s hands.

Deuteronomy 2:32
And Sihon king of Heshbon came out to meet us, he and all his people, unto battle at Jahaz.
  Numbers 21:23 — Sihon’s assembly for battle at Jahaz: Both passages name Sihon bringing all his people against Israel at Jahaz; the shared king, location, and battle assembly make this a particular narrative parallel.

Deuteronomy 2:33
And the Lord our God delivered him before us; and we smote him, and his sons, and all his people.
  Numbers 21:24 — Defeat of Sihon and possession of his land: Deuteronomy says the Lord delivered Sihon and Israel struck him, his sons, and all his people; Numbers records Israel striking him with the sword and taking his country. The sons are an additional detail in Deuteronomy.

Deuteronomy 2:34
And we took all his cities at that time, and utterly destroyed every city in succession, with their women and their children; we left none alive as a captive.
  Deuteronomy 3:6 — Sihon’s destruction explicitly used as Og comparison: The capture and utter destruction of Sihon's towns explicitly names their women and children; the Og recital expressly compares its utter destruction of towns, with women and children, to Sihon's treatment.

Deuteronomy 2:35
Only the cattle we took as plunder, and the spoil of the cities we took.
  Deuteronomy 3:7 — Cattle and town spoil in the two campaigns: The Sihon recital reserves cattle and spoil from the captured towns for Israel; the Og recital likewise specifies cattle and city spoil taken for themselves, while preserving the campaigns as distinct.

Deuteronomy 2:36
From Aroer, which is upon the bank of the torrent Arnon, and the city which is in the ravine, even unto the hill country of Gilead, there was no city that escaped us; the Lord our God delivered them all into our hands.
  Joshua 12:1-2 — Aroer, Arnon, and Gilead in Sihon’s territory: The conquest extends from Aroer on the Arnon ravine toward Gilead; Joshua’s king-list likewise locates Sihon’s rule from Aroer in the ravine through half Gilead toward Jabbok. The descriptions are not treated as identical complete boundary lists.

Deuteronomy 2:37
Only unto the land of the sons of Ammon we did not draw near, all that borders upon the torrent Jabbok, and the cities in the hill country, as the Lord our God commanded us.
  Numbers 21:24 — Ammon distinguished from conquered Amorite land: Deuteronomy says Israel did not approach the sons of Ammon or the specified Jabbok border; Numbers limits Sihon’s conquered territory from Arnon to Jabbok as far as Ammon, additionally naming Jazer as Ammon’s border.
  Deuteronomy 2:19 — Same-chapter territorial restriction and reported observance: The report that Israel avoided the sons of Ammon follows the specific prohibition on attacking them or taking their land; the target supplies the stated grant to Lot’s sons underlying that restriction.

Deuteronomy 3:1
And we turned and went up by the way unto Bashan; and Og king of Bashan came out to meet us, he and all his people, unto battle at Edrei.
  Numbers 21:33 — Og brings his people to battle at Edrei: Both narratives describe turning onto the road toward Bashan and Og king of Bashan coming out with all his people for battle at Edrei; these shared named particulars identify the narrated encounter.

Deuteronomy 3:2
And the Lord said unto me: Do not fear him, for I have delivered him, and all his people, and all his land into your hands; and you shall do unto him as you did unto Sihon king of the Amorites, who dwelt in Heshbon.
  Numbers 21:34 — Assurance against Og by comparison with Sihon: Both divine assurances forbid fear of Og, announce delivery of him, his people, and his land, and expressly instruct Moses to treat him as Sihon of Heshbon was treated.

Deuteronomy 3:3
And the Lord our God delivered him into our hands, even Og king of Bashan and all his people; and we smote him until none of his seed remained.
  Numbers 21:35 — Og’s defeat with family or seed details retained: The Lord delivers Og and all his people, and Israel strikes him until none of his seed remains; Numbers describes striking him, his sons, and all his people until none of his were left alive. The different expressions are retained.

Deuteronomy 3:4
And we took all his cities at that time; there was no city which we did not take from them, sixty cities, all the region of Argob, the kingdom of Og in Bashan.
  Joshua 13:30 — Sixty cities in Og’s Bashan territory: Both notices specify sixty cities in Og’s Bashan territory; Deuteronomy locates its captured cities in Argob, while Joshua names Jair’s villages in the territorial allotment. This does not identify every individual city in the two descriptions.
  Deuteronomy 3:13 — Same-chapter captured Argob and later allotment: The conquest inventory names Argob as the region of the sixty cities in Og’s Bashan kingdom; the subsequent distribution assigns all that Argob region and Bashan to the half-tribe of Manasseh.

Deuteronomy 3:5
All these were fortified cities, with high walls, gates, and bars, besides very many cities of the Perizzites.
  Deuteronomy 3:4 — Same-chapter fortifications describe the captured inventory: The high walls, gates, and bars describe the cities just counted among the captured Argob towns; the source additionally names many cities of the Perizzites, which are not silently rewritten as unwalled towns.

Deuteronomy 3:6
We utterly destroyed them, as we did unto Sihon king of Heshbon; and we utterly destroyed every city in succession, with the women and the children.
  Deuteronomy 2:33-34 — Explicit comparison with Sihon’s devoted towns: The Og campaign expressly compares its utter destruction of cities, with women and children, to Sihon's treatment; the earlier recital records the city's destruction and no captive left alive after Sihon's defeat.

Deuteronomy 3:7
And all the cattle and the spoil of the cities we took as plunder for ourselves.
  Deuteronomy 2:35 — Matching cattle and town-spoil reservations: The Og recital takes cattle and city spoil for Israel; the Sihon recital likewise explicitly reserves cattle and captured-town spoil. This compares two named campaigns rather than merging their plunder.

Deuteronomy 3:8
And at that time we took the land from the hands of the two kings of the Amorites who were beyond the Jordan, from the torrent Arnon unto Hermon.
  Joshua 12:1-5 — Two eastern kings and Arnon-to-Hermon territory: The summary takes land from two Amorite kings beyond the Jordan between Arnon and Hermon; Joshua’s king-list gives those same outer limits and separately identifies Sihon and Og with their territories.

Deuteronomy 3:9
The Phoenicians call Hermon Sanior, and the Amorites call it Senir.
  Deuteronomy 4:48 — Explicit alternative names for Hermon: The source expressly reports Sanior as the Phoenician name and Senir as the Amorite name for Hermon; the later geographical summary explicitly identifies Mount Sion as Hermon. No unrelated occurrence of Sion is equated with this mountain.

Deuteronomy 3:10
All the cities of Misor, and all Gilead, and all Bashan, as far as Salcah and Edrei, cities of the kingdom of Og in Bashan.
  Joshua 12:4-5 — Og’s named cities and eastern regions: The conquered inventory includes Gilead, Bashan, Salcah, and Edrei; Joshua’s notice of Og names Edrei, Salcah, Bashan, and half Gilead. The overlapping places do not turn the differing full lists into identical boundaries.

Deuteronomy 3:11
For only Og king of Bashan remained of the Rephaim. Behold, his bed was a bed of iron; behold, it is in the citadel of the sons of Ammon, nine cubits in length and four cubits in breadth, by the cubit of a man.
  Joshua 12:4 — Og described among the remaining giant population: Deuteronomy calls Og the only remaining Rephaim and describes his iron bed; Joshua calls Og king of Bashan one of the remaining giants. The different remnant descriptions are retained, without deriving bodily height from the bed’s dimensions.

Deuteronomy 3:12
And we inherited that land at that time. From Aroer, which is upon the bank of the torrent Arnon, and half the hill country of Gilead, with its cities, I gave unto Reuben and unto Gad.
  Numbers 32:33 — Moses’ eastern grants to Reuben and Gad: The Aroer and partial-Gilead land is given to Reuben and Gad; Numbers records Moses giving Reuben, Gad, and half Manasseh Sihon’s and Og’s kingdoms. The broader three-tribe grant is not reduced to this particular two-tribe portion.

Deuteronomy 3:13
And the rest of Gilead, and all Bashan, the kingdom of Og, I gave unto the half tribe of Manasseh, with all the region of Argob, all that Bashan; it is reckoned a land of Rephaim.
  Joshua 13:29-31 — Bashan and Gilead allotted to half Manasseh: Deuteronomy assigns the rest of Gilead and Og’s Bashan kingdom, including Argob, to half Manasseh; Joshua’s allotment notice likewise gives that half-tribe Bashan, Og’s kingdom, and part of Gilead with further named subdivisions.

Deuteronomy 3:14
And Jair son of Manasseh took all the region of Argob, unto the borders of Gargasi and the Maacathites; and he called those places of Bashan after his own name, Havvoth-jair, unto this day.
  Numbers 32:41 — Jair of Manasseh captures and names settlements: Both passages associate Jair with Manasseh and with captured settlements named after him; Deuteronomy gives Argob’s extent and Havvoth-jair, while Numbers says Settlements of Jair. No additional Jair in another genealogy is identified by assumption.

Deuteronomy 3:15
And unto Machir I gave Gilead.
  Numbers 32:40 — Gilead granted to Machir: Moses’ statement that he gave Gilead to Machir corresponds directly to Numbers’ grant of Gilead to Machir son of Manasseh and its notice that he settled there.

Deuteronomy 3:16
And unto Reuben and unto Gad I gave from Gilead unto the torrent Arnon, the midst of the torrent being the border, and unto Jabbok, the torrent which is the border of the sons of Ammon;
  Numbers 21:24 — Arnon and Jabbok limits of conquered eastern country: The grant to Reuben and Gad names Arnon and Jabbok beside Ammon’s border; Numbers names Arnon and Jabbok as the limits of the land taken from Sihon, distinguishing Ammon’s country.

Deuteronomy 3:17
and the Arabah, and the Jordan as the border, Machanareth, and as far as the Sea of Arabah, the Salt Sea, below Asedoth-Pisgah toward the east.
  Deuteronomy 4:47-49 — Repeated eastern boundary summary: The boundary description includes Arabah, Jordan, the Salt Sea, and the eastern Asedoth-Pisgah position; the later recap again summarizes the two eastern kingdoms and Arabah beyond Jordan toward sunrise below Asedoth. The differing place forms and additional details are retained.

Deuteronomy 3:18
And I commanded you at that time, saying: The Lord your God has given you this land for a possession. Go armed before your brethren, the sons of Israel, every able man.
  Numbers 32:20-22 — Eastern possession joined to armed service: Moses commands the recipients of eastern land to cross armed before their brothers; Numbers makes their armed service before the Lord and the subjugation of the land conditions for returning guiltless to that eastern possession.
  Joshua 1:13-15 — Joshua repeats Moses’ eastern-warrior obligation: The men given eastern land must go armed before their brothers; Joshua expressly recalls Moses’ word and repeats that strong men cross equipped and fight alongside their brothers until the others receive rest and inheritance.

Deuteronomy 3:19
Only let your wives, and your children, and your cattle, for I know that you have much cattle, dwell in your cities which I have given you,
  Numbers 32:26 — Families and livestock remain in eastern towns: The wives, children, and numerous cattle remain in the given towns while warriors cross; Reuben and Gad explicitly undertake to leave dependents, wives, and livestock in the towns of Gilead.
  Joshua 1:14 — Joshua retains the eastern household arrangement: Joshua tells the eastern tribes to leave wives, children, and livestock in their allotted country while armed men cross before their brothers, repeating the particular division of households and military service.

Deuteronomy 3:20
until the Lord your God gives rest unto your brethren, as unto you, and they also inherit the land which the Lord our God gives them beyond the Jordan; then you shall return, every man unto his inheritance which I have given you.
  Numbers 32:18 — Return waits until other Israelites inherit: Moses permits return to the eastern possession only after the brothers have rest and inheritance; the eastern tribes’ earlier undertaking forbids return to their homes until every Israelite has received his inheritance.
  Joshua 22:3-4 — Later release after the brothers receive rest: The eastern warriors may return after the Lord gives their brothers rest and inheritance; Joshua later acknowledges their sustained service, declares the brothers have received rest, and sends them home to Moses’ eastern grant.

Deuteronomy 3:21
And I commanded Joshua at that time, saying: Your eyes have seen all that the Lord our God has done unto these two kings; so shall the Lord our God do unto all the kingdoms whither you are passing over.
  Deuteronomy 3:8 — Same-chapter two-king conquest recalled for Joshua: Moses appeals to Joshua’s sight of what the Lord did to the two kings as assurance for future kingdoms; the earlier summary specifies the acquired country of the two Amorite kings beyond the Jordan.
  Deuteronomy 31:3-4 — Sihon and Og as precedent for Joshua’s advance: Moses’ assurance that the Lord will treat future kingdoms like the two defeated kings recurs in the later succession speech, which expressly names Sihon and Og while announcing Joshua will go before Israel.

Deuteronomy 3:22
You shall not fear, for the Lord our God Himself shall fight for you.
  Deuteronomy 1:29-30 — Repeated assurance against fear in land-entry speeches: The command not to fear because the Lord fights for Israel repeats the earlier assurance to the discouraged people; that earlier speech explicitly used the Egyptian deliverance as the precedent.

Deuteronomy 3:23
And I besought the Lord at that time, saying:
  Deuteronomy 3:24-25 — Same-chapter prayer introduction and actual petition: The introduction to Moses’ supplication is developed by his praise of the Lord’s mighty works and his specific request to cross the Jordan and see the good land and Anti-Lebanon.

Deuteronomy 3:24
O Lord, Lord, Thou hast begun to show Thy servant Thy strength, and Thy power, and Thy mighty hand, and Thine uplifted arm; for what god is there in heaven or upon earth who shall do as Thou hast done, and according to Thy strength?
  Psalms 85:8 — Incomparability expressed through divine works: Moses asks what god in heaven or earth can do works like the Lord’s mighty deeds; the psalm expressly says no god is like the Lord and no works are like His works.

Deuteronomy 3:25
Let me therefore pass over and see this good land beyond the Jordan, this good hill country and Anti-Lebanon.
  Deuteronomy 3:26-27 — Same-chapter entry petition and restricted answer: Moses requests crossing and seeing the good land; the answer refuses further pleading and forbids crossing Jordan, while directing him to ascend and look in the specified directions. Viewing is distinguished from entry.

Deuteronomy 3:26
And the Lord disregarded me for your sakes, and did not hearken unto me; and the Lord said unto me: Let it suffice you; speak no more of this matter.
  Deuteronomy 1:37 — Moses’ exclusion attributed to the people’s sakes: Both recitals connect the Lord’s adverse response to Moses with the people’s sakes; the earlier statement excludes entry, while the present answer refuses to hear and orders him to stop speaking about the request.

Deuteronomy 3:27
Go up unto the summit of the Hewn Place, and lift up your eyes toward the sea, and northward, and toward the southwest, and eastward, and behold with your eyes; for you shall not pass over this Jordan.
  Deuteronomy 34:1-4 — Later land-viewing without entry and different site names: The command permits Moses to view the land but forbids crossing Jordan; the ending describes God showing him the land while excluding entry. Here the summit is Hewn Place; the ending names Nebo and Pisgah, without an added independent place-equivalence claim.

Deuteronomy 3:28
And command Joshua, and strengthen him, and encourage him; for he shall pass over before this people, and he shall cause them to inherit the land which you have seen.
  Numbers 27:18-23 — Command to commission Joshua and public implementation: Moses must command and strengthen Joshua for leading the people into inheritance; Numbers records the Lord ordering Joshua son of Nun’s appointment and Moses publicly placing him before Eleazar and the congregation and laying hands on him.
  Deuteronomy 31:7-8 — Later charge to Joshua concerning inheritance: Moses is to strengthen Joshua to lead Israel and allot the seen land; the later public charge tells Joshua to be courageous and strong, lead the people into the sworn land, and give it as inheritance.

Deuteronomy 3:29
And we dwelt in the valley near the house of Peor.
  Deuteronomy 4:46 — Repeated setting near the house of Peor: The camp is in the valley near the house of Peor; the later law-discourse frame again places Moses’ words beyond Jordan in the ravine near the house of Peor, adding the former Sihon territory.

Deuteronomy 4:1
And now, O Israel, hear the ordinances and the judgments which I teach you this day to do, that you may live and be multiplied, and enter and inherit the land which the Lord, the God of your fathers, gives you.
  Deuteronomy 6:1-3 — Obedience, life and inherited land: Both introduce ordinances to be performed in the inherited land and connect heeding them with continued life, wellbeing and the people's multiplication.

Deuteronomy 4:2
You shall not add unto the word which I command you, neither shall you take away from it; you shall keep the commandments of the Lord your God, which I command you this day.
  Deuteronomy 13:1 — Repeated no-addition and no-subtraction command: Both prohibit adding to or taking away from the commanded word; the matching command is actually at13:1 in this edition.

Deuteronomy 4:3
Your eyes have seen all that the Lord our God did unto Baal-peor; for every man who went after Baal-peor, the Lord your God blotted him out from among you.
  Numbers 25:1-9 — Baal-peor recital and its narrative setting: The warning recalls destruction connected with following Baal-peor; Numbers describes Israel's initiation into its worship, the resulting anger and the deadly plague.

Deuteronomy 4:4
But you who cleave unto the Lord your God are all alive this day.
  Deuteronomy 4:3 — Same-chapter survival contrasted with Baal-peor judgment: Those cleaving to the Lord remain alive, in explicit contrast to the preceding statement about those blotted out for going after Baal-peor.

Deuteronomy 4:5
Behold, I have shown you ordinances and judgments, as the Lord commanded me, that you should do so in the land whither you go to inherit it.
  Deuteronomy 5:31 — Moses commissioned to teach land ordinances: Both identify Moses as the recipient of commands to teach Israel ordinances and judgments for performance in the land given for possession.

Deuteronomy 4:6
And you shall keep and do them; for this is your wisdom and understanding before all the nations, who shall hear all these ordinances and say: Behold, this great nation is a wise and understanding people.
  Deuteronomy 4:8 — Same-chapter wisdom before nations and righteous laws: The nations' recognition of Israel's wisdom is accompanied by the question about the incomparable righteousness of this nation's ordinances and judgments.

Deuteronomy 4:7
For what great nation is there whose god draws near unto them, as the Lord our God in all things wherein we call upon Him?
  Deuteronomy 4:33-34 — Same-chapter incomparable nearness and particular divine acts: The question about a nation's God drawing near is developed through Israel hearing God's fiery voice and being taken from Egypt through mighty signs.

Deuteronomy 4:8
And what great nation is there that has ordinances and judgments as righteous as all this law which I set before you this day?
  Deuteronomy 4:6 — Same-chapter righteous ordinances and recognized wisdom: The question about uniquely righteous laws supplies a particular ground for the preceding portrayal of nations recognizing wisdom in Israel's observance.

Deuteronomy 4:9
Take heed unto yourself, and guard your soul diligently, lest you forget all the things which your eyes have seen, and lest they depart from your heart all the days of your life. And you shall teach your sons and your sons’ sons
  Deuteronomy 6:6-7 — Keeping words inwardly and teaching descendants: Both require retaining the instruction inwardly and teaching children; chapter4 also expressly includes grandchildren and remembering events personally seen.

Deuteronomy 4:10
the day when you stood before the Lord your God in Horeb, in the day of the assembly, when the Lord said unto me: Assemble the people unto Me, and let them hear My words, that they may learn to fear Me all the days that they live upon the earth, and may teach their sons.
  Exodus 19:16-19 — Assembly at the mountain and divine voice: The recalled assembly to hear God's words corresponds to the people being brought beneath the fiery mountain while Moses speaks and God answers with a voice.
  Deuteronomy 6:2 — Fear learned across generations: Both connect fear of the Lord with lifelong response and instruction reaching descendants; chapter6 expressly includes sons and sons' sons keeping the ordinances.

Deuteronomy 4:11
And you drew near and stood beneath the mountain; and the mountain burned with fire unto heaven, with darkness, gloom, tempest, and a great sound.
  Exodus 19:16-18 — Mountain theophany described in historical recital: Both place the assembled people beneath the mountain with fire and cloud or darkness; Exodus also describes smoke and the terrifying trumpet sound.
  Hebrews 12:18-19 — Later recollection of fire, gloom and speaking voice: Hebrews recalls blazing fire, gloom, darkness, storm and a speaking voice while contrasting that approach with its readers' approach to heavenly Zion.

Deuteronomy 4:12
And the Lord spoke unto you out of the midst of the fire; you heard the sound of words, but saw no likeness, only a voice.
  Exodus 20:22-23 — Heard divine speech and prohibition of manufactured gods: Both recall God speaking to Israel and then prohibit making gods or images; Deuteronomy specifically states that the hearers saw no likeness, only heard the voice.

Deuteronomy 4:13
And He declared unto you His covenant, which He commanded you to do, even the ten words; and He wrote them upon two tablets of stone.
  Exodus 31:18 — Divinely written two stone tablets: Both describe God giving two stone tablets with divine writing; Deuteronomy specifies the covenant's ten words, while Exodus names tablets of testimony.
  Deuteronomy 10:4 — Ten words spoken in fire and written on tablets: Both identify the ten words spoken from the mountain's fire and written on stone tablets; chapter10 recounts the writing corresponding to the first tablets.

Deuteronomy 4:14
And the Lord commanded me at that time to teach you ordinances and judgments, that you should do them in the land whither you go to inherit it.
  Deuteronomy 5:31 — Teaching commission for life in the inherited land: Both recount the Lord commissioning Moses to teach ordinances and judgments that Israel must perform in the land given for possession; chapter5 additionally names commandments.

Deuteronomy 4:15
And you shall guard your souls diligently; for you saw no likeness in the day when the Lord spoke unto you in Horeb, on the mountain, out of the midst of the fire,
  Deuteronomy 4:12 — Same-chapter unseen likeness as reason for vigilance: The warning to guard their souls explicitly rests on seeing no likeness when God spoke from fire, repeating the earlier distinction between audible words and unseen form.

Deuteronomy 4:16
lest you act lawlessly and make for yourselves a graven likeness, any image, the likeness of male or female,
  Exodus 20:4 — Prohibition of manufactured likenesses: Both prohibit making an idol or likeness; Deuteronomy begins its particular examples with male or female, while Exodus states the larger heaven-earth-water prohibition.

Deuteronomy 4:17
a likeness of any animal on the earth, a likeness of any winged bird flying beneath heaven,
  Exodus 20:4 — Animal and bird examples under the image prohibition: The prohibited animal and flying-bird likenesses specify earth and heaven examples within the broader Exodus ban on likenesses of things in heaven, earth and water.

Deuteronomy 4:18
the likeness of any creeping thing that creeps upon the earth, the likeness of any fish that is in the waters beneath the earth.
  Exodus 20:4 — Earth-and-water creature likenesses forbidden: The creeping-creature and fish examples develop the same earth-and-water scope included in Exodus' prohibition of manufactured likenesses.

Deuteronomy 4:19
And lest you lift up your eyes unto heaven, and see the sun, and the moon, and the stars, and all the adornment of heaven, and be led astray to bow down unto them and serve them, which the Lord your God has allotted unto all the nations under heaven.
  Deuteronomy 17:2-5 — Heavenly-body worship prohibited and addressed as covenant breach: The warning against worshipping sun, moon and heavenly array corresponds to a later law identifying such worship as covenant transgression requiring careful inquiry.

Deuteronomy 4:20
But God took you, and brought you forth out of the iron furnace, out of Egypt, to be unto Him a people of inheritance, as at this day.
  Deuteronomy 4:37 — Same-chapter Egypt deliverance and chosen people: Being brought from Egypt as a people of inheritance corresponds to the later account of ancestral love, chosen descendants and deliverance by God's great strength.

Deuteronomy 4:21
And the Lord was angry with me concerning the things spoken by you, and sware that I should not pass over this Jordan, neither enter the land which the Lord God gives you for a possession.
  Deuteronomy 3:25-27 — Repeated denial of Moses' Jordan crossing: Both recall Moses being denied passage over Jordan; chapter3 records his request, the Lord's refusal and the instruction to look upon the land instead.

Deuteronomy 4:22
For I die in this land, and do not pass over this Jordan; but you pass over, and shall inherit this good land.
  Numbers 27:12-14 — Moses sees the land but approaches death: The declaration that Moses dies outside the inherited land corresponds to the command to view Canaan before being gathered to his people, with the earlier transgression stated there.

Deuteronomy 4:23
Take heed, lest you forget the covenant of the Lord your God, which He made with you, and make for yourselves a graven likeness of anything concerning which the Lord your God has given commandment.
  Exodus 34:12-17 — Covenant vigilance against image worship: Both warn against abandoning covenant allegiance through manufactured gods or foreign worship; Exodus pairs its covenant warning with the Lord's jealous character.

Deuteronomy 4:24
For the Lord your God is a consuming fire, a jealous God.
  Hebrews 12:28-29 — Explicit consuming-fire wording reused in exhortation: Hebrews grounds reverent service in the statement that God is a consuming fire, matching this description while applying it within an unshakable-kingdom exhortation.
  Exodus 34:14 — Jealous divine identity against rival worship: Both explicitly call the Lord jealous; Exodus places this description within its prohibition on bowing to another god, matching Deuteronomy's surrounding image warning.

Deuteronomy 4:25
And if you beget sons and sons’ sons, and you have remained long upon the land, and act lawlessly, and make a graven likeness of anything, and do evil before the Lord your God, to provoke Him to anger,
  Deuteronomy 4:26-28 — Same-chapter conditional image offense and announced consequences: The possibility of later generations making an image introduces the ensuing testimony, loss of the land, scattering and service to manufactured gods.

Deuteronomy 4:26
I call heaven and earth to witness against you this day, that you shall surely perish from the land whither you pass over the Jordan to inherit it. You shall not prolong your days upon it, but shall be utterly blotted out.
  Deuteronomy 30:19-20 — Heaven and earth invoked as covenant witnesses: Both call heaven and earth as witnesses concerning life and continued dwelling in the promised land; chapter30 adds the explicit appeal to choose life.

Deuteronomy 4:27
And the Lord shall scatter you among all the nations, and you shall be left few in number among the nations whither the Lord shall bring you.
  Deuteronomy 28:62-64 — Repeated scattering and diminished numbers warning: Both warn that disobedient Israel will be left few and scattered among nations; chapter28 contrasts that small remnant with the former stars-like multitude.

Deuteronomy 4:28
And there you shall serve other gods, the works of men’s hands, wood and stone, which shall neither see, nor hear, nor eat, nor smell.
  Deuteronomy 28:64 — Repeated foreign service to wooden and stone gods: Both describe scattered Israel serving other gods of wood and stone; chapter4 additionally specifies their inability to see, hear, eat or smell.

Deuteronomy 4:29
And there you shall seek the Lord your God, and shall find Him, when you seek Him with all your heart and with all your soul, in your distress.
  Deuteronomy 30:1-3 — Wholehearted return while dispersed: Seeking the Lord wholeheartedly in distress among the nations corresponds to the later description of wholehearted return from dispersion followed by mercy and gathering.

Deuteronomy 4:30
And all these things shall find you in the latter days, and you shall return unto the Lord your God, and hearken unto His voice.
  Deuteronomy 30:1-3 — Return and renewed hearing after covenant calamities: Both describe the people returning to the Lord and heeding His voice when the announced things have come upon them, with chapter30 developing mercy and gathering.

Deuteronomy 4:31
For the Lord your God is a merciful God; He will not forsake you, neither will He destroy you, nor forget the covenant of your fathers which He sware unto them.
  Exodus 34:6-7 — Mercy within covenant instruction: The Lord's merciful character and remembrance of the fathers' covenant correspond to the divine proclamation of compassion, mercy and faithfulness, while Exodus also retains judgment language.
  Deuteronomy 30:3-5 — Mercy toward scattered descendants: The assurance that God will not abandon or forget the ancestral covenant corresponds to the later promise of mercy, gathering and return to the fathers' land.

Deuteronomy 4:32
Ask now of the former days that were before you, from the day when God created man upon the earth, and from one end of heaven unto the other: has there been anything like this great matter, or has such a thing been heard?
  Deuteronomy 4:33-34 — Same-chapter rhetorical inquiry and specified incomparable events: The appeal to search former days and heaven's extent introduces particular questions about a nation hearing God from fire and being taken from another nation.

Deuteronomy 4:33
Has a nation heard the voice of the living God speaking out of the midst of the fire, as you have heard, and lived?
  Deuteronomy 5:24-26 — Hearing God's fiery voice and surviving: Both explicitly marvel that human hearers have heard God speaking from fire and lived; chapter5 includes the people's accompanying fear of further hearing.

Deuteronomy 4:34
Or has God attempted to go and take unto Himself a nation from the midst of a nation, by trial, and by signs, and by wonders, and by war, and by a mighty hand, and by an uplifted arm, and by great visions, according to all that the Lord our God did in Egypt before your eyes?
  Deuteronomy 6:21-22 — Egypt deliverance by mighty hand and signs: Both recall deliverance from Egypt through a mighty hand and signs before Israel's eyes; chapter6 presents the recollection as the answer to a child's question.

Deuteronomy 4:35
That you might know that the Lord your God, He is God, and there is none beside Him.
  Deuteronomy 4:39 — Same-chapter repeated exclusive divine identity: Both affirm that the Lord is God with none beside Him; the later statement explicitly applies this recognition to heaven above and earth beneath.
  Mark 12:32 — Exclusive divine identity affirmed in later dialogue: The scribe affirms that God is one and there is none besides Him, matching the exclusive divine identity emphasized in Deuteronomy's account of Israel's instruction.

Deuteronomy 4:36
Out of heaven His voice was made heard, to instruct you; and upon earth He showed you His great fire, and you heard His words out of the midst of the fire.
  Exodus 20:22 — Divine speech from heaven: Both recall the Lord speaking from heaven to Israel; Deuteronomy additionally relates that instruction to the great fire visible upon earth.

Deuteronomy 4:37
Because He loved your fathers, He chose their seed after them, even you, and Himself brought you forth out of Egypt by His great strength,
  Deuteronomy 10:15 — Ancestral love and choice of descendants: Both explicitly connect the Lord's love for the fathers with His choosing their seed afterward, identifying the addressed Israel as those descendants.

Deuteronomy 4:38
to destroy nations great and mightier than you from before you, to bring you in, and to give you their land to inherit, as you have this day.
  Exodus 34:11 — Nations removed before Israel: Both describe the Lord removing nations before Israel; Deuteronomy emphasizes their superior might and Israel's inheritance, while Exodus names the nations to be driven out.

Deuteronomy 4:39
And you shall know this day, and lay it to mind, that the Lord your God, He is God in heaven above and upon earth beneath; there is none beside Him.
  Deuteronomy 4:35 — Same-chapter exclusive divine identity to be internalized: The instruction to recognize and take to heart that no other God exists repeats the earlier exclusive claim, now explicitly extending it to heaven and earth.

Deuteronomy 4:40
And you shall keep His ordinances and His commandments which I command you this day, that it may be well with you and with your sons after you, and that you may prolong your days upon the land which the Lord your God gives you for ever.
  Deuteronomy 5:33 — Obedient walking, wellbeing and prolonged land tenure: Both connect keeping the Lord's commands with wellbeing and prolonged days upon the inherited land; chapter4 explicitly includes the hearers' sons after them.

Deuteronomy 4:41
Then Moses set apart three cities beyond the Jordan toward the sunrise,
  Numbers 35:13-14 — Three eastern refuge towns within the six-town provision: Moses' setting apart three towns beyond Jordan corresponds to the command dividing the six refuges into three across Jordan and three in Canaan.

Deuteronomy 4:42
that the slayer might flee thither, who has slain his neighbour unintentionally, having borne him no hatred aforetime; and he shall flee unto one of these cities, and live:
  Deuteronomy 19:4-6 — Unintentional killing without prior hatred and protective flight: Both describe the slayer who killed unintentionally without former hatred fleeing to a town to live; chapter19 illustrates the case with an axe accident and avenger pursuit.
  Numbers 35:11-12 — Refuge provision for unintentional killing: Both provide towns for the unintentional killer to flee and live; Numbers adds protection from the blood avenger pending congregational judgment, while Deuteronomy here specifies no prior hatred.

Deuteronomy 4:43
Bezer in the wilderness, in the plain country, for Reuben; Ramoth in Gilead for Gad; and Golan in Bashan for Manasseh.
  Joshua 20:8 — Eastern refuge list with preserved differing place forms: Both list eastern refuges in Reuben's wilderness plain, Gad's Gilead and Manasseh's Bashan; Bezer/Ramoth/Golan and Bosor/Aremoth/Gaulon forms remain distinct without independent name identification.

Deuteronomy 4:44
This is the law which Moses set before the sons of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 4:45 — Same-chapter law heading expanded by named categories: The heading identifying Moses' law is followed by the testimonies, ordinances and judgments he spoke to Israel, specifying the categories included under that heading.

Deuteronomy 4:45
These are the testimonies, and the ordinances, and the judgments which Moses spoke unto the sons of Israel in the wilderness, when they came forth out of the land of Egypt,
  Deuteronomy 6:4 — Repeated wilderness-and-Exodus instructional heading: Both introduce ordinances and judgments commanded or spoken to Israel in the wilderness after departure from Egypt; chapter6 continues directly into the confession to hear.

Deuteronomy 4:46
beyond the Jordan, in the ravine near the house of Peor, in the land of Sihon king of the Amorites, who dwelt in Heshbon, whom Moses and the sons of Israel smote when they came forth out of the land of Egypt.
  Deuteronomy 3:29 — Repeated camp location near the house of Peor: Both locate the people in the valley or ravine near the house of Peor; chapter4 further describes Sihon's former land and Israel's victory there.

Deuteronomy 4:47
And they inherited his land and the land of Og king of Bashan, the two Amorite kings beyond the Jordan toward the sunrise,
  Deuteronomy 3:8 — Land taken from the two eastern Amorite kings: Both summarize the possession taken from two Amorite kings beyond Jordan, with chapter4 naming Sihon and Og and chapter3 giving its Arnon-to-Hermon extent.

Deuteronomy 4:48
from Aroer on the edge of the Arnon torrent as far as Mount Sion, that is, Hermon,
  Deuteronomy 3:8-9 — Northern limit described with preserved mountain names: Both describe the inherited eastern land extending to Hermon; chapter4 calls Mount Sion Hermon, while chapter3 supplies the separate Phoenician and Amorite names Sanior and Senir.

Deuteronomy 4:49
all the Arabah beyond the Jordan toward the sunrise, below Asedoth, the hewn height.
  Deuteronomy 3:17 — Eastern Arabah and Jordan boundary descriptions: Both identify the Arabah beyond Jordan toward the east and use Asedoth wording; their particular sea and height descriptions are retained without importing a modern boundary identification.

Deuteronomy 5:1
And Moses called all Israel, and said unto them: Hear, O Israel, the ordinances and the judgments which I speak in your ears this day; and you shall learn them, and take heed to do them.
  Deuteronomy 4:1 — Repeated summons to hear and perform ordinances: Both address Israel with a summons to hear the ordinances and judgments Moses teaches, requiring active performance rather than mere hearing.

Deuteronomy 5:2
The Lord your God made a covenant with you in Horeb.
  Exodus 24:7-8 — Mountain covenant recital and covenant ratification: The statement that the Lord made a covenant at Horeb corresponds to the covenant-book reading and blood declaration in Exodus' mountain narrative, without identifying the two accounts' exact ceremony boundaries.

Deuteronomy 5:3
The Lord did not make this covenant with your fathers, but with you, even you who are all here alive this day.
  Deuteronomy 5:2 — Same-chapter covenant recipients emphasized: The living hearers rather than their fathers are named as recipients of the covenant just identified at Horeb; the comparison preserves that explicit rhetorical distinction.

Deuteronomy 5:4
The Lord spoke with you face to face on the mountain, from the midst of the fire.
  Deuteronomy 4:12 — Mountain speech with preserved descriptions of encounter: Both recount the Lord addressing Israel from the fire; chapter5 calls the encounter face to face, while chapter4 expressly states that no likeness was seen.

Deuteronomy 5:5
And I stood between the Lord and you at that time, to declare unto you the words of the Lord; for you were afraid of the fire, and did not go up into the mountain. He said:
  Exodus 20:18-21 — Moses mediates speech amid people's fear: Both describe the people's fear at the fiery mountain and Moses' mediating role; Exodus records their request for him to speak and his approach into darkness.

Deuteronomy 5:6
I am the Lord your God, who brought you forth out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage.
  Exodus 20:2 — Repeated divine self-identification and deliverance prologue: Both introduce the commandments with the Lord's identity as Israel's God who brought them out of Egypt and out of the house of bondage.

Deuteronomy 5:7
You shall have no other gods before Me.
  Exodus 20:3 — Parallel prohibition of rival gods: Both prohibit other gods before or except the Lord, preserving the source's differing prepositions within the same exclusive-allegiance command.

Deuteronomy 5:8
You shall not make unto yourself an idol, nor any likeness of anything in heaven above, or upon the earth beneath, or in the waters under the earth.
  Exodus 20:4 — Repeated idol-and-likeness prohibition: Both forbid making an idol or likeness of things in heaven above, earth beneath or waters under the earth, matching the threefold scope.

Deuteronomy 5:9
You shall not bow down unto them, neither shall you serve them; for I am the Lord your God, a jealous God, repaying the sins of fathers upon children, unto the third and fourth generation of them that hate Me,
  Exodus 20:5 — Repeated image-service prohibition and generational warning: Both prohibit bowing to or serving the images and describe the jealous Lord repaying fathers' sins upon third and fourth generations of those hating Him.
  Exodus 34:14 — Jealous identity grounds exclusive worship: Both explicitly call the Lord jealous and forbid bowing to a rival god or image; Deuteronomy additionally gives the generational warning.

Deuteronomy 5:10
and showing mercy unto thousands, unto them that love Me and keep My ordinances.
  Exodus 20:6 — Repeated mercy toward loving command-keepers: Both describe mercy shown to thousands who love the Lord and keep His ordinances, setting mercy alongside the preceding generational warning.
  Exodus 34:6-7 — Mercy to thousands within divine self-description: Exodus' proclamation of abundant mercy and mercy to thousands corresponds to this Decalogue promise; its wider description also includes forgiveness and judgment.

Deuteronomy 5:11
You shall not take the name of the Lord your God in vain; for the Lord will not hold him guiltless who takes His name in vain.
  Exodus 20:7 — Repeated divine-name prohibition and accountability: Both prohibit taking the Lord's name in vain and explicitly state that the Lord will not hold the person doing so guiltless.

Deuteronomy 5:12
Keep the Sabbath day, to sanctify it, as the Lord your God commanded you.
  Exodus 20:8 — Parallel Sabbath sanctification commands: Deuteronomy commands keeping the Sabbath holy as previously commanded, while Exodus commands remembering it to keep it holy, preserving their different introductory verbs.

Deuteronomy 5:13
You shall labour six days, and do all your works;
  Exodus 20:9 — Repeated six-day labour provision: Both assign six days for labour and all one's works before the following seventh-day rest instruction.

Deuteronomy 5:14
but the seventh day is a Sabbath unto the Lord your God. You shall do no work therein, you, nor your sons, nor your daughter, nor your male slave, nor your female slave, nor your ox, nor your beast of burden, nor any of your cattle, nor the sojourner dwelling with you; that your male slave and your female slave may rest as you do.
