  Exodus 20:10 — Parallel seventh-day rest for household, animals and alien: Both prohibit seventh-day work by the hearer, household, slaves, livestock and resident alien; Deuteronomy explicitly adds that male and female slaves may rest like the hearer.

Deuteronomy 5:15
And you shall remember that you were a household slave in the land of Egypt, and that the Lord your God brought you forth thence with a mighty hand and with an uplifted arm; therefore the Lord your God commanded you to keep the Sabbath day, and to sanctify it.
  Exodus 20:11 — Different stated grounds for Sabbath sanctification: Deuteronomy grounds Sabbath observance in remembered Egyptian slavery and deliverance, whereas Exodus gives six-day creation and seventh-day divine rest; the differing grounds are preserved.
  Deuteronomy 6:21 — Remembered slavery and mighty-hand deliverance: Both recall household slavery in Egypt and deliverance by the Lord's mighty hand and uplifted arm; one grounds Sabbath, while the other answers a child's question.

Deuteronomy 5:16
Honour your father and your mother, as the Lord your God commanded you, that it may be well with you, and that your days may be long upon the land which the Lord your God gives you.
  Exodus 20:12 — Repeated parental honour and land-life promise: Both command honouring father and mother and connect this with wellbeing and long days upon the land the Lord gives.

Deuteronomy 5:17
You shall not commit adultery.
  Exodus 20:13 — Parallel adultery prohibition in the actual source order: Both prohibit adultery; the actual Exodus counterpart is20:13, preserving this edition's numbering rather than substituting a different Decalogue order.

Deuteronomy 5:18
You shall not murder.
  Exodus 20:15 — Parallel murder prohibition in the actual source order: Both prohibit murder; the actual Exodus counterpart is20:15, while Deuteronomy places murder after adultery and before theft.

Deuteronomy 5:19
You shall not steal.
  Exodus 20:14 — Parallel theft prohibition in different local order: Both prohibit stealing, with the actual Exodus unit at20:14 and Deuteronomy's at5:19; their surrounding command order remains distinct.

Deuteronomy 5:20
You shall not bear false witness against your neighbour.
  Exodus 20:16 — Repeated false-witness prohibition: Both prohibit bearing false witness against one's neighbour, matching the named act and the neighbour toward whom it is directed.

Deuteronomy 5:21
You shall not covet your neighbour’s wife. You shall not covet your neighbour’s house, nor his field, nor his male slave, nor his female slave, nor his ox, nor his beast of burden, nor any of his cattle, nor anything that is your neighbour’s.
  Exodus 20:17 — Parallel coveting prohibition and named household objects: Both begin with the neighbour's wife and enumerate house, field, slaves, ox, burden animal and other property; the full actual wording is retained.

Deuteronomy 5:22
These words the Lord spoke unto all your assembly on the mountain, out of the midst of the fire, with darkness, gloom, tempest, and a great voice; and He added no more. And He wrote them upon two tablets of stone, and gave them unto me.
  Exodus 31:18 — Divine speech completed and tablets given: Both describe the close of divine speaking and the giving of two written stone tablets; Deuteronomy also states that the words were spoken to the assembly from fire.
  Deuteronomy 10:4 — Spoken ten words and corresponding tablet writing: The account of words spoken from the mountain's fire and written on two tablets corresponds to the later recital explicitly calling that writing the ten words.

Deuteronomy 5:23
And it came to pass, when you heard the voice out of the midst of the fire, and the mountain burned with fire, that you drew near unto me, all the heads of your tribes and your elders,
  Exodus 20:18-19 — Fearful hearers approach Moses for mediation: Both place the people's fearful response after hearing the mountain's sounds and seeing its fiery or smoking appearance; Deuteronomy specifically names tribal heads and elders approaching Moses.

Deuteronomy 5:24
and said: Behold, the Lord our God has shown us His glory, and we have heard His voice out of the midst of the fire. This day we have seen that God speaks with man, and he lives.
  Deuteronomy 4:33 — Survival after hearing God from fire: Both explicitly ask or declare that Israel heard God speaking from the midst of fire and lived, identifying the same remarkable feature of the encounter.

Deuteronomy 5:25
And now let us not die, for this great fire will consume us; if we hear the voice of the Lord our God any more, we shall die.
  Exodus 20:19 — Fear of death prompts request to stop direct speech: Both record the people's concern that further direct divine speaking will cause their death and their consequent turn toward Moses as intermediary.
  Hebrews 12:18-19 — Later recollection of hearers requesting no further word: Hebrews describes hearers at the fiery, storm-darkened encounter begging that no further word be spoken, corresponding to Israel's fear of further hearing in Deuteronomy.

Deuteronomy 5:26
For what flesh has heard the voice of the living God speaking out of the midst of the fire, as we have, and shall live?
  Deuteronomy 4:33 — Repeated question about hearing the living God and living: Both marvel through a rhetorical question that people heard the living God speaking from fire and survived, preserving the specific voice-and-life connection.

Deuteronomy 5:27
Draw near, and hear all that the Lord our God shall say; and speak unto us all that the Lord our God shall speak unto you, and we will hear and do it.
  Exodus 20:19 — Moses requested as the hearers' intermediary: Both ask Moses to hear or speak on behalf of the people rather than continue direct divine speech; Deuteronomy additionally states their promise to hear and do.
  Exodus 24:7 — Expressed commitment to hear and perform divine words: Both record the people's pledge to do and obey the Lord's words, although Exodus places it at the reading of the covenant book rather than the mediation request.

Deuteronomy 5:28
And the Lord heard the voice of your words as you spoke unto me; and the Lord said unto me: I have heard the voice of the words of this people, which they have spoken unto you; all that they have spoken is right.
  Deuteronomy 5:27 — Same-chapter approval of the preceding request: The Lord's declaration that the people's words are right responds to their request for Moses to hear divine instruction and relay it for their obedience.

Deuteronomy 5:29
O that their heart were thus in them, to fear Me and to keep My commandments all their days, that it might be well with them and with their sons for ever!
  Deuteronomy 6:2 — Enduring fear and command-keeping across generations: Both connect lasting fear of the Lord and keeping commands with wellbeing or prolonged days extending to descendants, expressing the desired continuing response.

Deuteronomy 5:30
Go, say unto them: Return unto your houses.
  Deuteronomy 5:31 — Same-chapter people dismissed while Moses remains: The command for the people to return home is paired with the contrasting direction for Moses to stand with the Lord and receive further teaching instructions.

Deuteronomy 5:31
But you, stand here with Me, and I will speak unto you the commandments, and the ordinances, and the judgments which you shall teach them; and let them do them in the land which I give them for a possession.
  Deuteronomy 4:14 — Moses receives ordinances to teach for land life: Both recount the Lord assigning Moses to teach ordinances and judgments to Israel so that the people may perform them in the inherited land.

Deuteronomy 5:32
And you shall take heed to do as the Lord your God commanded you; you shall turn neither to the right nor to the left,
  Deuteronomy 5:33 — Same-chapter undeviating observance and walking the commanded way: The prohibition on turning right or left is developed by the positive instruction to walk in all the commanded way, with wellbeing and continued land life attached.

Deuteronomy 5:33
but walk in all the way which the Lord your God commanded you, that He may give you rest, and that it may be well with you, and that you may prolong your days upon the land which you shall inherit.
  Deuteronomy 4:40 — Command-keeping linked with wellbeing and prolonged days: Both connect observance of the Lord's instruction with wellbeing and long days in the given land; chapter4 expressly includes sons after the hearers.

Deuteronomy 6:1
And these are the commandments, and the ordinances, and the judgments which the Lord our God commanded me to teach you, that you should do them in the land whither you go to inherit it,
  Deuteronomy 5:31 — Teaching heading follows the stated commission: The heading identifies commandments, ordinances and judgments Moses must teach for performance in the inherited land, matching the commission given at the close of chapter5.

Deuteronomy 6:2
that you may fear the Lord your God, and keep all His ordinances and His commandments which I command you this day, you, and your sons, and your sons’ sons, all the days of your life, that you may prolong your days.
  Deuteronomy 4:9-10 — Lifelong fear and instruction of descendants: Both extend the required response across generations and lifelong fear; chapter4 expressly commands teaching sons and grandsons the remembered mountain encounter.

Deuteronomy 6:3
And hear, O Israel, and take heed to do them, that it may be well with you, and that you may multiply greatly, as the Lord, the God of your fathers, has spoken, to give you a land flowing with milk and honey.
  Deuteronomy 4:1 — Heeding ordinances for multiplication and inherited land: Both address Israel's hearing and performance of instruction in connection with multiplication and entering the Lord's given land; chapter6 explicitly names milk and honey.

Deuteronomy 6:4
And these are the ordinances and judgments which the Lord commanded the sons of Israel in the wilderness, when they came forth out of the land of Egypt: Hear, O Israel: the Lord our God, the Lord is one.
  Mark 12:29 — Explicit reuse of the hear-Israel confession: Jesus' answer about the first commandment begins with the confession that Israel's God is one, reproducing the distinctive summons and divine identity stated here.
  Deuteronomy 4:45 — Related wilderness-and-Exodus instructional headings: Both introduce ordinances and judgments delivered to Israel in the wilderness after departure from Egypt; this verse additionally contains the hear-Israel confession.

Deuteronomy 6:5
And you shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your strength.
  Mark 12:30 — Explicit reuse of the wholehearted love command: Jesus' first-commandment answer requires love with heart, soul and strength as here, while Mark's wording additionally names the mind; the enumerations remain distinct.
  Matthew 22:37-38 — Explicit use of the love command as first and great: Jesus identifies loving the Lord with heart, soul and mind as the great first commandment; Deuteronomy names heart, soul and strength, preserving the different wording.
  Deuteronomy 10:12 — Wholehearted love and service in covenant demand: Both require wholehearted love for the Lord; chapter10 includes fear, walking and service with heart and soul alongside that love.

Deuteronomy 6:6
And these words, which I command you this day, shall be in your heart and in your soul.
  Deuteronomy 4:9 — Inward preservation of instruction and witnessed events: The words are to remain in heart and soul, corresponding to the earlier warning that witnessed things must not depart from the heart throughout life.

Deuteronomy 6:7
And you shall teach them to your sons, and speak of them when you sit in your house, and when you walk in the way, and when you lie down, and when you rise up.
  Deuteronomy 4:9-10 — Teaching sons the received divine instruction: Both command teaching sons, with chapter4 also naming grandsons and the mountain assembly; chapter6 specifies the recurring daily occasions for speaking the words.

Deuteronomy 6:8
And you shall bind them for a sign upon your hand, and it shall be immovable before your eyes.
  Deuteronomy 11:18 — Repeated hand sign and immovable reminder before eyes: Both command binding the words as a sign upon the hand and making them immovable before the eyes, preserving the edition's actual physical reminder wording.

Deuteronomy 6:9
And you shall write them upon the doorposts of your houses and of your gates.
  Deuteronomy 11:20 — Repeated inscription on doorposts and gates: Both command writing the words upon house doorposts and gates, specifying the same visible household and communal locations.

Deuteronomy 6:10
And it shall be, when the Lord your God brings you into the land which He sware unto your fathers, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, to give you, great and good cities which you did not build,
  Joshua 24:13 — Promised unbuilt cities and later settlement testimony: The promise of cities Israel did not build corresponds to Joshua's later declaration that they were settled in cities they had not built upon land not laboured for.
  Deuteronomy 6:11-12 — Same-chapter inherited abundance and ensuing warning: The unbuilt cities promised to Israel are followed by houses they had not filled, cisterns they had not hewn, and vineyards and olive groves they had not planted, then the warning not to forget the deliverer after becoming full.

Deuteronomy 6:11
houses full of all good things which you did not fill, hewn cisterns which you did not hew, vineyards and olive groves which you did not plant, and you have eaten and are filled,
  Joshua 24:13 — Unplanted vineyards and olive groves promised and recalled: Both explicitly identify vineyards and olive groves Israel did not plant as received provision; Joshua describes the people eating from them after settlement.

Deuteronomy 6:12
take heed unto yourself, lest you forget the Lord your God, who brought you forth out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage.
  Deuteronomy 6:10-11 — Same-chapter abundance as setting for the forgetfulness warning: The warning not to forget the Lord who delivered from bondage follows the promised unearned houses, cisterns and crops and the experience of eating to fullness.

Deuteronomy 6:13
You shall fear the Lord your God, and you shall serve Him, and you shall cleave unto Him, and you shall swear by His name.
  Deuteronomy 10:20 — Repeated fear, service, cleaving and divine-name oath: Both command fearing and serving the Lord, cleaving to Him and swearing by His name, giving the same particular fourfold allegiance instruction.
  Matthew 4:10 — Related scriptural appeal during temptation with distinct wording: Jesus rejects the tempter with worship of the Lord and service to Him alone; Deuteronomy says fear, serve, cleave and swear, without the identical worship-and-alone formulation.
  Luke 4:8 — Related exclusive-service appeal with preserved wording difference: Jesus answers the demand for worship with worship and service to God alone; the Deuteronomy instruction uses fear, service, cleaving and oath language instead.

Deuteronomy 6:14
You shall not go after other gods, of the gods of the nations round about you,
  Exodus 34:14 — Prohibition of following or worshipping another god: Both forbid allegiance to another god, with Exodus explicitly grounding the worship prohibition in the Lord's jealous name and character.

Deuteronomy 6:15
for the Lord your God in the midst of you is a jealous God; lest the Lord your God be kindled to wrath against you, and destroy you from the face of the earth.
  Exodus 20:5 — Jealous divine identity and rival-worship warning: Both describe the Lord as jealous in a warning against rival worship; Deuteronomy warns of destruction, while Exodus states the generational repayment of sins.

Deuteronomy 6:16
You shall not put the Lord your God to the test, as you tested Him at Testing.
  Exodus 17:2-7 — Testing prohibition and the named wilderness incident: The prohibition explicitly recalls Testing; Exodus records the thirst complaint, Moses' question about testing the Lord and the place being named Testing and Reviling.
  Matthew 4:7 — Explicit scriptural testing prohibition in temptation response: Jesus answers the temple-pinnacle challenge with the prohibition on putting the Lord God to the test, matching the command's opening clause without repeating its wilderness example.
  Luke 4:12 — Explicit testing prohibition in the temple-pinnacle episode: Jesus rejects the challenge with the command not to test the Lord God, corresponding to the prohibition whose Deuteronomy context recalls Testing.

Deuteronomy 6:17
You shall diligently keep the commandments of the Lord your God, the testimonies and the ordinances which He commanded you.
  Deuteronomy 5:32-33 — Diligent command-keeping and undeviating walking: Both insist upon careful performance of the Lord's commands; the preceding chapter describes this as neither turning aside nor leaving the commanded way.

Deuteronomy 6:18
And you shall do that which is pleasing and good before the Lord your God, that it may be well with you, and that you may enter and inherit the good land which the Lord sware unto your fathers,
  Deuteronomy 4:40 — Right conduct and wellbeing in the promised land: Both connect doing the Lord's instruction with wellbeing and the land given to Israel; chapter6 explicitly speaks of entering and inheriting the good land promised to the fathers.

Deuteronomy 6:19
to drive out all your enemies from before you, as He has spoken.
  Exodus 34:11 — Removal of enemies and nations before Israel: The promise to drive enemies out before Israel corresponds to the earlier assurance that the Lord is driving the named nations from before them.

Deuteronomy 6:20
And it shall be, when your son asks you in time to come, saying: What are the testimonies, and the ordinances, and the judgments which the Lord our God commanded us?
  Deuteronomy 6:21-24 — Same-chapter child's question and historical-instructional answer: The son's question about testimonies, ordinances and judgments receives an answer recounting slavery, mighty deliverance, the promised land and the purpose of keeping the ordinances.

Deuteronomy 6:21
that you shall say unto your son: We were household slaves unto Pharaoh in the land of Egypt; and the Lord brought us forth thence with a mighty hand and with an uplifted arm.
  Deuteronomy 5:15 — Household slavery and mighty-hand deliverance recalled: Both describe household slavery in Egypt and release by the Lord's mighty hand and uplifted arm; chapter5 applies that memory specifically to Sabbath observance.

Deuteronomy 6:22
And the Lord gave signs and wonders, great and grievous, in Egypt, against Pharaoh and against his house, before our eyes.
  Deuteronomy 4:34 — Egyptian signs and wonders before Israel's eyes: Both recall great signs and wonders in Egypt before Israel's eyes; chapter6 specifically names Pharaoh and his house, while chapter4 describes taking a nation from another nation.

Deuteronomy 6:23
And He brought us forth thence, that He might bring us in, to give us this land which He sware to give unto our fathers.
  Deuteronomy 4:37-38 — Deliverance from Egypt leading to the ancestral land: Both connect being brought out of Egypt with being brought into the land given for inheritance, grounding this course in ancestral choice or promise.

Deuteronomy 6:24
And the Lord commanded us to do all these ordinances, to fear the Lord our God, that it might be well with us all our days, and that we might live, as at this day.
  Deuteronomy 4:1 — Ordinance observance connected with continuing life: Both connect doing the taught ordinances with Israel's life; chapter6 additionally names fear of the Lord and enduring wellbeing as the stated purpose.

Deuteronomy 6:25
And there shall be mercy for us, if we take heed to do all these commandments before the Lord our God, as the Lord commanded us.
  Deuteronomy 6:24 — Same-chapter mercy conditional on performing the commanded ordinances: The promise of mercy upon careful performance follows the explanation that the ordinances are for fear, wellbeing and life; the source's mercy wording is retained without substitution.

Deuteronomy 7:1
And when the Lord your God brings you into the land whither you go to inherit it, and removes great nations from before you, the Hittites, and the Girgashites, and the Amorites, and the Canaanites, and the Perizzites, and the Hivites, and the Jebusites, seven nations numerous and mightier than you,
  Exodus 23:23 — Parallel lists of nations to be displaced: Both name the Hittites, Girgashites, Amorites, Canaanites, Perizzites, Hivites and Jebusites as peoples encountered in the land, while Exodus describes the angel going ahead.
  Deuteronomy 11:23 — Repeated promise concerning stronger nations: Both describe the Lord removing nations greater and mightier than Israel so that Israel may inherit, explicitly distinguishing divine agency from superior Israelite numbers.

Deuteronomy 7:2
and the Lord your God delivers them into your hands, and you strike them, you shall utterly destroy them. You shall make no covenant with them, neither shall you show them mercy.
  Exodus 23:32 — Repeated prohibition of covenants with the nations: Both prohibit making a covenant with the land's inhabitants; Exodus also names their gods, while this verse adds the stated destruction and no-mercy instructions.
  Exodus 34:11-12 — Displacement promise and covenant warning: Exodus announces driving out the named nations and warns against covenanting with their inhabitants, corresponding to the paired delivery and no-covenant clauses here.

Deuteronomy 7:3
Neither shall you intermarry with them; you shall not give your daughter unto his son, nor take his daughter for your son.
  Exodus 34:16 — Parallel intermarriage prohibitions: Both prohibit exchanging daughters and sons in marriage with the nations, while Exodus explicitly connects these marriages with leading sons into the nations' worship.
  Joshua 23:12-13 — Marriage warning and later farewell admonition: Joshua warns against intermarrying and mingling with remaining nations and predicts their becoming snares, corresponding to Deuteronomy's marriage prohibition and its surrounding apostasy warning.

Deuteronomy 7:4
For he will turn your son away from Me, and he will serve other gods; and the Lord shall be kindled to wrath against you, and shall destroy you speedily.
  Exodus 34:16 — Stated danger of marriage leading to other gods: Both describe family relationships leading sons toward the nations' gods; Deuteronomy adds the Lord's anger and threatened rapid destruction as consequences.
  Deuteronomy 6:14-15 — Other-god worship and threatened divine anger: Both warn against following surrounding gods and describe the Lord's anger bringing destruction, providing the particular covenant danger behind this marriage warning.

Deuteronomy 7:5
But you shall do thus unto them: you shall pull down their altars, and break their pillars, and cut down their sacred groves, and burn the graven images of their gods with fire.
  Exodus 34:13 — Parallel destruction of cult objects: Both enumerate tearing down altars, breaking pillars, cutting sacred groves and burning carved images, preserving the specific actions and objects in each instruction.
  Exodus 23:24 — Related refusal and demolition of foreign worship: Exodus prohibits bowing to or serving the gods and commands overthrowing their installations and pillars; Deuteronomy gives a fuller list including groves and burned images.

Deuteronomy 7:6
For you are a holy people unto the Lord your God; and the Lord your God has chosen you to be unto Him a people for His own possession, above all the nations upon the face of the earth.
  Deuteronomy 14:2 — Repeated holy and chosen possession description: Both identify Israel as holy to the Lord and chosen as His possession from the nations upon earth, placing the same identity formula within different instructions.
  Exodus 19:5-6 — Covenant possession and holy people: Exodus associates obedience to the covenant with being the Lord's treasured people and holy nation; Deuteronomy uses holy-people and chosen-possession language to ground its commands.

Deuteronomy 7:7
The Lord preferred you and chose you, not because you were more numerous than all the nations, for you are the fewest of all the nations,
  Deuteronomy 7:8 — Same-chapter rejected and stated grounds of election: Israel's numbers are rejected as the reason for the Lord's choice; the following verse gives His love and faithfulness to the ancestral oath as the reasons.
  Deuteronomy 10:15 — Related descriptions of the Lord's preferential choice: Both describe the Lord preferring and choosing Israel, with chapter10 explicitly connecting the choice of descendants to love for their fathers rather than numerical superiority.

Deuteronomy 7:8
but because the Lord loved you, and kept the oath which He sware unto your fathers, the Lord brought you forth with a mighty hand and with an uplifted arm, and redeemed you out of the house of bondage, out of the hand of Pharaoh king of Egypt.
  Deuteronomy 6:21-23 — Ancestral pledge and Egyptian deliverance: Both recall mighty-hand deliverance from Egyptian slavery and connect departure with the land sworn to the fathers; this verse expressly identifies divine love as its motive.
  Deuteronomy 4:31 — Faithfulness to the oath sworn to the fathers: Both ground divine mercy or deliverance in the covenant sworn to the fathers, while chapter4 applies that faithfulness to Israel's future distress and return.

Deuteronomy 7:9
And you shall know that the Lord your God, He is God, the faithful God, who keeps covenant and mercy with them that love Him and keep His commandments, unto a thousand generations,
  Exodus 20:6 — Mercy associated with loving and keeping instruction: Both connect the Lord's mercy with those who love Him and keep His commandments, while retaining the distinct formulations of thousands and a thousand generations.
  Exodus 34:6-7 — Divine faithfulness and mercy in self-description: Exodus proclaims the Lord merciful and true, showing mercy to thousands; Deuteronomy describes Him as the faithful God who keeps covenant and mercy across generations.

Deuteronomy 7:10
and repays them that hate Him to their face, to destroy them. He shall not delay with them that hate Him; He shall repay them to their face.
  Exodus 20:5 — Recompense concerning those who hate the Lord: Both describe divine repayment in relation to hatred of the Lord; Exodus's intergenerational wording differs from Deuteronomy's direct and undelayed recompense to the hater.
  Deuteronomy 7:9 — Same-chapter contrasting responses to love and hatred: The repayment of those who hate the Lord is set beside covenant mercy for those who love Him and keep His commands, preserving both sides of the contrast.

Deuteronomy 7:11
And you shall keep these commandments, and ordinances, and judgments, which I command you this day to do.
  Deuteronomy 6:1-2 — Repeated categories of instruction to be kept: Both combine commandments, ordinances and judgments with the requirement to do them; chapter6 additionally specifies their teaching and observance across succeeding generations in the land.

Deuteronomy 7:12
And it shall be, when you hearken unto all these ordinances, and keep and do them, that the Lord your God shall keep with you the covenant and the mercy which He sware unto your fathers.
  Deuteronomy 7:9 — Same-chapter covenant mercy stated and condition applied: The promised keeping of covenant and mercy upon hearing and doing the ordinances applies the earlier description of God keeping covenant mercy with command-keeping lovers.
  Leviticus 26:3-9 — Obedience, promised blessings and covenant establishment: Both introduce agricultural and population blessings through obedience, and both explicitly connect those blessings with maintaining or establishing the Lord's covenant.

Deuteronomy 7:13
And He shall love you, and bless you, and multiply you; and He shall bless the offspring of your womb, and the fruit of your land, your grain, and your wine, and your oil, the herds of your cattle and the flocks of your sheep, upon the land which the Lord sware unto your fathers to give you.
  Deuteronomy 11:13-15 — Related lists of agricultural covenant provision: Both name grain, wine, oil and cattle provision within obedient covenant life; this verse additionally names multiplication and offspring, while chapter11 specifies rain and pasture.
  Leviticus 26:4-9 — Parallel agricultural and population blessings: Leviticus promises seasonal rain, produce, satisfied eating and multiplication under obedience; Deuteronomy specifies human offspring, livestock increase and grain, wine and oil within that blessing pattern.

Deuteronomy 7:14
You shall be blessed above all the nations; there shall be no barren male or female among you, nor among your cattle.
  Exodus 23:26 — Parallel promise against barrenness: Both promise absence of barrenness in the land; Deuteronomy expressly includes male and female persons and cattle, while Exodus also speaks of fulfilled days.

Deuteronomy 7:15
And the Lord shall take away from you every infirmity; and none of the grievous diseases of Egypt which you have seen and known shall He lay upon you, but He shall lay them upon all that hate you.
  Exodus 15:26 — Disease promise explicitly recalling Egypt: Both promise that Egyptian diseases will not be laid upon obedient Israel; Deuteronomy also speaks of removing infirmity and placing those diseases upon Israel's haters.
  Exodus 23:25 — Service and promised removal of sickness: Exodus connects serving the Lord with blessed food and water and sickness turned away; Deuteronomy similarly promises removal of infirmity within its obedient covenant blessing.

Deuteronomy 7:16
And you shall consume all the spoil of the nations which the Lord your God gives you; your eye shall not spare them, neither shall you serve their gods, for that is a snare unto you.
  Exodus 23:33 — Foreign worship described as a snare: Both warn that serving the nations' gods becomes a snare or stumbling block, connecting the restriction on their presence or spoil with fidelity to the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 7:25-26 — Same-chapter spoil warning and prohibited cult property: The command concerning the nations' spoil is limited by the later specific prohibition on taking precious metals from idols or bringing devoted abominations into the house.

Deuteronomy 7:17
And if you say in your mind: This nation is more numerous than I; how shall I be able to destroy them?
  Deuteronomy 7:18-19 — Same-chapter fearful numerical calculation and answer: The imagined objection that the nation is more numerous receives an explicit answer: do not fear, but remember the Lord's witnessed Egyptian signs and deliverance.
  Deuteronomy 1:28-30 — Earlier numerical fear and countervailing divine assurance: The earlier report of stronger, numerous people and great cities caused fear; Moses answered that the Lord would fight as He had in Egypt, matching the argument here.

Deuteronomy 7:18
you shall not fear them. You shall surely remember all that the Lord your God did unto Pharaoh and unto all the Egyptians,
  Deuteronomy 1:29-30 — Egyptian deliverance used against fear of enemies: Both counter fear of the land's opponents by recalling what the Lord did in Egypt, with chapter1 explicitly stating that He will fight before Israel.

Deuteronomy 7:19
the great trials which your eyes saw, the signs and those great wonders, the mighty hand and the uplifted arm, wherewith the Lord your God brought you forth. So shall the Lord our God do unto all the nations before whom you are afraid.
  Deuteronomy 29:1-2 — Repeated eyewitness recital of Egyptian trials and signs: Both address hearers who saw the Egyptian deeds, calling them great trials, signs and wonders; this verse additionally applies their remembrance to the feared nations.

Deuteronomy 7:20
And the Lord your God shall send hornets among them, until those who are left, and those who hide from you, are blotted out.
  Exodus 23:28 — Parallel promise of hornets preceding displacement: Both explicitly promise hornets as an agent of driving out the nations, with Deuteronomy emphasizing remaining and hiding people and Exodus naming particular nations.

Deuteronomy 7:21
You shall not be wounded before them, for the Lord your God is in the midst of you, a great and mighty God.
  Joshua 1:9 — Related divine-presence reassurance in entry commands: Deuteronomy's assurance that the great and mighty God is in Israel's midst corresponds to Joshua's assurance that the Lord is with him wherever he goes; their exact warning language differs.

Deuteronomy 7:22
And the Lord your God shall consume these nations from before you little by little; you shall not be able to consume them speedily, lest the land become desolate, and the wild beasts multiply against you.
  Exodus 23:29-30 — Gradual displacement and wild-animal rationale: Both specify little-by-little removal instead of immediate clearing and expressly connect the gradual pace with avoiding desolation and multiplication of wild beasts.

Deuteronomy 7:23
And the Lord your God shall deliver them into your hands, and destroy them with a great destruction, until He has utterly destroyed them.
  Exodus 23:31 — Parallel delivery of the inhabitants into Israel's hands: Both promise delivery of the land's inhabitants into Israel's hands; Exodus additionally gives territorial borders and speaks of casting them out, while Deuteronomy speaks of destruction.

Deuteronomy 7:24
And He shall deliver their kings into your hands, and their name shall perish from that place. None shall stand before you, until you have destroyed them.
  Joshua 23:9-10 — No opponent standing and subsequent conquest summary: Joshua describes great strong nations destroyed and no one standing against Israel, corresponding to the promise that no one will stand until the named opponents are destroyed.
  Deuteronomy 11:25 — Repeated assurance that none will withstand Israel: Both explicitly state that none will stand before Israel, while this verse additionally speaks of kings and erased names and chapter11 names dread upon the land.

Deuteronomy 7:25
You shall burn the graven images of their gods with fire. You shall not covet the silver or gold upon them, nor take it unto yourself, lest you stumble thereby; for it is an abomination unto the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 7:5 — Same-chapter repeated burning of carved images: The later ban on coveting idol metals repeats the earlier command to burn carved images, giving a particular restriction on appropriating their precious materials.

Deuteronomy 7:26
And you shall not bring an abomination into your house, lest you become a thing under the ban, like unto it. You shall utterly reject it, and utterly abhor it, for it is a thing under the ban.
  Deuteronomy 7:25 — Same-chapter household ban following the idol-metal warning: The ban on bringing an abomination home follows the prohibition on taking idol silver or gold, extending the warning to the household and identifying the property as devoted.

Deuteronomy 8:1
All the commandments which I command you this day you shall take heed to do, that you may live and multiply, and enter and inherit the land which the Lord your God sware unto your fathers.
  Deuteronomy 4:1 — Repeated obedience, life, multiplication and inheritance sequence: Both command performance of the delivered instruction so that Israel may live, multiply and enter to inherit the land given by the God of the fathers.
  Deuteronomy 11:8-9 — Repeated command-keeping purpose and ancestral land oath: Both connect keeping all the commandments with living, multiplying and entering the land, explicitly relating that inheritance to the Lord's oath to the fathers.

Deuteronomy 8:2
And you shall remember all the way wherein the Lord your God led you in the wilderness, to afflict you and to test you, that the things in your heart might be known, whether you would keep His commandments or not.
  Exodus 16:4 — Wilderness provision as a test of obedience: Both describe wilderness testing to disclose whether Israel will keep divine instruction; Exodus specifically places the test in the daily gathering of heaven's bread.

Deuteronomy 8:3
And He afflicted you, and made you hunger, and fed you with manna, which your fathers did not know, that He might declare unto you that man shall not live by bread alone, but man shall live by every word that proceeds through the mouth of God.
  Matthew 4:4 — Explicit citation of life beyond bread alone: Jesus answers the bread-making temptation with the written statement that human life depends on every word proceeding through God's mouth, reproducing the key teaching of this verse.
  Luke 4:4 — Shorter explicit citation in the temptation account: Luke records Jesus replying that a human shall not live on bread alone; the saved text stops there and does not reproduce Deuteronomy's following every-word clause.
  Exodus 16:14-15 — Unknown wilderness food identified as divine bread: Both describe previously unfamiliar wilderness food; Exodus records the people's question and Moses' identification of it as bread given by the Lord, while Deuteronomy explains its lesson.

Deuteronomy 8:4
Your garments did not wear out upon you, neither did your feet become calloused; behold, these forty years.
  Deuteronomy 29:4 — Forty-year preservation with different bodily details: Both affirm that garments did not wear out during forty wilderness years; chapter8 names feet not becoming calloused, whereas chapter29 names sandals not wearing away.

Deuteronomy 8:5
And you shall know in your heart that, as a man chastens his son, so shall the Lord your God chasten you.
  Deuteronomy 1:31 — Father-and-son imagery for wilderness dealings: Both compare the Lord's treatment of Israel with a man's treatment of his son, distinguishing chapter1's nurturing image from this verse's explicit chastening.
  Hebrews 12:7-10 — Divine discipline explained through fatherhood: Both use a father disciplining a son to explain divine chastening; Hebrews expands this into submission and benefit, without claiming that its accompanying quotation is from Deuteronomy.

Deuteronomy 8:6
And you shall keep the commandments of the Lord your God, to walk in His ways and to fear Him.
  Deuteronomy 10:12-13 — Fear, walking and command keeping combined: Both require fearing the Lord, walking in His ways and keeping His commandments; chapter10 also expressly includes wholehearted love and service for Israel's good.

Deuteronomy 8:7
For the Lord your God brings you into a good and spacious land, where are torrents of water and springs of the depths, flowing through the plains and through the hills;
  Deuteronomy 11:11-12 — Related descriptions of the land's water and care: Chapter8 describes torrents and springs across plains and hills; chapter11 describes hills and plains drinking heaven's rain and receiving the Lord's continuing care, preserving the distinct water sources.

Deuteronomy 8:8
a land of wheat and barley, vines, fig trees, and pomegranates, a land of olive oil and honey,
  Deuteronomy 11:14 — Land produce and promised harvest provision: The named wheat, barley, vines and olive oil correspond to the later promise of grain, wine and oil through seasonal rain, without equating the two crop lists completely.

Deuteronomy 8:9
a land wherein you shall eat your bread without poverty, and shall lack nothing; a land whose stones are iron, and out of whose hills you shall mine copper.
  Deuteronomy 8:7-8 — Same-chapter completion of the good-land description: Bread without poverty, lack of nothing and mineral-bearing terrain complete the preceding description of abundant water and crops, distinguishing the several resources named in the land encomium.

Deuteronomy 8:10
And you shall eat and be filled, and bless the Lord your God upon the good land which He has given you.
  Deuteronomy 8:11-14 — Same-chapter satisfied eating and contrasting responses: The command to bless after eating in the good land is contrasted with the ensuing danger that filled tables, good houses and increasing possessions lead to forgetting the giver.

Deuteronomy 8:11
Take heed unto yourself, lest you forget the Lord your God, and fail to keep His commandments, and His judgments, and His ordinances which I command you this day;
  Deuteronomy 6:10-12 — Parallel warning against forgetting amid prosperity: Both warn against forgetting the Lord after receiving houses and satisfying food in the promised land, explicitly linking remembered identity with deliverance from Egyptian bondage.

Deuteronomy 8:12
lest, when you have eaten and are filled, and have built good houses and dwelt in them,
  Deuteronomy 6:10-12 — Shared houses, satisfied eating and remembrance warning: Both combine good houses and eating to fullness with a warning against forgetting the Lord; chapter6 additionally specifies that the houses were not built by the recipients.

Deuteronomy 8:13
and your herds and your flocks are multiplied, and your silver and your gold are multiplied, and all that you have is multiplied,
  Deuteronomy 8:17-18 — Same-chapter multiplied possessions and their source: The increase of herds, flocks, silver and gold provides the concrete wealth later attributed wrongly to one's own power and correctly to strength given by the Lord.

Deuteronomy 8:14
you become lifted up in heart, and forget the Lord your God, who brought you forth out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage;
  Deuteronomy 6:12 — Repeated danger of forgetting the deliverer from bondage: Both explicitly identify the forgotten Lord as the One who brought Israel out of Egypt and the house of bondage; this verse particularly names a lifted-up heart.

Deuteronomy 8:15
who led you through that great and fearful wilderness, wherein were biting serpents, and scorpions, and thirst, where there was no water; who brought forth for you a spring of water out of the sheer rock;
  Exodus 17:5-6 — Rock-water provision in the wilderness: The recital of water brought from sheer rock corresponds to the command to strike the Horeb rock and the resulting drinking water, retaining the narrative's explicit setting.
  Numbers 21:4-9 — Serpents within the wilderness journey: The fearful wilderness description includes biting serpents; Numbers narrates a particular serpent judgment and subsequent remedy, without claiming that every wilderness serpent mentioned here belongs to that episode.

Deuteronomy 8:16
who fed you with manna in the wilderness, which your fathers did not know, to afflict you and to test you, and to do you good in your latter days.
  Exodus 16:4 — Manna and testing obedience: Both connect the wilderness bread with divine testing; Exodus states the question of walking in the law, while Deuteronomy adds humbling and intended later good.
  Deuteronomy 8:2-3 — Same-chapter repeated humbling, testing and manna explanation: This verse resumes the preceding account of wilderness humbling, testing and unfamiliar manna, now explicitly adding the purpose of doing good in the latter days.

Deuteronomy 8:17
Do not say in your heart: My strength and the might of my hand have gotten me this great wealth.
  Deuteronomy 8:18 — Same-chapter false and corrected accounts of wealth: The inner claim that one's own strength gained wealth is answered by remembering the Lord as the giver of strength to gain it and keeper of the ancestral covenant.

Deuteronomy 8:18
And you shall remember the Lord your God, for He gives you strength to gain wealth, that He may establish His covenant which the Lord sware unto your fathers, as at this day.
  Deuteronomy 8:17 — Same-chapter divine gift correcting self-attribution: The statement that the Lord gives strength to gain wealth directly corrects the preceding assertion that one's own hand alone obtained the great wealth.

Deuteronomy 8:19
And it shall be, if you utterly forget the Lord your God, and go after other gods, and serve them, and bow down unto them, I call heaven and earth to witness against you this day, that you shall surely perish.
  Deuteronomy 4:25-26 — Idolatry, heaven-and-earth witness and threatened perishing: Both invoke heaven and earth as witnesses against Israel and announce perishing in connection with idolatry; chapter4 specifically describes making a graven likeness after long residence.
  Deuteronomy 6:14-15 — Repeated prohibition of other-god allegiance: Both warn against following the nations' gods and connect the action with threatened destruction, while this verse expressly includes forgetting, serving and bowing down.

Deuteronomy 8:20
As the other nations which the Lord destroys from before you, so shall you perish, because you did not hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 7:1-4 — Israel's warning measured against the displaced nations: The threat that Israel will perish like displaced nations recalls the earlier destruction and other-god warnings; the comparison makes obedience, rather than possession alone, decisive.

Deuteronomy 9:1
Hear, O Israel: you pass over the Jordan this day, to enter and inherit nations great and mightier than you, cities great and walled up to heaven,
  Deuteronomy 1:28 — Repeated report of great nations and heaven-high walls: Both describe great, numerous or stronger peoples and cities walled up to heaven, while the earlier passage reports the words that caused the hearers' hearts to fail.

Deuteronomy 9:2
a people great and numerous and tall, the sons of Anak, whom you know, and of whom you have heard: Who shall stand before the sons of Anak?
  Deuteronomy 1:28 — Tall opponents and earlier report of giants: Chapter9 names the tall sons of Anak, while the earlier report describes sons of giants among the intimidating inhabitants; the comparison preserves the differing identification language.

Deuteronomy 9:3
And you shall know this day that the Lord your God, He goes before you; He is a consuming fire. He shall destroy them, and He shall turn them back from before you, and you shall destroy them, as the Lord has spoken unto you.
  Deuteronomy 4:24 — Repeated consuming-fire description in different settings: Both call the Lord a consuming fire; chapter4 uses the image with jealousy and an idol warning, while chapter9 applies it to divine action against the land's opponents.
  Deuteronomy 1:30 — Divine action preceding Israel against the opponents: Both state that the Lord goes before Israel and acts against the enemies; chapter1 speaks of fighting, while chapter9 describes consuming fire and destruction.

Deuteronomy 9:4
Do not say in your heart, when the Lord your God consumes these nations from before you: Because of my righteous deeds the Lord has brought me in to inherit this good land. Rather, because of the ungodliness of these nations the Lord shall destroy them from before you.
  Genesis 15:16 — Land transfer related to the inhabitants' sins: Deuteronomy denies Israelite righteousness as the cause and names the nations' ungodliness; Genesis explains delayed return through the Amorites' sins not yet being filled up.
  Deuteronomy 9:5-6 — Same-chapter denial of meritorious grounds repeated: The warning against saying one's righteous deeds secured the land is expanded by the following denial of righteousness or heart-holiness and the description of Israel as stiff-necked.

Deuteronomy 9:5
You do not enter to inherit their land for your righteousness, nor for the holiness of your heart; but because of the ungodliness of these nations the Lord shall destroy them from before you, and that He may establish His covenant which He sware unto your fathers, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.
  Genesis 15:18-21 — Ancestral covenant and explicitly named promised peoples: Genesis records the covenant giving Abram's seed land occupied by named peoples; Deuteronomy relates inheritance to establishing the oath sworn to Abraham, Isaac and Jacob rather than Israel's righteousness.
  Deuteronomy 7:7-8 — Ancestral oath distinguished from Israel's superiority: Both reject an Israelite basis for boasting and affirm the Lord's ancestral oath, with chapter7 rejecting superior numbers and chapter9 rejecting righteous deeds or heart-holiness.

Deuteronomy 9:6
And you shall know this day that the Lord your God does not give you this good land to inherit for your righteous deeds; for you are a stiff-necked people.
  Exodus 34:9 — Stiff-necked people and Moses' appeal for forgiveness: Both describe Israel as stiff-necked; in Exodus Moses nevertheless asks the Lord to accompany and forgive them, matching the denial that their righteousness earned the land.

Deuteronomy 9:7
Remember, and do not forget, how you provoked the Lord your God in the wilderness. From the day when you came forth out of Egypt until you came unto this place, you continued in disobedience toward the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 9:22-24 — Same-chapter summary of provocation and specified episodes: The general charge of continuing wilderness disobedience is later illustrated by Burning, Testing, Graves of Desire and the refused Kadesh-barnea entry instruction.

Deuteronomy 9:8
And in Horeb you provoked the Lord, and the Lord was angry with you, to destroy you,
  Exodus 32:7-10 — Horeb provocation and threatened destruction: The recital of anger and threatened destruction corresponds to the Lord's report of the molten calf and His announced intention to destroy the people; the actual Exodus sequence omits32:9.

Deuteronomy 9:9
when I went up into the mountain to receive the tablets of stone, the tablets of the covenant which the Lord made with you. And I abode in the mountain forty days and forty nights; I ate no bread, and drank no water.
  Exodus 24:12-18 — Mountain ascent for tablets and forty-day stay: Both describe Moses ascending to receive stone tablets and remaining forty days and nights; Deuteronomy also expressly states his abstention from bread and water.
  Exodus 34:28 — Forty-day fast in a distinct tablet episode: Both explicitly mention forty days and nights without bread or water; Exodus34 concerns the renewed covenant tablets, whereas Deuteronomy here recalls the initial reception before the calf.

Deuteronomy 9:10
And the Lord gave me the two tablets of stone, written with the finger of God; and upon them were written all the words which the Lord spoke unto you in the mountain, in the day of the assembly.
  Exodus 31:18 — Stone tablets written with God's finger: Both identify the two stone tablets as written with God's finger; Deuteronomy further connects their words with the public mountain assembly.

Deuteronomy 9:11
And after forty days and forty nights, the Lord gave me the two stone tablets, the tablets of the covenant.
  Exodus 24:18 — Forty-day mountain period before tablet reception: The receipt of the tablets after forty days and nights corresponds to Exodus's account of Moses remaining within the cloud on the mountain for that duration.

Deuteronomy 9:12
And the Lord said unto me: Arise, go down speedily from hence; for your people, whom you brought forth out of the land of Egypt, have acted lawlessly. They have quickly turned aside from the way which you commanded them; they have made for themselves a molten image.
  Exodus 32:7-8 — Divine command to descend because of the calf: Both have the Lord command Moses to descend quickly because the people brought from Egypt have transgressed the commanded way and made a molten calf or image.

Deuteronomy 9:13
And the Lord said unto me: I have spoken unto you once and twice, saying: I have seen this people, and behold, it is a stiff-necked people.
  Deuteronomy 9:6 — Same-chapter repeated stiff-necked description: The reported divine description of the people as stiff-necked repeats the earlier description used to deny their righteous deeds as the ground of receiving the land.

Deuteronomy 9:14
Let Me destroy them, and I will blot out their name from under heaven, and make you a nation great and strong and more numerous than this.
  Exodus 32:10 — Threatened destruction and a new nation from Moses: Both announce destruction of the people and a replacement nation made from Moses; Deuteronomy additionally specifies erased names and greater strength and numbers.

Deuteronomy 9:15
And I turned and came down from the mountain, and the mountain burned with fire, and the two tablets were in my two hands.
  Exodus 32:15 — Moses' descent with tablets in his hands: Both recount Moses turning and descending from the mountain carrying two tablets; Deuteronomy mentions the burning mountain and Exodus additionally describes writing on both sides.

Deuteronomy 9:16
And I saw that you had sinned before the Lord your God, and made for yourselves a molten image, and turned aside from the way which the Lord commanded you.
  Exodus 32:19 — Moses sees the calf on approaching the people: Both narrate Moses personally seeing the calf or molten image after descending; Exodus also names the dancing and the anger preceding the tablets' destruction.

Deuteronomy 9:17
And I took hold of the two tablets, and cast them out of my two hands, and broke them before your eyes.
  Exodus 32:19 — Breaking of the two tablets: Both recount Moses casting the two tablets from his hands and breaking them; Deuteronomy stresses the people's sight of it and Exodus locates it at the mountain's foot.

Deuteronomy 9:18
And I besought the Lord a second time, as at the first, forty days and forty nights; I ate no bread, and drank no water, because of all your sins which you had sinned, in doing evil before the Lord your God, to provoke Him.
  Exodus 32:30-32 — Renewed intercession after the calf sin: Both describe Moses returning to plead over the people's great sin; Deuteronomy gives the forty-day fast, while Exodus records the appeal for forgiveness and offered erasure from the book.
  Deuteronomy 9:25 — Same-chapter repeated duration of intercession: Both state forty days and forty nights of entreaty in response to the sin and threatened destruction; verse18 additionally explicitly describes fasting from bread and water.

Deuteronomy 9:19
And I am afraid because of the wrath and the fury wherewith the Lord was provoked against you, to destroy you. And the Lord hearkened unto me at that time also.
  Exodus 32:14 — Intercession heard amid threatened judgment: Deuteronomy affirms that the Lord heard Moses at that time; Exodus describes the Lord being appeased concerning the calamity He had announced against His people.

Deuteronomy 9:20
And the Lord was very angry with Aaron, intending to destroy him; I prayed for Aaron also at that time.
  Exodus 32:21-25 — Aaron's particular involvement in the calf offense: Deuteronomy reports anger against Aaron and prayer for him; Exodus supplies Moses' accusation and Aaron's explanation, without itself stating the separate prayer recorded here.

Deuteronomy 9:21
And I took your sin, the calf which you had made, and burned it with fire, and broke it in pieces, grinding it thoroughly until it became fine; and it became as dust, and I cast the dust into the torrent that descended from the mountain.
  Exodus 32:20 — Destruction and dispersal of the calf with different details: Both describe burning and finely grinding the calf and dispersing the residue into water; Deuteronomy names the mountain torrent, while Exodus adds making Israel drink it.

Deuteronomy 9:22
And at Burning, and at Testing, and at the Graves of Desire, you provoked the Lord your God.
  Numbers 11:1-3 — Named Burning provocation and its narrative: The recital names Burning among the provocations; Numbers explains that name through the fire kindled after grumbling and quenched when Moses prayed.
  Exodus 17:1-7 — Named Testing provocation and water dispute: The recital names Testing; Exodus identifies Testing and Reviling after the people's thirsty dispute and question whether the Lord was among them.
  Numbers 11:31-35 — Related craving-burial episode with distinct place-name forms: Deuteronomy names Graves of Desire among provocations; Numbers narrates quail, plague and burials at Graves of Craving, preserving the two supplied name forms rather than replacing either.

Deuteronomy 9:23
And when the Lord sent you forth from Kadesh-barnea, saying: Go up and inherit the land which I give you, you disobeyed the word of the Lord your God, and did not believe Him, and did not hearken unto His voice.
  Deuteronomy 1:19-26 — Kadesh-barnea command and refusal retold: Both locate the entry instruction at Kadesh-barnea and record the command to ascend and inherit followed by refusal to go up and disobedience to the Lord's word.
  Deuteronomy 1:32 — Repeated characterization as failure to trust: The accusation of not believing the Lord at the land-entry refusal corresponds to the earlier statement that the people did not trust Him in that matter.

Deuteronomy 9:24
You were disobedient toward the Lord from the day when He became known unto you.
  Deuteronomy 9:7 — Same-chapter encompassing charge of disobedience: Both frame the particular episodes with a broad accusation of continuing disobedience, preserving their different starting formulations of departure from Egypt and the Lord's becoming known.

Deuteronomy 9:25
And I besought the Lord forty days and forty nights, during which I besought Him; for the Lord had said that He would destroy you.
  Deuteronomy 9:18-19 — Same-chapter intercession repeated against threatened destruction: The forty-day entreaty because of threatened destruction resumes the earlier forty-day fast and fear of divine wrath, where Moses states that the Lord heard him.

Deuteronomy 9:26
And I prayed unto God, and said: O Lord, Lord, King of the gods, destroy not Thy people and Thy portion, whom Thou hast redeemed by Thy great strength, whom Thou hast brought forth out of the land of Egypt by Thy great strength, and by Thy mighty hand, and by Thine uplifted arm.
  Exodus 32:11 — Intercession appeals to the delivered people: Both plead for the people on the ground that the Lord brought them out of Egypt by great strength and an uplifted arm; Deuteronomy expands the invocation and redemption wording.

Deuteronomy 9:27
Remember Thy servants Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, unto whom Thou didst swear by Thyself. Look not upon the hardness of this people, nor upon their ungodliness, nor upon their sins,
  Exodus 32:13 — Intercession appeals to Abraham, Isaac and Jacob: Both ask the Lord to remember the named patriarchal servants and His self-sworn pledge, while Deuteronomy also pleads against considering the people's hardness and sins.

Deuteronomy 9:28
lest the inhabitants of the land whence Thou hast brought us forth say: Because the Lord was not able to bring them into the land which He promised them, and because He hated them, He brought them forth to slay them in the wilderness.
  Exodus 32:12 — Intercession invokes possible Egyptian interpretation: Both plead against the former land's inhabitants interpreting the departure as an intention to kill Israel; Deuteronomy additionally names alleged inability to bring them into the promised land.

Deuteronomy 9:29
And these are Thy people and Thine inheritance, whom Thou hast brought forth out of the land of Egypt by Thy great strength and by Thine uplifted arm.
  Exodus 32:11 — Divine ownership and Egyptian deliverance in the plea: Both call Israel the Lord's people and appeal to their mighty deliverance from Egypt; Deuteronomy also calls them His inheritance in this concluding appeal.
  Deuteronomy 9:26 — Same-chapter people-and-portion appeal frames the prayer: The closing identification as God's people and inheritance repeats the opening appeal for His redeemed people and portion, with great strength and uplifted arm recurring in both.

Deuteronomy 10:1
At that time the Lord said unto me: Hew for yourself two tablets of stone like unto the first, and come up unto Me into the mountain; and you shall make for yourself an ark of wood.
  Exodus 34:1-2 — Replacement-tablet command with added ark instruction: Both command hewing two stone tablets like the broken first pair and ascending the mountain; Deuteronomy additionally includes making a wooden ark in this command.

Deuteronomy 10:2
And I will write upon the tablets the words which were upon the first tablets, which you broke; and you shall put them in the ark.
  Exodus 34:1 — Promise to rewrite the broken tablets' words: Both promise writing the former words upon replacement stone tablets; Deuteronomy additionally commands placing them in the ark, which Exodus's replacement command does not mention.
  Exodus 25:16 — Tablets and the ark's instructed contents: The command to put the rewritten tablets in the ark corresponds to Exodus's instruction to place the given testimonies inside the ark, retaining the differing content descriptions.

Deuteronomy 10:3
And I made an ark of decay-resistant wood, and hewed two tablets of stone like unto the first; and I went up into the mountain, with the two tablets in my hands.
  Exodus 34:4 — Moses hews replacement tablets and ascends: Both narrate Moses cutting two tablets like the first and ascending with them; Deuteronomy also describes the decay-resistant wooden ark he made.
  Exodus 25:10 — Ark material in recital and construction instruction: Both specify an ark of decay-resistant wood; Exodus supplies the testimony-ark dimensions and later gold instructions, while Deuteronomy's recital here names the wood without those details.

Deuteronomy 10:4
And He wrote upon the tablets, according to the first writing, the ten words which the Lord spoke unto you in the mountain out of the midst of the fire; and the Lord gave them unto me.
  Exodus 34:28 — Ten words on the renewed covenant tablets: Both identify the renewed tablet contents as the ten words; Deuteronomy explicitly attributes the writing to the Lord and recalls the mountain and fire, preserving Exodus's own narration.

Deuteronomy 10:5
And I turned and came down from the mountain, and put the tablets into the ark which I had made; and they were there, as the Lord commanded me.
  Exodus 25:16 — Placement corresponding to the ark-content command: Deuteronomy narrates Moses placing the tablets in the ark as commanded; Exodus gives the instruction to place the divinely given testimonies inside it.

Deuteronomy 10:6
And the sons of Israel set out from Beeroth of the sons of Iakim to Misadai. Aaron died there and was buried there, and his son Eleazar served as priest in his place.
  Numbers 20:25-28 — Aaron's death and Eleazar's succession with distinct locations: Both recount Aaron's death and Eleazar's succeeding priestly role; Deuteronomy locates the death at Misadai, whereas Numbers specifies Mount Hor, without harmonizing the locations.

Deuteronomy 10:7
From there they set out to Gadgad, and from Gadgad to Jotbathah, a land of flowing torrents.
  Numbers 33:32-34 — Shared Gadgad-to-Jotbathah itinerary segment: Both give departure from Gadgad toward Jotbathah; Numbers calls the former Mount Gadgad and continues onward to Abronah, while Deuteronomy characterizes Jotbathah by flowing torrents.

Deuteronomy 10:8
At that time the Lord set apart the tribe of Levi, to bear the ark of the covenant of the Lord, to stand before the Lord to minister, and to bless in His name, unto this day.
  Deuteronomy 18:5 — Levitical choice for standing, ministry and blessing: Deuteronomy10 sets apart the tribe of Levi, while chapter18 speaks of the chosen priestly minister and his sons; both name standing, ministry and blessing, with ark-bearing additionally stated here.
  Numbers 4:15 — Ark-bearing tribe and particular carrying assignment: Deuteronomy describes Levi's separation to bear the covenant ark; Numbers assigns covered holy-object carrying specifically to Kohath's sons after the priests prepare them, preserving that narrower role.

Deuteronomy 10:9
Therefore the Levites have no portion or inheritance among their brethren; the Lord Himself is his inheritance, as He spoke unto him.
  Deuteronomy 18:1-2 — Repeated Levitical inheritance formula: Both deny Levi a portion or inheritance among the brethren and state that the Lord is his inheritance, with chapter18 also naming offerings as their food inheritance.
  Numbers 18:20-24 — No territorial inheritance and divine provision: Numbers directly tells Aaron that the Lord is his inheritance and separately denies territorial inheritance to Levites while assigning tithes; Deuteronomy applies its inheritance statement to Levites.

Deuteronomy 10:10
And I abode in the mountain forty days and forty nights; and the Lord hearkened unto me at that time also, and the Lord would not destroy you.
  Deuteronomy 9:18-19 — Renewed forty-day stay and heard intercession: Both mention forty days and nights and the Lord hearing Moses amid threatened destruction; chapter9 expressly adds fasting and fear of the Lord's wrath.
  Exodus 34:28 — Forty-day renewed-tablet mountain stay: Both describe Moses remaining before the Lord forty days and nights in the renewed-tablet setting; Exodus additionally names fasting and the covenant's ten words.

Deuteronomy 10:11
And the Lord said unto me: Go, set forth before this people, and let them enter and inherit the land which I sware unto their fathers to give them.
  Exodus 32:34 — Renewed instruction to lead the people onward: Both follow the calf crisis with a command for Moses to lead the people to the announced destination; Deuteronomy explicitly identifies entry and ancestral sworn inheritance.

Deuteronomy 10:12
And now, O Israel, what does the Lord your God require of you, but to fear the Lord your God, to walk in all His ways, and to love Him, and to serve the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul,
  Deuteronomy 6:5 — Wholehearted love within the required allegiance: Both require love for the Lord with all heart and soul; chapter6 also says all strength, while this verse combines love with fear, walking and service.
  Deuteronomy 6:13 — Fear and service in repeated allegiance instructions: Both require fearing and serving the Lord; chapter6 additionally commands cleaving and swearing by His name, while chapter10 expressly calls for wholehearted love and walking in His ways.

Deuteronomy 10:13
to keep the commandments of the Lord your God, and His ordinances which I command you this day, that it may be well with you?
  Deuteronomy 6:24 — Command keeping and the stated good of Israel: Both connect keeping divine ordinances with Israel's wellbeing, with chapter6 additionally stating fear of the Lord and continued life as purposes of that instruction.

Deuteronomy 10:14
Behold, unto the Lord your God belong heaven and the heaven of heaven, the earth and all that is therein.
  Exodus 19:5 — Universal divine ownership beside Israel's special covenant: Deuteronomy attributes heaven and earth with their contents to the Lord; Exodus likewise says all the earth is His while describing Israel's particular covenant possession.

Deuteronomy 10:15
Yet the Lord preferred your fathers, to love them, and chose their seed after them, even you, above all the nations, as at this day.
  Deuteronomy 7:7-8 — Love and choice linked to the fathers: Both connect Israel's election with divine love and the fathers, while chapter7 explicitly rejects superior population as the reason and recalls the ancestral oath.

Deuteronomy 10:16
And you shall circumcise your hardness of heart, and shall stiffen your neck no more.
  Deuteronomy 9:6 — Hard heart command answers the stiff-necked diagnosis: The command to circumcise heart-hardness and cease stiffening the neck answers the earlier description of Israel as stiff-necked, without reducing the metaphor to a physical procedure.

Deuteronomy 10:17
For the Lord your God, He is God of gods and Lord of lords, the great and mighty and fearful God, who shows no partiality, neither takes a bribe;
  Deuteronomy 16:19 — Divine impartiality and corresponding judicial prohibitions: The Lord is described as taking neither partiality nor a bribe; the later instruction forbids those same practices in Israel's judges, naming the distortion bribery causes.

Deuteronomy 10:18
who executes judgment for the sojourner, and the orphan, and the widow, and loves the sojourner, to give him bread and clothing.
  Deuteronomy 24:17-21 — Divine care and communal provision for vulnerable groups: The Lord executes judgment for sojourner, orphan and widow and gives the sojourner food and clothing; chapter24 requires their just treatment and access to harvest produce.

Deuteronomy 10:19
And you shall love the sojourner, for you were sojourners in the land of Egypt.
  Leviticus 19:33-34 — Love for resident foreigners grounded in Egyptian experience: Both command love for the resident foreigner or sojourner and give Israel's own foreign residence in Egypt as the explicit reason, with Leviticus also prohibiting oppression.
  Exodus 22:20 — Egyptian sojourning grounds treatment of foreigners: Both ground the treatment of a resident foreigner in Israel's own residence in Egypt, with Deuteronomy commanding love and Exodus prohibiting ill-treatment and affliction.

Deuteronomy 10:20
You shall fear the Lord your God, and you shall serve Him, and you shall cleave unto Him, and you shall swear by His name.
  Deuteronomy 6:13 — Repeated fear, service, cleaving and oath formula: Both command fearing the Lord, serving Him, cleaving to Him and swearing by His name, retaining this edition's complete four-part instruction.

Deuteronomy 10:21
He is your boast, and He is your God, who has done among you these great and glorious things which your eyes have seen.
  Deuteronomy 11:2-7 — Witnessed divine works as grounds for allegiance: The great glorious deeds seen by Israel's eyes are specified in the following recital of Egyptian signs, sea deliverance and wilderness events witnessed by the addressed hearers.

Deuteronomy 10:22
Your fathers went down into Egypt with seventy souls; but now the Lord your God has made you as the stars of heaven in multitude.
  Genesis 15:5 — Star-multitude pledge and claimed multiplication: Genesis promises Abram descendants like the stars he cannot count; Deuteronomy describes the people's present multitude by that same star image after their Egyptian sojourn.
  Genesis 46:26-27 — Egyptian household totals preserved as differing accounts: Deuteronomy says the fathers entered Egypt with seventy souls; Genesis distinguishes sixty-six direct entrants and a household total of seventy-five, so no numerical equivalence is asserted.

Deuteronomy 11:1
And you shall love the Lord your God, and keep His charge, and His ordinances, and His judgments, all your days.
  Deuteronomy 10:12-13 — Renewed covenant demand: The command to love the Lord and keep His charges resumes the preceding demand to love, serve and keep His commandments for Israel's good.

Deuteronomy 11:2
And know this day that I speak unto you, not unto your children, who have neither known nor seen the chastening of the Lord your God, and His mighty deeds, and His mighty hand, and His uplifted arm,
  Deuteronomy 11:7 — Same-chapter eyewitness framing: Moses distinguishes his hearers from children without these experiences; the later statement specifies that the hearers' own eyes saw the Lord's great works.

Deuteronomy 11:3
and His signs and His wonders which He did in the midst of Egypt unto Pharaoh king of Egypt and unto all his land,
  Exodus 7:3-5 — Historical recital and announced Egyptian signs: The recital names signs against Pharaoh and Egypt; Exodus announces multiplied signs and judgments by which Egypt will know the Lord when Israel departs.

Deuteronomy 11:4
and what He did unto the host of the Egyptians, unto their chariots and their horsemen, when He caused the water of the Red Sea to overflow them as they pursued you, and the Lord destroyed them unto this day,
  Exodus 14:26-30 — Historical recital and sea-deliverance account: Moses recalls the destruction of Egypt's horsemen and chariots by the sea; Exodus describes returning waters covering the pursuing chariots, horsemen and army.

Deuteronomy 11:5
and what He did unto you in the wilderness, until you came unto this place,
  Deuteronomy 11:6 — Same-chapter wilderness recital and particular example: The summary of the Lord's wilderness deeds is followed by the specific judgment on Dathan and Abiram, whose households and tents the earth swallowed.

Deuteronomy 11:6
and what He did unto Dathan and Abiram, the sons of Eliab son of Reuben, when the earth opened her mouth and swallowed them, and their houses, and their tents, and all their substance that was with them, in the midst of all Israel.
  Numbers 16:27-33 — Historical recital and named wilderness judgment: Both identify Dathan and Abiram and describe the earth opening to swallow their households and possessions; Numbers supplies the immediate narrative surrounding this judgment.

Deuteronomy 11:7
For your eyes have seen all the great works of the Lord, which He has done unto you this day.
  Deuteronomy 11:2 — Same-chapter eyewitness framing: The affirmation that the hearers' own eyes saw these deeds answers the earlier distinction between these hearers and children who had not known or seen them.

Deuteronomy 11:8
And you shall keep all His commandments which I command you this day, that you may live and multiply, and enter and inherit the land whither you pass over the Jordan to inherit it,
  Deuteronomy 4:1 — Obedience linked with entry and possession: Both urge Israel to keep the delivered ordinances so that the people may enter and inherit the promised land; chapter4 also explicitly connects this with living.

Deuteronomy 11:9
that you may prolong your days upon the land which the Lord sware unto your fathers to give unto them and unto their seed after them, a land flowing with milk and honey.
  Deuteronomy 6:3 — Promised land, ancestral pledge and obedient prosperity: Both connect heeding instruction with the land flowing with milk and honey, while invoking the Lord's pledge to the fathers and promised wellbeing there.

Deuteronomy 11:10
For the land whither you go to inherit it is not as the land of Egypt, whence you came forth, where they sow seed and water it with their feet, as a garden of herbs.
  Deuteronomy 11:11 — Same-chapter contrasting water supplies: Egypt's seed and watering with the feet are contrasted with the coming land's hills and plains drinking heaven's rain; no particular irrigation device is identified.

Deuteronomy 11:11
But the land whither you go to inherit it is a land of hills and plains; it shall drink water of the rain of heaven,
  Deuteronomy 11:10 — Same-chapter contrasting water supplies: The land watered by rain from heaven is explicitly contrasted with Egypt's sowing and watering with the feet, preserving the two descriptions without importing a mechanism.

Deuteronomy 11:12
a land which the Lord your God cares for; the eyes of the Lord your God are always upon it, from the beginning of the year even unto the end of the year.
  Deuteronomy 11:13-15 — Same-chapter divine care and specified provision: The land under the Lord's eyes throughout the year receives specified rain, crops and cattle grass in the following conditional promise; the descriptions of care and obedience remain distinct.

Deuteronomy 11:13
And if you diligently hearken unto all His commandments which I command you this day, to love the Lord your God, and to serve Him with all your heart and with all your soul,
  Deuteronomy 6:5 — Wholehearted love in covenant instruction: Both require love for the Lord with the whole heart and soul; this passage additionally states hearkening to commands and serving Him in that wholehearted manner.

Deuteronomy 11:14
He shall give the rain unto your land in its season, the early and the latter rain; and you shall gather in your grain, and your wine, and your oil.
  Leviticus 26:3-5 — Parallel obedience-and-rain promises: Both connect keeping divine instruction with seasonal rain and agricultural yield; Deuteronomy specifies early and latter rain together with grain, wine and oil.

Deuteronomy 11:15
And He shall give grass in your fields for your cattle; and when you have eaten and are filled,
  Deuteronomy 11:14 — Same-chapter complementary agricultural provisions: Grass for cattle and human fullness complete the preceding rain-and-crop promise, distinguishing pasture for livestock from the named grain, wine and oil harvests.

Deuteronomy 11:16
take heed unto yourself, lest your heart be enlarged, and you transgress, and serve other gods, and bow down unto them,
  Deuteronomy 6:14-15 — Warning against other gods and divine wrath: Both warn Israel against turning to other gods; the earlier passage expressly connects this with the Lord's jealousy and anger, developed here by the following drought warning.

Deuteronomy 11:17
and the Lord be kindled to wrath against you, and shut up heaven, so that there is no rain, and the land does not yield her fruit, and you perish speedily from the good land which the Lord has given you.
  Deuteronomy 28:23-24 — Parallel covenant drought warnings: The closed heaven and withheld rain correspond to the later curse describing bronze heaven, iron earth and dust instead of rain, without equating their distinct imagery.

Deuteronomy 11:18
And you shall lay these words in your heart and in your soul, and bind them for a sign upon your hand, and it shall be immovable before your eyes.
  Deuteronomy 6:6-8 — Repeated heart, hand and eye instruction: Both place these words in the heart and soul and bind them as a hand sign and something immovable before the eyes, preserving the repeated household-remembrance instruction.

Deuteronomy 11:19
And you shall teach them unto your children, to speak of them when you sit in the house, and when you walk in the way, and when you lie down, and when you rise up.
  Deuteronomy 6:7 — Repeated settings for teaching children: Both command teaching the words to children while sitting at home, walking on the road, lying down and rising, giving the same recurring daily occasions.

Deuteronomy 11:20
And you shall write them upon the doorposts of your houses and of your gates,
  Deuteronomy 6:9 — Repeated written household reminder: Both command writing the words on the doorposts of houses and on gates, preserving a particular household and communal location for remembrance.

Deuteronomy 11:21
that your days and the days of your sons may be prolonged upon the land which the Lord sware unto your fathers to give them, as the days of heaven upon the earth.
  Deuteronomy 6:2 — Obedience and lengthened generations in the land: The promise of multiplied days for the hearers and children corresponds to the earlier instruction concerning hearers, sons and sons' sons, with prolonged days as its stated purpose.

Deuteronomy 11:22
And it shall be, if you diligently hearken unto all these commandments which I command you this day to do, to love the Lord our God, and to walk in all His ways, and to cleave unto Him,
  Deuteronomy 10:12-13 — Repeated love, walking and command-keeping: Both combine loving the Lord, walking in His ways and keeping His instruction; this verse's cleaving language is retained without treating the two lists as identical.

Deuteronomy 11:23
that the Lord shall cast out all these nations from before you, and you shall inherit nations great and mightier than you.
