  Deuteronomy 7:1-2 — Expulsion of stronger nations: Both describe the Lord handing over or driving out nations before Israel, explicitly calling those nations stronger or greater than the receiving people.

Deuteronomy 11:24
Every place whereon the sole of your foot shall tread shall be yours. From the wilderness and Anti-Lebanon, and from the great river, the river Euphrates, even unto the western sea, shall be your borders.
  Joshua 1:3-4 — Promised territory and footsole formula: Both promise the territory trodden by the feet and name Anti-Lebanon, the Euphrates and western sea among the boundaries, preserving this edition's geographical wording.

Deuteronomy 11:25
None shall stand before you; the Lord your God shall lay the dread of you and the fear of you upon all the land whereon you shall tread, as the Lord has spoken unto you.
  Joshua 1:5 — Related assurances that opponents cannot withstand: The assurance to Israel that no one will withstand them corresponds to the assurance addressed personally to Joshua; the different recipients and accompanying language remain distinct.

Deuteronomy 11:26
Behold, I set before you this day a blessing and a curse:
  Joshua 9:2e — Blessing-and-curse announcement and later law reading: Moses sets blessing and curse before Israel; the actual Joshua continuation unit records the reading of all the law's words, including its blessings and curses.

Deuteronomy 11:27
the blessing, if you hearken unto the commandments of the Lord your God, which I command you this day;
  Deuteronomy 28:1-6 — Conditional blessing and developed blessing list: Blessing is conditioned on hearkening to the Lord's commands; the later chapter introduces the same condition and specifies blessings upon city, field, offspring and daily movements.

Deuteronomy 11:28
and the curses, if you do not hearken unto the commandments of the Lord your God, which I command you this day, and wander from the way which I commanded you, going to serve other gods which you have not known.
  Deuteronomy 28:15-19 — Conditional curse and developed curse list: Failure to hearken brings the announced curse; the later chapter develops curses upon city, field, offspring and movements, while this verse specifically also mentions other gods.

Deuteronomy 11:29
And it shall be, when the Lord your God brings you into the land whither you pass over to inherit it, that you shall set the blessing upon Mount Gerizim, and the curse upon Mount Ebal.
  Deuteronomy 27:12-13 — Mountain assignment and later tribal arrangement: Gerizim is assigned blessing and Ebal curse; the later instruction names the tribes standing upon each mountain for those respective declarations.
  Joshua 9:2d — Mountain command and subsequent ceremony: The later Joshua unit places halves of Israel near Gerizim and Ebal with the ark and officials, expressly relating the arrangement to Moses' prior blessing command.

Deuteronomy 11:30
Behold, are they not beyond the Jordan, behind the way of the setting sun, in the land of Canaan that lies toward the west, adjoining Gilgal, near the lofty oak?
  Deuteronomy 11:29 — Same-chapter location of the specified mountains: The location beyond Jordan toward the west and adjoining Gilgal identifies the mountains just named for blessing and curse; the lofty oak receives no imported site identification.

Deuteronomy 11:31
For you pass over the Jordan, to enter and inherit the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession for ever; and you shall inherit it, and dwell therein.
  Joshua 1:2 — Jordan crossing and inheritance instruction: Both announce crossing Jordan into the land given to Israel; Joshua receives the direct command to rise and lead the people across after Moses' death.

Deuteronomy 11:32
And you shall take heed to do all His ordinances and these judgments, which I set before you this day.
  Deuteronomy 12:1 — Transition into the detailed land ordinances: The requirement to perform the ordinances and judgments leads directly into the heading identifying ordinances and judgments to observe throughout life in the inherited land.

Deuteronomy 12:1
And these are the ordinances and judgments which you shall take heed to do in the land which the Lord, the God of your fathers, gives you for a possession, all the days that you live upon the earth.
  Deuteronomy 12:2-7 — Same-chapter legal heading and specified commands: The heading about ordinances in the inherited land introduces particular instructions to destroy prior cult sites and bring gifts to the Lord's chosen place for rejoicing.

Deuteronomy 12:2
You shall utterly destroy all the places wherein the nations whose possession you inherit served their gods, upon the high mountains, and upon the hills, and under the leafy tree.
  Deuteronomy 7:5 — Parallel destruction of prior cult installations: Both require destruction of the nations' cult installations; this verse locates their worship on mountains, hills and under trees, while chapter7 specifies altars and other objects.

Deuteronomy 12:3
And you shall pull down their altars, and break their pillars, and cut down their sacred groves, and burn the graven images of their gods with fire; and their name shall perish from that place.
  Deuteronomy 7:5 — Repeated instructions against cult objects: Both enumerate altars, pillars, groves and carved images for destruction, with this verse also commanding removal of their names from that place.

Deuteronomy 12:4
You shall not do so unto the Lord your God,
  Deuteronomy 12:5 — Same-chapter negative and positive worship instruction: The prohibition against doing so to the Lord is followed by seeking His chosen place and name; the link preserves the stated contrast without resolving every possible antecedent.

Deuteronomy 12:5
but unto the place which the Lord your God shall choose in one of your tribes, to set His name there, that it may be called upon, you shall seek and come thither.
  2 Chronicles 6:5-6 — Chosen place and later explicit Jerusalem testimony: Deuteronomy commands seeking the place chosen for the Lord's name without naming it; the later testimony explicitly identifies Jerusalem as the chosen city for that name.

Deuteronomy 12:6
And you shall bring thither your whole burnt offerings, and your sacrifices, and your firstfruits, and your vowed offerings, and your freewill offerings, and the firstborn of your cattle and of your sheep.
  Deuteronomy 12:11 — Same-chapter repeated destination for offerings: Both direct listed offerings and gifts to the place chosen for the Lord's name, though their particular lists and descriptions are not identical.

Deuteronomy 12:7
And you shall eat there before the Lord your God, and rejoice in all that you put your hand unto, you and your houses, because the Lord your God has blessed you.
  Deuteronomy 16:11 — Rejoicing before the Lord with the household: Both associate rejoicing with the Lord's chosen place and household participation; the festival instruction additionally enumerates Levites, sojourners, orphans and widows.

Deuteronomy 12:8
You shall not do all that we do here this day, every man that which is pleasing in his own sight;
  Deuteronomy 12:9-11 — Same-chapter present practice and coming settlement: The prohibition on continuing each person's present practice is explained by the lack of rest and inheritance, followed by settlement and the chosen offering place.

Deuteronomy 12:9
for you have not yet come unto the rest and unto the inheritance which the Lord your God gives you.
  Joshua 21:43-45 — Awaited land and rest and later settlement summary: The rest and inheritance not yet reached are later described as land given to Israel with rest around them and the fulfillment of the Lord's good word.

Deuteronomy 12:10
And you shall pass over the Jordan, and dwell in the land which the Lord your God gives you to inherit; and He shall give you rest from all your enemies round about, and you shall dwell in safety.
  Joshua 21:44 — Promised rest from enemies and later narrative summary: The promised settlement and rest from surrounding enemies correspond to Joshua's statement that the Lord gave rest around Israel and delivered their enemies into their hands.

Deuteronomy 12:11
And it shall be, at the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there, you shall bring thither all that I command you this day: your whole burnt offerings, and your sacrifices, and your tithes, and the firstfruits of your hands, and your gifts, and all the choice gifts which you shall vow unto your God.
  Deuteronomy 12:5-6 — Same-chapter chosen destination for named gifts: The command to bring gifts to the chosen place resumes the earlier direction to seek that place and bring the named offerings there, preserving differences between the lists.

Deuteronomy 12:12
And you shall rejoice before the Lord your God, you, and your sons, and your daughters, and your male slaves, and your female slaves, and the Levite within your gates; for he has neither portion nor inheritance with you.
  Numbers 18:23-24 — Levites' lack of territorial inheritance: The household rejoicing instruction includes the Levite because he has no allotment with Israel; Numbers similarly denies Levites an inheritance and assigns tithes for their service.

Deuteronomy 12:13
Take heed unto yourself, that you do not offer your whole burnt offerings in every place that you see,
  Leviticus 17:8-9 — Related restrictions upon sacrificial location: Both reject offering sacrifices at an arbitrary location; Leviticus specifies the Tent entrance, while Deuteronomy's following instruction specifies the chosen place, without identifying the sites as one.

Deuteronomy 12:14
but in the place which the Lord your God shall choose in one of your tribes, you shall offer there your whole burnt offerings, and you shall do there all that I command you this day.
  Deuteronomy 12:13 — Same-chapter chosen-place exception to arbitrary sacrifice: The requirement to offer burnt offerings at the chosen place answers the immediately preceding prohibition against presenting them in every place one sees.

Deuteronomy 12:15
Nevertheless, you may slaughter and eat flesh according to all your desire, according to the blessing of the Lord your God which He has given you in every city. The unclean among you and the clean may eat it together, as the gazelle or the deer.
  Deuteronomy 12:20-22 — Same-chapter local meat permission repeated and qualified: Both permit local meat eating for clean and unclean persons using gazelle and deer comparisons; the later instruction additionally addresses enlarged territory and distance from the chosen place.

Deuteronomy 12:16
Only you shall not eat the blood; you shall pour it upon the earth as water.
  Leviticus 17:13 — Parallel blood-pouring instructions with distinct details: Both require blood to be poured onto earth; Deuteronomy compares it with water, while Leviticus adds earth covering in its instruction concerning hunted animals and birds.

Deuteronomy 12:17
You may not eat within your cities the tithe of your grain, or of your wine, or of your oil, nor the firstborn of your cattle or of your sheep, nor any of the vows which you shall vow, nor your pledged offerings, nor the firstfruits of your hands;
  Deuteronomy 12:18 — Same-chapter forbidden local consumption and required destination: The prohibition on eating specified tithes and gifts within the cities is answered by eating before the Lord in His chosen place with the named participants.

Deuteronomy 12:18
but you shall eat them before the Lord your God, in the place which the Lord your God shall choose for Himself, you, and your son, and your daughter, and your male slave, and your female slave, and the sojourner within your cities. And you shall rejoice before the Lord your God in all that you put your hand unto.
  Deuteronomy 12:17 — Same-chapter required destination for restricted foods: Eating these gifts before the Lord at the chosen place is contrasted with the preceding prohibition on city consumption; this verse's household and sojourner wording is retained.

Deuteronomy 12:19
Take heed unto yourself, that you do not forsake the Levite all the time that you live upon the land.
  Deuteronomy 14:27 — Levite's inclusion and the warning against neglect: The warning not to forsake the Levite corresponds to his inclusion in the later household meal, where his lack of portion and inheritance is explicitly stated.

Deuteronomy 12:20
And if the Lord your God enlarges your borders, as He has spoken unto you, and you say: I will eat flesh, because your soul desires to eat flesh, you may eat flesh according to all the desire of your soul.
  Deuteronomy 12:21-22 — Same-chapter expanded-territory meat permission and implementation: The permission to eat desired meat after territorial enlargement is developed through distance from the chosen place, local slaughter and shared clean-and-unclean eating.

Deuteronomy 12:21
And if the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there is too far from you, you shall slaughter of your cattle and of your sheep which God has given you, as I commanded you, and eat within your cities according to the desire of your soul.
  Leviticus 17:3-5 — Different stated slaughter regulations: Deuteronomy permits local cattle and sheep slaughter when the chosen place is distant; Leviticus requires bringing the named slaughtered animals to the Tent entrance, preserving the differing instructions.

Deuteronomy 12:22
As the gazelle and the deer are eaten, so you shall eat it; the unclean among you and the clean shall eat it alike.
  Deuteronomy 15:22-23 — Related local clean-and-unclean meal rules: Both compare local meat eating with gazelle and deer and allow clean and unclean persons; chapter15 applies this to a blemished firstborn and separately prohibits blood.

Deuteronomy 12:23
Take diligent heed that you do not eat blood; for its blood is life, and the life shall not be eaten with the flesh.
  Leviticus 17:11-14 — Blood prohibition grounded in life: Both connect blood with life and forbid its consumption; Leviticus additionally states its assigned altar role and gives particular handling instructions for hunted animals.

Deuteronomy 12:24
You shall not eat it; you shall pour it upon the earth as water.
  Deuteronomy 12:16 — Same-chapter repeated blood disposal formula: Both prohibit consuming blood and command pouring it onto the earth like water, repeating the particular prohibition alongside the chapter's meat permissions.

Deuteronomy 12:25
You shall not eat it, that it may be well with you and with your sons after you, if you do that which is good and pleasing before the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 12:23-24 — Same-chapter wellbeing attached to the blood prohibition: The promise of wellbeing for the hearer and children follows the explicit command not to consume blood and to pour it upon the earth.

Deuteronomy 12:26
Only your holy things which you have, and your vowed offerings, you shall take, and come unto the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there.
  Deuteronomy 12:21-22 — Same-chapter holy gifts distinguished from ordinary local meat: Holy things and vows must be carried to the chosen place, whereas the earlier permission allows distant local slaughter and clean-and-unclean eating of ordinary meat.

Deuteronomy 12:27
And you shall prepare your whole burnt offerings; you shall offer the flesh upon the altar of the Lord your God, and you shall pour the blood of your sacrifices against the base of the altar of the Lord your God; and you shall eat the flesh.
  Leviticus 17:6 — Related altar treatment with different specifications: Both direct sacrificial blood to the Lord's altar; Deuteronomy describes its base and flesh instructions, while Leviticus specifies blood around the altar and fat upon it.

Deuteronomy 12:28
Keep and hear and do all the words which I command you, that it may be well with you and with your sons for ever, if you do that which is good and pleasing before the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 12:25 — Same-chapter repeated intergenerational wellbeing formula: Both connect doing what is right before the Lord with wellbeing for the hearer and descendants; verse28 extends the admonition to all these words.

Deuteronomy 12:29
And if the Lord your God destroys from before you the nations whither you go to inherit their land, and you inherit their possession and dwell in their land,
  Deuteronomy 7:1-2 — Dispossession preceding Israel's occupation: Both describe the Lord removing or delivering the nations before Israel's entry and possession, setting the following cult warnings within that anticipated occupation.

Deuteronomy 12:30
take heed unto yourself, that you do not seek to follow them after they have been destroyed from before you. You shall not inquire after their gods, saying: How do these nations serve their gods? I also will do so.
  Deuteronomy 12:31 — Same-chapter imitation warning and specified abomination: The warning against asking how the nations served their gods is followed by the particular example of sons and daughters burned for those gods.

Deuteronomy 12:31
You shall not do so unto the Lord your God; for the abominations which the Lord hated they did unto their gods, for they burn their sons and their daughters with fire unto their gods.
  Jeremiah 7:31 — Prohibited child-burning and later prophetic condemnation: Deuteronomy condemns burning sons and daughters for gods; Jeremiah condemns burning sons and daughters at Topheth and says this was neither commanded nor conceived by the Lord.

Deuteronomy 13:1
Every word which I command you this day, you shall take heed to do; you shall not add unto it, neither shall you take away from it.
  Deuteronomy 4:2 — Repeated prohibition on adding or removing words: Both prohibit adding to or taking away from the commanded words, preserving this edition's placement at13:1 rather than substituting a different chapter division.

Deuteronomy 13:2
And if there arises among you a prophet, or a dreamer of dreams, and he gives you a sign or a wonder,
  Deuteronomy 13:3-4 — Same-chapter prophet-sign case and response: The introduction of a prophet or dreamer offering a sign is developed by the sign's occurrence, enticement to other gods and the command not to listen.

Deuteronomy 13:3
and the sign or the wonder comes to pass, whereof he spoke unto you, saying: Let us go and serve other gods, which you have not known,
  Deuteronomy 18:20-22 — Different prophetic tests: Here a fulfilled sign accompanies enticement to other gods and must be rejected; chapter18 also addresses other-god speech and distinguishes a prediction that does not occur.

Deuteronomy 13:4
you shall not hearken unto the words of that prophet, or unto that dreamer of dreams; for the Lord God tests you, to know whether you love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul.
  Deuteronomy 8:2-5 — Testing the heart in distinct circumstances: Both describe divine testing to reveal the heart's response; the wilderness account concerns humbled dependence and command keeping, while this passage tests wholehearted love amid religious enticement.

Deuteronomy 13:5
You shall walk after the Lord your God, and fear Him, and keep His commandments, and hearken unto His voice, and be joined unto Him.
  Deuteronomy 10:20 — Repeated exclusive devotion instructions: Both command fearing the Lord and remaining joined or cleaving to Him; chapter13 adds walking and heeding His voice, while chapter10 names service and swearing by His name.

Deuteronomy 13:6
And that prophet, or that dreamer of dreams, shall die; for he spoke to lead you astray from the Lord your God, who brought you forth out of the land of Egypt, and redeemed you from bondage, to thrust you out of the way wherein the Lord your God commanded you to walk. And you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.
  Deuteronomy 18:20 — Parallel death judgments for other-god prophetic speech: Both prescribe death in a case involving prophetic speech for other gods; chapter13 also names the dreamer and grounds the offense in turning Israel from its deliverer.

Deuteronomy 13:7
And if your brother, the son of your father or of your mother, or your son, or your daughter, or the wife of your bosom, or your friend who is as your own soul, entices you secretly, saying: Let us go and serve other gods, which you have not known, you nor your fathers,
  Deuteronomy 13:9-11 — Same-chapter intimate enticement case and prescribed response: The enticement by a brother, child, wife or close friend receives a particular response of refusing consent, reporting and the stated communal judgment procedure.

Deuteronomy 13:8
of the gods of the nations round about you, whether near unto you or far from you, from one end of the earth unto the other,
  Deuteronomy 13:7 — Same-chapter scope of the enticer's proposed gods: The surrounding nations' gods, whether near or distant, continue the preceding private invitation to worship gods unknown to the hearer and ancestors.

Deuteronomy 13:9
you shall not consent unto him, neither shall you hearken unto him; neither shall your eye spare him, nor shall you yearn for him, nor conceal him.
  Deuteronomy 13:10-11 — Same-chapter refusal followed by reporting and judgment: The prohibitions on consent, listening, pity and concealment are followed by reporting, first-hand participation and communal stoning; the source's reporting wording is preserved.

Deuteronomy 13:10
You shall surely report him; your hands shall be first upon him to put him to death, and afterward the hands of all the people.
  Deuteronomy 17:7 — Initial hands and subsequent communal participation: Both put particular hands first before those of all the people in execution; chapter17 specifically identifies witnesses, whereas this case addresses the person being enticed.

Deuteronomy 13:11
And they shall stone him with stones, and he shall die; for he sought to turn you away from the Lord your God, who brought you forth out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage.
  Deuteronomy 13:7-10 — Same-chapter stated reason for the enticer's judgment: Stoning is grounded in the attempted turning from the Lord who brought Israel from Egypt; the preceding verses define the private enticement and required response.

Deuteronomy 13:12
And all Israel shall hear and fear, and shall no more do such an evil thing among you.
  Deuteronomy 17:12-13 — Communal hearing and fear as deterrence: Both conclude a prescribed death judgment with Israel hearing and fearing so that the offense is not repeated; chapter17 concerns defiance of priestly or judicial authority.

Deuteronomy 13:13
And if you hear it said in one of your cities which the Lord your God gives you to dwell in,
  Deuteronomy 13:14-16 — Same-chapter reported city offense and following process: A report about a city given for dwelling is developed into the charge of enticement, diligent investigation and judgment only after the allegation is established.

Deuteronomy 13:14
Lawless men have gone forth from among you, and have turned aside all the inhabitants of their city, saying: Let us go and serve other gods, which you have not known,
  Deuteronomy 13:15 — Same-chapter city allegation requiring investigation: The allegation that lawless men led a city's inhabitants after other gods is immediately followed by searching, inquiry and verification that the alleged abomination is true.

Deuteronomy 13:15
then you shall inquire and search diligently; and behold, if the matter is clearly true, and this abomination has been done among you,
  Deuteronomy 17:4-6 — Parallel investigation before idolatry judgment: Both require careful inquiry and confirmation of an alleged abomination before judgment; chapter17 adds witness requirements for the individual case rather than this city-wide case.

Deuteronomy 13:16
you shall surely slay all the inhabitants of that city with the sword; you shall utterly devote it to destruction, and all that is therein.
  Deuteronomy 13:14-15 — Same-chapter city judgment conditioned on verified charge: The prescribed destruction of the city follows the specific allegation of other-god enticement and the requirement to establish its truth through diligent investigation.

Deuteronomy 13:17
And you shall gather all its spoil into its streets, and burn the city with fire, and all its spoil, all together before the Lord your God; and it shall be uninhabited for ever, and shall not be built again.
  Joshua 6:24-26 — Related city-burning and non-rebuilding language: Both describe burning a city and language against rebuilding; Joshua concerns Jericho, includes metal-treasure exceptions and a builder's curse, rather than this law's proven apostate city.

Deuteronomy 13:18
And nothing of that which is devoted to destruction shall cleave to your hand, that the Lord may turn from the fierceness of His wrath, and grant you mercy, and have mercy upon you, and multiply you, as the Lord sware unto your fathers,
  Joshua 6:18-19 — Devoted property barred from private appropriation: Both warn against taking devoted property; Joshua separately assigns specified metals to the Lord's treasury, whereas Deuteronomy's preceding city instruction burns its spoil.

Deuteronomy 13:19
if you hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God, to keep all His commandments which I command you this day, to do that which is good and pleasing before the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 13:4-5 — Same-chapter obedience concluding the testing-and-allegiance frame: The closing condition of hearing, keeping commands and doing right returns to the earlier test of wholehearted love and command to follow, heed and cleave to the Lord.

Deuteronomy 14:1
You are sons of the Lord your God; you shall not perform purification rites, neither shall you make a bald place between your eyes for the dead.
  Leviticus 21:1-5 — Related mourning baldness prohibitions with different addressees: Deuteronomy addresses the Lord's children and prohibits purification rites and a bald place for the dead; Leviticus' priestly instruction includes the particular mourning bald-patch prohibition.

Deuteronomy 14:2
For you are a holy people unto the Lord your God; and the Lord your God has chosen you to be unto Him a people for His own possession, out of all the nations upon the face of the earth.
  Deuteronomy 7:6 — Repeated holy and chosen possession description: Both describe Israel as holy to the Lord and chosen from the peoples as His possession, providing the stated communal identity behind the surrounding instructions.

Deuteronomy 14:3
You shall eat no abominable thing.
  Leviticus 11:46-47 — Dietary prohibition and clean-unclean discrimination: The prohibition on eating abominations introduces a food list; Leviticus closes its animal law by distinguishing clean from unclean and edible from inedible creatures.

Deuteronomy 14:4
These are the beasts which you may eat: a calf of the cattle, and a lamb of the sheep, and a young male goat of the goats,
  Deuteronomy 14:6 — Same-chapter named permitted livestock and qualifying criteria: The permitted calf, sheep and goat list is followed by the split-hoof and cud-chewing criteria for eating beasts, linking the named livestock with the stated food rule.

Deuteronomy 14:5
deer, gazelle, bubalos, goat-stag, white-rumped antelope, oryx, and giraffe.
  Deuteronomy 14:6 — Same-chapter named wild animals and stated food criteria: The named wild-animal list precedes the split-hoof and cud-chewing criterion; the connection does not adjudicate the modern zoological identities of bubalos, goat-stag, oryx or giraffe.

Deuteronomy 14:6
Every beast that divides the hoof, and cleaves its hoof into two parts, and brings up the cud among the beasts, these you may eat.
  Leviticus 11:3 — Parallel split-hoof and cud-chewing criteria: Both permit eating beasts that divide the hoof and chew the cud, stating the two traits together rather than treating either trait alone as sufficient.

Deuteronomy 14:7
And these you shall not eat of those that bring up the cud, and of those that divide the hoof and cleave its parts: the camel, and the hare, and the rock hyrax; for they bring up the cud, but do not divide the hoof; they are unclean unto you.
  Leviticus 11:4-6 — Parallel exceptions to the two-trait rule: Both exclude named cud-chewing animals that lack divided hooves and share camel and hare examples; rock-hyrax and choirogryllios wording is preserved without asserting name equivalence.

Deuteronomy 14:8
And the swine, for it divides the hoof and cleaves its parts, but does not chew the cud, is unclean unto you. Of their flesh you shall not eat, neither shall you touch their carcasses.
  Leviticus 11:7-8 — Parallel pig and carcass prohibitions: Both exclude the pig because its divided hoof is not accompanied by cud chewing and prohibit eating its flesh and touching the specified carcasses.

Deuteronomy 14:9
And these you may eat of all that are in the waters: whatsoever has fins and scales, you may eat.
  Leviticus 11:9 — Parallel aquatic food criteria: Both allow water creatures possessing fins and scales, joining the same two characteristics as the condition for eating them.

Deuteronomy 14:10
And whatsoever does not have fins and scales, you shall not eat; they are unclean unto you.
  Leviticus 11:10-12 — Parallel exclusion of aquatic creatures lacking criteria: Both exclude water creatures lacking fins and scales; Leviticus develops the prohibition with abomination language and flesh-and-carcass instructions.

Deuteronomy 14:11
Every clean bird you may eat.
  Deuteronomy 14:20 — Same-chapter clean-flying-creature permissions framing exclusions: The clean-bird permission before the prohibited list corresponds to the clean-flying-creature permission after it, preserving the source's differing terms rather than claiming identical wording.

Deuteronomy 14:12
And these you shall not eat of them: the eagle, and the griffon vulture, and the sea-eagle,
  Leviticus 11:13 — Parallel prohibited-bird list opening with preserved names: Both open the forbidden-bird examples with eagle and sea-eagle; Deuteronomy's griffon-vulture and Leviticus' gryps forms remain distinct without a zoological identification claim.

Deuteronomy 14:13
and the vulture, and the kite, and those like it,
  Leviticus 11:14 — Parallel vulture-and-kite exclusion: Both prohibit the vulture and kite with the accompanying kind-language, giving a specific shared element of the two prohibited-bird lists.

Deuteronomy 14:14
and every raven, and those like it,
  Leviticus 11:15 — Parallel raven-kind exclusion: Both exclude the raven and its like, retaining a particular shared list entry rather than a general resemblance between food laws.

Deuteronomy 14:15
and the ostrich, and the owl, and the gull,
  Leviticus 11:16 — Parallel ostrich, owl and gull list entries: Both list ostrich, owl and gull among the forbidden creatures; Leviticus also places the hawk in this unit, preserving the differing grouping.

Deuteronomy 14:16
and the heron, and the swan, and the ibis,
  Leviticus 11:17-19 — Parallel prohibited names distributed across different units: Deuteronomy groups heron, swan and ibis together; Leviticus includes these names across several units, so the comparison does not impose identical list positions.

Deuteronomy 14:17
and the diving bird, and the hawk, and those like it, and the hoopoe, and the night-raven,
  Leviticus 11:16-19 — Parallel prohibited names with different list grouping: The diving bird, hawk, hoopoe and night-raven appear within Leviticus' longer grouping as well; the actual unit boundaries and order are preserved rather than aligned artificially.

Deuteronomy 14:18
and the pelican, and the plover, and those like it, and the purple swamphen, and the bat.
  Leviticus 11:18-19 — Parallel pelican, plover and bat entries with preserved names: Both passages include pelican, plover and bat; Deuteronomy's purple-swamphen and Leviticus' purple-waterbird wording remains distinct without claiming a modern species equivalence.

Deuteronomy 14:19
All creeping things among the flying creatures are unclean unto you; you shall not eat of them.
  Leviticus 11:20-23 — Related creeping-flying prohibition with stated exceptions in Leviticus: Both restrict creeping flying creatures; Leviticus additionally permits specified leaping kinds, a qualification retained rather than silently added to Deuteronomy's shorter prohibition.

Deuteronomy 14:20
Every clean flying creature you may eat.
  Deuteronomy 14:11 — Same-chapter clean-creature permissions around the prohibited list: The clean-flying-creature permission closes the list introduced after the clean-bird permission, preserving their particular terms without treating them as identical sentences.

Deuteronomy 14:21
You shall not eat anything that dies of itself; it shall be given unto the resident alien within your cities, and he shall eat it, or you may sell it unto a foreigner; for you are a holy people unto the Lord your God. You shall not boil a lamb in its mother’s milk.
  Leviticus 17:15-16 — Different dead-animal food instructions: Deuteronomy forbids Israel to eat an animal dead of itself and permits transfer to aliens or foreigners; Leviticus describes washing and impurity after native or alien consumption.
  Exodus 23:19 — Repeated prohibition concerning a lamb in its mother's milk: Both forbid boiling a lamb in its mother's milk; the adjacent instructions differ, with Exodus naming firstfruits and Deuteronomy discussing dead-animal food and holy identity.

Deuteronomy 14:22
You shall surely tithe all the produce of your seed, the yield of your field, year by year.
  Leviticus 27:30 — Related seed-and-produce tithe instructions: Deuteronomy specifies an annual tithe of field produce; Leviticus declares the land's seed and fruit tithe holy to the Lord without stating that annual timing in this verse.

Deuteronomy 14:23
And you shall eat it before the Lord your God, in the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there. You shall bring the tithes of your grain, and of your wine, and of your oil, and the firstborn of your cattle and of your sheep, that you may learn to fear the Lord your God all your days.
  Deuteronomy 12:17-18 — Chosen-place consumption of restricted gifts: Both direct consumption of specified tithes and firstborn gifts before the Lord at His chosen place; chapter12 explicitly contrasts prohibited city consumption and lists participants.

Deuteronomy 14:24
And if the way is too long for you, so that you cannot carry them, because the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there is far from you, when the Lord your God has blessed you,
  Deuteronomy 14:25-26 — Same-chapter distance condition and silver provision: When distance prevents carrying the gifts, the following instructions allow conversion to silver, transport to the chosen place and purchase for the household meal.

Deuteronomy 14:25
then you shall sell them for silver, and take the silver in your hands, and go unto the place which the Lord your God shall choose.
  Deuteronomy 14:24 — Same-chapter silver transport linked to inability to carry: The command to convert into silver and travel to the chosen place answers the preceding condition of distance and inability to carry what the Lord has blessed.

Deuteronomy 14:26
And you shall spend the silver for whatsoever your soul desires, for cattle, or for sheep, or for wine, or for strong drink, or for whatsoever your soul desires; and you shall eat there before the Lord your God, and rejoice, you and your house,
  Deuteronomy 14:24-25 — Same-chapter chosen-place spending after silver conversion: Spending on cattle, sheep, wine, strong drink and desired food implements the preceding distant-carriage provision, followed here by household eating and rejoicing before the Lord.

Deuteronomy 14:27
and the Levite within your cities; for he has neither portion nor inheritance with you.
  Deuteronomy 12:12 — Levite's lack of inheritance grounds inclusion: Both connect care or inclusion of the Levite with his lack of portion and inheritance alongside Israel; chapter12 places him within rejoicing at the chosen place.

Deuteronomy 14:28
After three years you shall bring forth all the tithe of your produce; in that year you shall lay it up within your cities.
  Deuteronomy 26:12-13 — Third-year local tithe distribution with distinct descriptions: Both describe third-year provision for the Levite, sojourner, orphan and widow; chapter14 says all the tithe, whereas chapter26 calls it the second tithe, without harmonizing the descriptions.

Deuteronomy 14:29
And the Levite, for he has neither portion nor inheritance with you, and the sojourner, and the orphan, and the widow within your cities shall come, and eat, and be filled; that the Lord your God may bless you in all the works which you do.
  Deuteronomy 26:12-13 — Named four groups eating the third-year provision: Both name Levite, sojourner, orphan and widow receiving and eating the provision; chapter26 also includes the giver's declaration that the holy things have been distributed.

Deuteronomy 15:1
At the end of seven years you shall make a release.
  Deuteronomy 31:10-11 — Seven-year release and later public-reading appointment: The seven-year release is named again in Moses' appointment of public law reading at the Feast of the Pitching of Tents in the release year.
  Nehemiah 10:32 — Seventh-year release and later communal undertaking: The instruction to make a release after seven years corresponds to the community's undertaking to release the seventh year and the demand of every hand.

Deuteronomy 15:2
And this is the ordinance of the release: you shall release every debt of yours which your neighbour owes you; and you shall not exact it of your brother, for a release unto the Lord your God has been proclaimed.
  Nehemiah 10:32 — Debt-release instruction and later release undertaking: The command to release a neighbour's debt and refrain from exacting it corresponds to Nehemiah's undertaking to release the demand of every hand in the seventh year.

Deuteronomy 15:3
Of a foreigner you may exact whatsoever he owes you; but you shall release your brother’s debt unto you.
  Deuteronomy 15:2 — Same-chapter debt-release scope and explicit distinction: The requirement to release a brother's debt repeats the preceding rule, while this verse distinguishes the foreigner from whom the debt may still be exacted.

Deuteronomy 15:4
For there shall be none in need among you; for the Lord your God shall surely bless you in the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession, to inherit it,
  Deuteronomy 15:5 — Same-chapter promised absence of need and its condition: The promised absence of need through the Lord's blessing in the inherited land is followed by the explicit condition of diligently hearing and keeping His commandments.

Deuteronomy 15:5
if you diligently hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God, to keep and do all these commandments which I command you this day.
  Deuteronomy 15:4 — Same-chapter command-keeping condition and preceding promise: Diligent obedience states the condition attached to the preceding assurance of blessing and absence of need, rather than presenting that assurance without its stated qualification.

Deuteronomy 15:6
For the Lord your God has blessed you, as He has spoken unto you; and you shall lend unto many nations, but you shall not borrow; and you shall rule over many nations, but they shall not rule over you.
  Deuteronomy 28:12-13 — Repeated lending and rule blessing: Both promise lending to many nations without borrowing and ruling without being ruled; chapter28 places the assurance within blessings explicitly conditioned on command keeping.

Deuteronomy 15:7
And if there is among you one of your brethren in need, within any of your cities in the land which the Lord your God gives you, you shall not withhold the affection of your heart, neither shall you close your hand against your needy brother.
  Leviticus 25:35-37 — Support rather than financial exploitation of a poor brother: Both address an impoverished brother and require supporting him; Deuteronomy prohibits closing the hand, while Leviticus specifically prohibits interest or increase on money and food.

Deuteronomy 15:8
You shall freely open your hands unto him, and shall surely lend him as much as he needs, according to his want.
  Deuteronomy 15:7 — Same-chapter open-hand response to the stated need: The command to open the hands and lend according to need answers the preceding prohibition on withholding affection and closing the hand against a needy brother.

Deuteronomy 15:9
Take heed unto yourself, lest there be a hidden, lawless thought in your heart, saying: The seventh year, the year of release, draws near; and your eye be evil toward your needy brother, and you give him nothing, and he cry against you unto the Lord, and there be great sin upon you.
  Deuteronomy 15:1-2 — Same-chapter release rule and warning against evading its effect: The approaching release year must not become a reason to withhold help; the opening rule explains the anticipated debt release behind that particular temptation.

Deuteronomy 15:10
You shall freely give unto him, and shall surely lend him as much as he needs; and your heart shall not be grieved when you give unto him. For because of this the Lord your God shall bless you in all your works, and in all that you put your hand unto.
  Deuteronomy 15:8 — Same-chapter repeated sufficient lending with willing giving: Both require open-handed provision and lending sufficient for the brother's need; verse10 additionally prohibits a grieving heart and promises blessing upon the giver's work.

Deuteronomy 15:11
For the needy shall never cease out of the land; therefore I command you to do this thing, saying: You shall freely open your hands unto your poor brother and unto the needy in your land.
  Mark 14:7 — Continuing presence of the poor and opportunity to help: The statement that the needy will not cease grounds an open-hand command; Jesus likewise says the poor remain with the hearers and they can do them good.
  Deuteronomy 15:4-5 — Same-chapter need statements with their distinct qualifications: The continuing-needy statement grounds the duty to help, while the earlier absence-of-need promise is joined to divine blessing and diligent obedience; neither statement is silently removed.

Deuteronomy 15:12
And if your brother, a Hebrew man or a Hebrew woman, is sold unto you, he shall serve you six years; and in the seventh year you shall send him forth free from you.
  Exodus 21:2 — Six-year Hebrew service and seventh-year freedom: Both require freedom in the seventh year after six years of Hebrew service; Deuteronomy expressly includes a Hebrew woman in this instruction.

Deuteronomy 15:13
And when you send him forth free from you, you shall not send him away empty.
  Deuteronomy 15:14 — Same-chapter prohibition of empty release and specified provision: The instruction not to release the servant empty is developed by generous provision from sheep, grain and winepress according to the owner's received blessing.

Deuteronomy 15:14
You shall furnish him generously out of your sheep, and out of your grain, and out of your winepress; as the Lord your God has blessed you, you shall give unto him.
  Deuteronomy 15:13 — Same-chapter departure gifts implement nonempty release: The specified sheep, grain and winepress provision implements the preceding prohibition against sending the freed servant away empty, with divine blessing setting the measure.

Deuteronomy 15:15
And you shall remember that you were a household slave in the land of Egypt, and the Lord your God redeemed you thence; therefore I command you to do this thing.
  Leviticus 25:42 — Egyptian redemption as reason for service obligations: Both ground obligations concerning Israelite servants in the Lord's redemption from Egypt; Leviticus describes them as His servants and restricts their sale, while Deuteronomy commands remembered bondage.

Deuteronomy 15:16
And if he says unto you: I will not go forth from you, because he loves you and your house, and because it is well with him beside you,
  Exodus 21:5 — Servant's stated love and refusal of release: Both describe a servant declaring love and refusing freedom; Deuteronomy names the master and household's wellbeing, while Exodus expressly names master, wife and children.

Deuteronomy 15:17
then you shall take an awl, and pierce his ear against the door, and he shall be your household slave for ever; and unto your female slave you shall do likewise.
  Exodus 21:6 — Awl, ear and doorway procedure for lasting service: Both prescribe piercing the ear with an awl at a doorway and lasting service; Exodus adds the tribunal and doorpost, while Deuteronomy explicitly extends the instruction to a female servant.

Deuteronomy 15:18
It shall not seem hard unto you when they are sent forth free from you, for he served you six years for the yearly wage of a hired worker; and the Lord your God shall bless you in all that you do.
  Exodus 21:2 — Six-year service and required release: The owner must not resent releasing someone who served six years, corresponding to Exodus' six-year service and seventh-year freedom; Deuteronomy's yearly-wage comparison remains its own wording.

Deuteronomy 15:19
Every firstborn male that is born among your cattle and among your sheep you shall sanctify unto the Lord your God. You shall do no work with your firstborn calf, neither shall you shear the firstborn of your sheep.
  Exodus 34:19 — Firstborn male livestock belonging to the Lord: Both identify firstborn male cattle and sheep as belonging or sanctified to the Lord; Deuteronomy additionally prohibits work with the firstborn calf and shearing the firstborn sheep.

Deuteronomy 15:20
You shall eat it before the Lord, year by year, in the place which the Lord your God shall choose, you and your house.
  Deuteronomy 12:17-18 — Firstborn meal at the chosen place: The annual household eating before the Lord at His chosen place corresponds to the earlier restriction on firstborn consumption in cities and direction to eat it before Him.

Deuteronomy 15:21
And if there is a blemish in it, lameness or blindness or any grievous blemish, you shall not sacrifice it unto the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 22:20-22 — Blemished animals excluded from sacrifice: Both reject blemished animals as offerings and explicitly include blindness; Deuteronomy additionally names lameness, while Leviticus supplies its own longer list and offering context.

Deuteronomy 15:22
You shall eat it within your cities; the unclean among you and the clean shall eat it alike, as the gazelle or the deer.
  Deuteronomy 12:22 — Repeated local clean-and-unclean meat permission: Both permit clean and unclean persons alike to eat locally as gazelle and deer are eaten; the current context concerns the blemished firstborn rather than ordinary meat generally.

Deuteronomy 15:23
Only you shall not eat the blood; you shall pour it upon the earth as water.
  Deuteronomy 12:16 — Repeated blood prohibition and earth-water disposal formula: Both prohibit eating blood and require pouring it onto the earth like water, retaining the same particular restriction alongside their respective local meat permissions.

Deuteronomy 16:1
Keep the month of new produce, and observe the Passover unto the Lord your God; for in the month of new produce you came forth out of Egypt by night.
  Exodus 34:18 — Departure month and Unleavened Bread remembrance: Both connect the prescribed observance with the month of new produce or new grain and departure from Egypt, preserving their respective month descriptions and festival emphasis.
  Exodus 12:29-31 — Nighttime Egyptian deliverance context: The Passover remembrance expressly recalls departure by night; Exodus narrates the midnight firstborn judgment and Pharaoh summoning Moses and Aaron at night to order Israel's departure.

Deuteronomy 16:2
And you shall sacrifice the Passover unto the Lord your God, sheep and cattle, in the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there.
  Exodus 12:3-6 — Passover sacrifice with differing livestock and setting details: Both command the Passover sacrifice; Deuteronomy names sheep and cattle at the chosen place, while Exodus instructs Israelite households in Egypt concerning a lamb or young goat and its evening slaughter.

Deuteronomy 16:3
You shall eat no leaven with it; you shall eat unleavened bread with it for seven days, the bread of affliction, for you came forth out of Egypt in haste; that you may remember the day of your departure out of the land of Egypt all the days of your life.
  Exodus 12:39 — Unleavened bread and hasty departure: Deuteronomy calls the bread affliction and recalls departure in haste; Exodus explains the unleavened cakes by the Egyptians driving Israel out before the dough could leaven.
  Exodus 12:15-17 — Seven-day unleavened observance as departure memorial: Both prescribe seven days of unleavened bread in connection with the departure from Egypt, with Exodus also setting first- and seventh-day assemblies and work restrictions.

Deuteronomy 16:4
No leaven shall be seen with you in all your borders seven days; neither shall any of the flesh which you sacrifice in the evening of the first day remain overnight until the morning.
  Exodus 12:10 — Passover flesh not retained until morning: Both forbid leaving the sacrificed flesh until morning; Exodus additionally commands burning what remains, while Deuteronomy specifies the evening sacrifice of the first day.
  Exodus 12:19 — Seven-day absence of leaven: Both require leaven to be absent for seven days; Deuteronomy describes all the borders, while Exodus specifies houses and the consequence for eating leavened food.

Deuteronomy 16:5
You may not sacrifice the Passover in any of your cities which the Lord your God gives you,
  Deuteronomy 16:6 — Same-chapter prohibited city sacrifice and chosen destination: The prohibition on sacrificing Passover in any of the cities is immediately answered by the command to sacrifice at the Lord's chosen place at evening.

Deuteronomy 16:6
but in the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there, you shall sacrifice the Passover in the evening, at the setting of the sun, at the time when you came forth out of Egypt.
  Exodus 12:6 — Passover slaughter at evening: Both assign Passover slaughter to evening; Deuteronomy further specifies sunset, the departure remembrance and the chosen place, whereas Exodus supplies the fourteenth-day household arrangement.

Deuteronomy 16:7
And you shall boil it, and roast it, and eat it in the place which the Lord your God shall choose; and in the morning you shall turn and go unto your houses.
  Exodus 12:8-9 — Passover preparation instructions with preserved difference: Deuteronomy says to boil and roast and eat at the chosen place; Exodus prescribes roasting with fire and explicitly prohibits boiling in water, without harmonizing those instructions.

Deuteronomy 16:8
You shall eat unleavened bread for six days; and on the seventh day shall be a concluding feast, a feast unto the Lord your God. You shall do no work therein, save that which is done for a person’s needs.
  Exodus 12:15-16 — Unleavened observance and concluding work restriction: Deuteronomy specifies six days and a seventh-day concluding feast; Exodus states seven days and first- and seventh-day holiness, with both allowing work for a person's necessary needs.

Deuteronomy 16:9
You shall count seven complete weeks unto yourself; when you begin to put the sickle to the harvest, you shall begin to count seven weeks.
  Leviticus 23:15-16 — Seven-week counting with distinct starting descriptions: Both require seven complete weeks; Deuteronomy begins with the sickle's first harvest, while Leviticus counts from the day after the Sabbath and sheaf presentation, then names fifty days.

Deuteronomy 16:10
And you shall keep the Feast of Weeks unto the Lord your God, according to the ability of your hand, of that which He gives you, as the Lord your God has blessed you.
  Exodus 34:22 — Feast of Weeks and harvest observance: Both command keeping the Feast of Weeks; Exodus explicitly names the beginning of wheat harvest, while Deuteronomy measures the observance by ability and received blessing.

Deuteronomy 16:11
And you shall rejoice before the Lord your God, you, and your son, and your daughter, and your male slave, and your female slave, and the Levite within your cities, and the sojourner, and the orphan, and the widow among you, in the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there.
  Deuteronomy 16:14 — Same-chapter repeated inclusive festival rejoicing: The Weeks rejoicing and later Tents rejoicing both name the household, slaves, Levite, sojourner, orphan and widow, keeping the two feasts distinct despite the shared participant list.

Deuteronomy 16:12
And you shall remember that you were a household slave in the land of Egypt; and you shall keep and do these commandments.
  Deuteronomy 15:15 — Egyptian household bondage remembered in communal obligations: Both command remembrance of household slavery in Egypt as motivation for the surrounding obligations; chapter15 concerns servant release, while this instruction follows inclusive festival rejoicing.

Deuteronomy 16:13
You shall keep the Feast of Tents seven days, when you have gathered in from your threshing floor and from your winepress.
  Leviticus 23:39-43 — Seven-day ingathering feast with distinct additional details: Both connect a seven-day celebration with gathered produce; Leviticus additionally names the seventh-month date, branches, tent dwelling and the explanatory memory of wilderness tents.

Deuteronomy 16:14
And you shall rejoice in your feast, you, and your son, and your daughter, and your male slave, and your female slave, and the Levite, and the sojourner, and the orphan, and the widow within your cities.
  Deuteronomy 16:11 — Same-chapter shared participant list across distinct feasts: The Tents rejoicing repeats the Weeks list of household, slaves, Levite, sojourner, orphan and widow, preserving the particular inclusive pattern without merging the festivals.

Deuteronomy 16:15
You shall keep a feast for seven days unto the Lord your God, in the place which the Lord your God shall choose for Himself; and if the Lord your God blesses you in all your produce and in all the work of your hands, you shall rejoice.
  Leviticus 23:39-41 — Seven-day harvest rejoicing before the Lord: Both specify seven days of harvest celebration and rejoicing before the Lord; Deuteronomy names His chosen place and blessing, whereas Leviticus specifies the calendar and branches.

Deuteronomy 16:16
Three times in the year shall every male among you appear before the Lord your God, in the place which the Lord shall choose: in the Feast of Unleavened Bread, and in the Feast of Weeks, and in the Feast of the Pitching of Tents. You shall not appear before the Lord your God empty,
  Exodus 23:14-17 — Three annual appearances and named agricultural feasts: Both prescribe three annual appearances for males and name unleavened and harvest-related feasts; Deuteronomy explicitly names Weeks and Tents and the chosen place, preserving differing festival labels.

Deuteronomy 16:17
but every man according to the ability of your hands, according to the blessing of the Lord your God which He has given you.
  Deuteronomy 16:16 — Same-chapter nonempty appearance and proportionate gift: Giving according to ability and divine blessing specifies how the preceding prohibition on appearing empty before the Lord is to be fulfilled.

Deuteronomy 16:18
You shall appoint judges and recording officers for yourself in all your cities which the Lord your God gives you, according to your tribes; and they shall judge the people with righteous judgment.
  Deuteronomy 1:13-17 — Appointment and righteous work of tribal judges: The command to appoint judges and recording officers by tribes corresponds to Moses' earlier appointment of graded leaders and recording officers, with instructions for just hearings and difficult cases.

Deuteronomy 16:19
They shall not pervert judgment, neither shall they show partiality, nor take a bribe; for bribes blind the eyes of the wise, and overthrow the words of the righteous.
  Exodus 23:6-8 — Judgment and gifts that corrupt judicial sight and words: Both prohibit perverting judgment and receiving corrupting gifts, explaining that such gifts blind the eyes and distort righteous words; Deuteronomy additionally names partiality.

Deuteronomy 16:20
You shall pursue justly that which is just, that you may live, and enter and inherit the land which the Lord your God gives you.
  Leviticus 19:15 — Pursuit of justice and concrete impartial judgment: The command to pursue what is just corresponds to Leviticus' requirement of righteous neighbour judgment without favouring poor or powerful, while Deuteronomy adds life and land inheritance.

Deuteronomy 16:21
You shall not plant for yourself a sacred grove, any tree, beside the altar of the Lord your God which you shall make for yourself.
  Deuteronomy 7:5 — Sacred grove forbidden beside the Lord's altar: The ban on planting a sacred grove beside the Lord's altar corresponds to the instruction to cut down the nations' sacred groves rather than adopt those installations.

Deuteronomy 16:22
You shall not set up for yourself a pillar, which the Lord your God has hated.
  Leviticus 26:1 — Parallel prohibition on erecting a pillar: Both prohibit setting up a pillar; Leviticus includes it among forbidden images and worship stones, while Deuteronomy states that the Lord has hated it.

Deuteronomy 17:1
You shall not sacrifice unto the Lord your God a calf or a sheep wherein is a blemish, any evil defect; for it is an abomination unto the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 22:20-22 — Blemished sacrificial animals prohibited: Both reject blemished animals as offerings to the Lord; Leviticus supplies particular defects and offering circumstances, while Deuteronomy calls the defective calf or sheep an abomination.

Deuteronomy 17:2
And if there is found among you, within any of your cities which the Lord your God gives you, a man or a woman who does evil before the Lord your God, transgressing His covenant,
  Deuteronomy 17:3-5 — Same-chapter covenant offense and specified inquiry: The case of a man or woman doing covenant evil is developed through other-god worship, diligent investigation and the prescribed judgment after the report is verified.

Deuteronomy 17:3
and they go and serve other gods, and bow down unto them, unto the sun, or unto the moon, or unto any of the array of heaven, which He has not commanded,
  Deuteronomy 4:19 — Heavenly bodies prohibited as objects of worship: Both explicitly prohibit being drawn into serving and bowing to the sun, moon and heavenly array, connecting the earlier warning with this defined covenant offense.

Deuteronomy 17:4
and it is told you, you shall inquire diligently; and behold, if the matter is true, and this abomination has been done in Israel,
  Deuteronomy 13:15 — Diligent verification before idolatry judgment: Both require diligent inquiry and confirmation that an alleged abomination is true; chapter13 concerns a city's enticement, whereas this chapter addresses an individual man or woman.

Deuteronomy 17:5
then you shall bring forth that man or that woman, and you shall stone them with stones, and they shall die.
  Deuteronomy 13:11 — Stoning in distinct other-god offenses: Both prescribe stoning in an other-god offense; chapter13 concerns the private enticer, while this case follows verified worship by the named man or woman.

Deuteronomy 17:6
Upon the testimony of two witnesses or three witnesses shall he who is condemned to death be put to death; he shall not die upon the testimony of one witness.
  Numbers 35:30 — A single witness insufficient for death judgment: Both exclude execution on one witness alone; Deuteronomy explicitly names two or three witnesses, while Numbers applies its witness requirement to the killer's judgment.
  Deuteronomy 19:15 — Two-or-three-witness rule in capital and broader cases: Both require two or three witnesses instead of one; chapter17 states the death-judgment case, whereas chapter19 applies the formula to every matter of wrongdoing.

Deuteronomy 17:7
And the hand of the witnesses shall be first upon him to put him to death, and afterward the hand of all the people; and you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.
  Deuteronomy 13:10 — First hands followed by all the people: Both place particular hands first in execution and afterward those of all the people; chapter17 specifies witnesses, while chapter13 addresses the person who received the enticement.

Deuteronomy 17:8
And if a matter in judgment is too hard for you, between blood and blood, and between judgment and judgment, and between injury and injury, and between dispute and dispute, matters of judgment within your cities, you shall arise and go up unto the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there.
  Exodus 18:22 — Referral of difficult judgments with distinct authorities: Both refer difficult matters beyond ordinary judgment; Exodus sends weighty matters to Moses, while Deuteronomy directs the inquirer to the Lord's chosen place and its authorities.

Deuteronomy 17:9
And you shall come unto the Levitical priests, and unto the judge who shall be in those days; and they shall inquire, and declare unto you the judgment.
  Deuteronomy 19:17 — Priests and judges in a disputed case: Both bring a judicial dispute before priests and the judge or judges then serving, with chapter19 specifically addressing the two men in a witness dispute.

Deuteronomy 17:10
And you shall do according to the matter which they declare unto you from the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there; and you shall take diligent heed to do according to all that is laid down for you as law.
  Deuteronomy 17:8-9 — Same-chapter referral followed by compliance: The requirement to carry out the declared judgment follows referral of the difficult case to the chosen place and inquiry by its priests and judge.

Deuteronomy 17:11
You shall do according to the law and according to the judgment which they tell you; you shall not turn aside from the word which they declare unto you, to the right hand or to the left.
  Deuteronomy 17:9-10 — Same-chapter declared judgment and prohibition of deviation: The prohibition on turning right or left specifies compliance with the judgment and law delivered by the previously identified priests and judge at the chosen place.

Deuteronomy 17:12
And the man who acts presumptuously, and will not hearken unto the priest who stands to minister in the name of the Lord your God, or unto the judge who shall be in those days, that man shall die; and you shall remove the evil one from Israel.
  Deuteronomy 17:10-11 — Same-chapter presumptuous refusal contrasted with required obedience: The death judgment concerns refusal to heed the priest or judge, contrasted with the preceding requirement to carry out their declared law without deviation.

Deuteronomy 17:13
And all the people shall hear and fear, and shall act ungodly no more.
  Deuteronomy 13:12 — Hearing and fear as a stated deterrent: Both conclude a judgment with the people hearing and fearing so that the wrongdoing is not repeated, while preserving their different offenses and procedures.

Deuteronomy 17:14
And if you enter the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession, and inherit it, and dwell therein, and say: I will set a ruler over me, as do the other nations round about me,
  1 Samuel 8:4-7 — Anticipated ruler request and later request like the nations: Deuteronomy anticipates requesting a ruler like surrounding nations; the men of Israel later request a king like the nations, and the narrative additionally records Samuel's and the Lord's responses.

Deuteronomy 17:15
you shall surely set over yourself a ruler whom the Lord your God shall choose. You shall set a ruler over yourself from among your brethren; you may not set a foreign man over yourself, for he is not your brother.
  1 Samuel 10:20-24 — Chosen Israelite ruler and Saul's public selection: The law requires a ruler chosen by the Lord from Israel's brothers; Saul is selected from Benjamin and publicly described by Samuel as the one the Lord chose.

Deuteronomy 17:16
For he shall not multiply horses unto himself, neither shall he cause the people to return unto Egypt, that he may multiply horses unto himself; for the Lord has said: You shall not return by this way again.
  1 Kings 10:28-29 — Egyptian horse acquisition contrasted with royal restriction: The ruler must not multiply horses and must not return the people to Egypt to acquire them; Kings records Solomon's Egyptian horse and chariot trade, without explicitly narrating a return of the people.

Deuteronomy 17:17
And he shall not multiply wives unto himself, neither shall his heart turn aside; and he shall not greatly multiply silver and gold unto himself.
  1 Kings 11:4 — Warning about wives and the later turning of Solomon's heart: The ruler must not multiply wives or let his heart turn aside; Kings explicitly says Solomon's foreign wives turned his heart after their gods.
  1 Kings 10:27 — Royal accumulation of silver and gold compared with the restriction: The ruler is forbidden greatly multiplying silver and gold; Kings describes Solomon making gold and silver in Jerusalem as common as stones, giving a particular contrast in royal wealth.

Deuteronomy 17:18
And it shall be, when he sits in his rule, that he shall write for himself this second law in a scroll, from the Levitical priests.
  Deuteronomy 31:9 — Written law and Levitical priestly custody: The ruler must write the second law from the Levitical priests; the later account records Moses writing the law and giving it to Levi's priests and the elders.

Deuteronomy 17:19
And it shall be with him, and he shall read therein all the days of his life, that he may learn to fear the Lord his God, to keep all these commandments and these ordinances, to do them,
  Joshua 1:8 — Continual engagement with the law for obedient leadership: The ruler must keep and read the law throughout life to learn fear and obedience; Joshua is commanded to meditate on the law day and night to do what is written.

Deuteronomy 17:20
that his heart may not be lifted up above his brethren, and that he may not turn aside from the commandments, to the right hand or to the left; that he may prolong his days in his rule, he and his sons, among the sons of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 17:18-19 — Same-chapter written-law discipline and its stated purposes: The purposes of humility among brothers, fidelity and prolonged rule follow the requirement to write, retain and read the law, without importing a different royal succession rule.

Deuteronomy 18:1
The Levitical priests, the whole tribe of Levi, shall have neither portion nor inheritance with Israel. The offerings of the Lord are their inheritance; they shall eat them.
  Numbers 18:23-24 — Levites' lack of territorial inheritance and assigned provision: Both deny Levites an inheritance among Israel; Numbers assigns them tithes for their service, while Deuteronomy describes eating the Lord's offerings as their inheritance.

Deuteronomy 18:2
And they shall have no inheritance among their brethren; the Lord Himself is his inheritance, as He spoke unto him.
  Numbers 18:20 — The Lord as inheritance with distinct addressees: Both identify the Lord Himself as portion or inheritance instead of a share among Israel; Numbers addresses Aaron, whereas Deuteronomy's preceding heading concerns the Levitical priests and tribe.

Deuteronomy 18:3
And this shall be the priests’ due from the people, from them that sacrifice a sacrifice, whether a calf or a sheep: they shall give unto the priest the foreleg, and the cheeks, and the stomach.
  Leviticus 7:31-34 — Priestly meat portions with differing specified cuts: Both assign portions of sacrificed livestock to priests; Deuteronomy names foreleg, cheeks and stomach, while Leviticus' deliverance-offering instruction names breast and right foreleg.

Deuteronomy 18:4
And you shall give unto him the firstfruits of your grain, and of your wine, and of your oil, and the first of the fleece of your sheep.
  Numbers 18:12-13 — Firstfruits of grain, wine and oil for priestly provision: Both give the firstfruits of grain, wine and oil to the priestly recipient; Deuteronomy additionally names the first fleece, which is not added to Numbers' list.

Deuteronomy 18:5
For the Lord your God has chosen him out of all your tribes, to stand before the Lord your God, to minister and to bless in His name, he and his sons among the sons of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 10:8 — Chosen priest's ministry and Levi's assigned roles: The chosen priest and his sons receive standing, ministering and blessing roles here; chapter10 assigns those roles to the tribe of Levi and additionally names bearing the ark, preserving the different scopes.

Deuteronomy 18:6
And if a Levite comes from any of your cities, from among all the sons of Israel where he sojourns, according to the desire of his soul, unto the place which the Lord shall choose,
  Deuteronomy 18:7-8 — Same-chapter incoming Levite and his service and portion: The Levite's move from a city to the chosen place is followed by permission to minister with his brothers and eat an allotted portion, retaining the ancestral-sale qualification.

Deuteronomy 18:7
then he shall minister in the name of the Lord his God, as do all his brethren the Levites who stand there before the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 18:5 — Same-chapter incoming Levite shares the stated ministry: The Levite arriving at the chosen place ministers in the Lord's name like the standing Levites, corresponding to the preceding description of the chosen ministry.

Deuteronomy 18:8
He shall eat an allotted portion, apart from the sale according to his ancestral family.
  Deuteronomy 18:6-7 — Same-chapter allotted food for the incoming minister: The allotted eating portion follows the case of a Levite coming to the chosen place and ministering there; the separate ancestral-sale wording is preserved without an inferred property arrangement.

Deuteronomy 18:9
And when you enter the land which the Lord your God gives you, you shall not learn to do after the abominations of those nations.
  Deuteronomy 12:30-31 — Prohibition on learning the nations' religious practices: Both warn against adopting the nations' cult practices after entering their land; chapter12 specifies inquiry into their worship and burning sons and daughters, while chapter18 develops its own list.

Deuteronomy 18:10
There shall not be found among you one who purifies his son or his daughter by fire, or practises divination, or interprets spoken omens, or observes auguries, or works sorcery,
  Leviticus 19:26 — Parallel prohibitions on divination and observed omens: Both prohibit divination through omens and observed signs; Leviticus specifically mentions birds, while Deuteronomy includes spoken omens, auguries and sorcery within its longer list.

Deuteronomy 18:11
or chants spells, or is a medium, or observes portents, or inquires of the dead.
  Leviticus 19:31 — Mediums and enchanters excluded from Israel's practice: Both prohibit recourse to mediums and related occult practitioners; Deuteronomy additionally names spells, portents and inquiry of the dead, while Leviticus states the resulting defilement.

Deuteronomy 18:12
For everyone who does these things is an abomination unto the Lord your God; for because of these abominations the Lord shall destroy them from before you.
  Leviticus 18:24-28 — Abominations linked with removal of prior inhabitants: Both connect the nations' abominations with their removal and warn Israel against participation; the particular lists of prohibited deeds differ and are not merged.

Deuteronomy 18:13
You shall be perfect before the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 18:14 — Same-chapter perfect allegiance contrasted with divination: The command to be perfect before the Lord is followed by the contrast between nations hearkening to omens and divinations and the Lord's withholding that permission from Israel.

Deuteronomy 18:14
For these nations whose possession you inherit hearken unto omens and divinations; but the Lord your God has not granted you so to do.
  Deuteronomy 18:10-11 — Same-chapter contrasted national practice and explicit prohibitions: The refusal of permission to follow omens and divinations recalls the specific preceding exclusions of divination, augury, sorcery, spells, mediums and inquiry of the dead.

Deuteronomy 18:15
The Lord your God shall raise up unto you a prophet from among your brethren, like unto me; you shall hearken unto him,
  Acts 3:22 — Moses' prophet promise explicitly quoted in apostolic speech: Peter's speech quotes Moses' promise of a prophet like him from among the brothers and the instruction to listen, placing the citation within its proclamation concerning Jesus.
  Acts 7:37 — Moses' prophet promise explicitly quoted in Stephen's speech: Stephen identifies Moses as the speaker of the promise that God would raise a prophet like him from among the brothers, retaining the later speech's stated attribution.

Deuteronomy 18:16
according to all that you asked of the Lord your God in Horeb, in the day of the assembly, saying: We will no more hear the voice of the Lord our God, neither will we see this great fire any more, lest we die.
  Deuteronomy 5:25-27 — Horeb request not to hear the divine voice directly: Both recount the hearers' fear of death from the great fire and divine voice, with chapter5 explicitly asking Moses to hear and relay the Lord's words.

Deuteronomy 18:17
And the Lord said unto me: All that they have spoken is right.
  Deuteronomy 5:28 — Repeated divine approval of the people's words: Both report the Lord's response that all the people's words were right, connecting this brief approval with the fuller account of the Horeb request.

Deuteronomy 18:18
I will raise up for them a prophet from among their brethren, like unto you, and will put My word in his mouth; and he shall speak unto them all that I command him.
  Acts 3:22 — Promised prophet like Moses in later explicit citation: The promise to raise a prophet like Moses from the brothers corresponds to the later citation attributed to Moses; Deuteronomy additionally states that God puts His word in the prophet's mouth.

Deuteronomy 18:19
And the man who will not hearken unto whatsoever the prophet shall speak in My name, I will exact retribution from him.
  Acts 3:23 — Liability for refusing the promised prophet with differing formulations: Deuteronomy says God will exact retribution for refusing His prophet's words; Acts' associated citation describes destruction from the people, preserving the different judgment formulations.

Deuteronomy 18:20
But the prophet who shall act ungodly, speaking in My name a word which I commanded him not to speak, or who shall speak in the name of other gods, that prophet shall die.
  Deuteronomy 13:6 — Death judgment in distinct false-prophet instructions: Both prescribe death concerning a prophet directing allegiance away from the Lord; chapter18 also names unauthorized speech in His name, while chapter13 includes the dreamer and Egyptian redemption rationale.

Deuteronomy 18:21
And if you say in your heart: How shall we know the word which the Lord has not spoken?
  Deuteronomy 18:22 — Same-chapter question about unauthorized speech and stated test: The question of recognizing a word the Lord has not spoken is immediately answered by the criterion of a prediction not occurring or being fulfilled.

Deuteronomy 18:22
Whatsoever the prophet speaks in the name of the Lord, if the word does not come to pass, nor is fulfilled, that is the word which the Lord has not spoken. That prophet has spoken in ungodliness; you shall not spare him.
  Deuteronomy 13:3-4 — Contrasting prophetic tests without equating their criteria: Chapter18 identifies nonfulfillment as a sign of unauthorized speech; chapter13 requires rejecting other-god enticement even when the sign occurs, so occurrence alone is not presented as sufficient.

Deuteronomy 19:1
And when the Lord your God destroys the nations whose land your God gives you, and you inherit their possession, and dwell in their cities and in their houses,
  Deuteronomy 12:29 — Repeated inherited-land occupation setting: Both introduce instructions by describing the Lord removing nations and Israel inheriting their land and dwelling there; chapter19 additionally explicitly names their cities and houses.

Deuteronomy 19:2
you shall set apart three cities for yourself in the midst of your land which the Lord your God gives you.
  Numbers 35:13-14 — Three refuge cities alongside the six-city distribution: Deuteronomy commands setting apart three cities in the given land; Numbers specifies six refuges distributed as three across Jordan and three in Canaan, preserving its fuller arrangement.

Deuteronomy 19:3
Mark out the way for yourself, and divide into three parts the territory of your land which the Lord your God apportions unto you; and there shall be a refuge there for every slayer.
  Numbers 35:11-12 — Refuge provision for the slayer: Both require appointed places to which a slayer may flee; Deuteronomy adds marked routes and three territorial divisions, while Numbers explicitly protects against death before congregational judgment.

Deuteronomy 19:4
And this shall be the ordinance for the slayer who shall flee thither and live: whosoever strikes his neighbour unintentionally, having borne him no hatred aforetime,
  Numbers 35:22-25 — Unintentional killing distinguished from prior enmity: Both distinguish killing without enmity from hostile killing and provide protective refuge; Numbers adds congregational adjudication and residence until the high priest's death.

Deuteronomy 19:5
as when a man goes with his neighbour into the forest to gather wood, and his hand is jolted with the axe as he cuts the wood, and the iron slips from the wood and strikes his neighbour, so that he dies, he shall flee unto one of these cities and live;
  Numbers 35:22-23 — Different examples of a fatal accident without hostility: Deuteronomy illustrates unintended death with an axe-head slipping during woodcutting; Numbers illustrates a sudden nonhostile push, an object without ambush or an unknowing fatal stone fall.

Deuteronomy 19:6
lest the kinsman who avenges blood pursue the slayer while his heart is hot, and overtake him, if the way is long, and strike him mortally, so that he dies, though no sentence of death is due unto him, for he bore his neighbour no hatred aforetime.
  Numbers 35:12 — Refuge protects from the blood-avenging kinsman: Both provide protection from the avenging kinsman; Deuteronomy emphasizes the danger of a long journey and absence of a death sentence, while Numbers requires standing for congregational judgment.

Deuteronomy 19:7
Therefore I command you this thing, saying: You shall set apart three cities for yourself.
