  Deuteronomy 19:2 — Same-chapter repeated three-city command: The renewed command to set apart three cities repeats the initial city appointment after the explanation of unintentional killing and the danger of pursuit.

Deuteronomy 19:8
And if the Lord your God enlarges your borders, as He sware unto your fathers, and the Lord gives you all the land which He promised to give unto your fathers,
  Deuteronomy 19:9 — Same-chapter enlarged-land condition and additional refuges: The conditional grant of enlarged borders is followed by the obedience condition and command to add three more cities, preserving the conditional structure rather than asserting completion.

Deuteronomy 19:9
if you hearken to do all these commandments which I command you this day, to love the Lord your God, and to walk in all His ways all your days, then you shall add three cities more unto these three,
  Deuteronomy 19:2 — Same-chapter initial three refuges and conditional addition: The instruction to add three more explicitly builds on the initial three-city appointment, with love, obedience and the preceding enlarged-land condition qualifying the addition.

Deuteronomy 19:10
that innocent blood may not be shed in your land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession, and that there may be none guilty of blood among you.
  Numbers 35:33-34 — Protection of the land from blood guilt: Deuteronomy gives prevention of innocent bloodshed and blood guilt as the purpose of refuge provision; Numbers describes land stained by murder and its stated expiation, preserving the different formulations.

Deuteronomy 19:11
But if a man hates his neighbour, and lies in wait for him, and rises up against him, and strikes him mortally, so that he dies, and flees unto one of these cities,
  Numbers 35:20-21 — Hostility and ambush distinguished from accidental killing: Both identify enmity or ambush in a fatal attack as grounds for a murder judgment, preserving the contrast with the preceding nonhostile accident cases.

Deuteronomy 19:12
then the elders of his city shall send and take him thence, and deliver him into the hands of the kinsman who avenges blood, and he shall die.
  Numbers 35:21 — Avenging kinsman and the hostile killer's death: Both assign death of the hostile killer to the avenging kinsman; Deuteronomy adds the elders' duty to retrieve him from the refuge and deliver him.

Deuteronomy 19:13
Your eye shall not spare him; and you shall cleanse the guilt of innocent blood from Israel, and it shall be well with you.
  Numbers 35:31-33 — Death judgment and removal of murder's blood guilt: Deuteronomy prohibits sparing the intentional killer and requires cleansing innocent blood guilt; Numbers rejects ransom for the guilty killer and connects the shedder's blood with expiating murder in the land.

Deuteronomy 19:14
You shall not remove your neighbour’s boundaries, which your fathers set in your inheritance, which was allotted unto you in the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession.
  Deuteronomy 27:17 — Boundary-removal prohibition and covenant curse: The law forbids moving a neighbour's inherited boundaries; the later curse expressly condemns removing the neighbour's boundaries and requires the people's assent.

Deuteronomy 19:15
One witness shall not suffice to testify against a man concerning any injustice, or any trespass, or any sin which he sins. At the mouth of two witnesses, and at the mouth of three witnesses, shall every matter be established.
  Numbers 35:30 — One witness insufficient in broader and capital matters: Deuteronomy requires two or three witnesses to establish every matter; Numbers excludes a single witness's testimony as sufficient to cause death in a killing case.
  Matthew 18:16 — Two-or-three-witness formula reused in communal correction: Matthew uses the same formula about every matter established by two or three witnesses within instructions on correcting a brother; that church context is distinguished from Deuteronomy's judicial setting.

Deuteronomy 19:16
And if an unrighteous witness rises up against a man, accusing him of ungodliness,
  Exodus 23:1 — Unrighteous witness opposed by a prior prohibition: The case of an unrighteous accuser corresponds to Exodus' prohibition on agreeing with the unrighteous as an unrighteous witness; Deuteronomy develops the investigation and consequences.

Deuteronomy 19:17
then the two men between whom is the dispute shall stand before the Lord, and before the priests, and before the judges who shall be in those days.
  Deuteronomy 17:9 — Priests and judges hear the disputed matter: The two disputants must appear before priests and judges, corresponding to the earlier direction to approach the Levitical priests and judge for a difficult judgment.

Deuteronomy 19:18
And the judges shall inquire diligently; and behold, if the unrighteous witness has testified falsely, and has stood against his brother,
  Deuteronomy 17:4 — Diligent inquiry into a reported offense: Both require judges or inquirers to establish an allegation diligently; chapter19 concerns false testimony, whereas chapter17 concerns a reported act of other-god worship.

Deuteronomy 19:19
then you shall do unto him as he wickedly purposed to do unto his brother; and you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.
  Deuteronomy 19:16-18 — Same-chapter false-witness case and the resulting penalty: Doing to the false witness what he planned against his brother follows the defined accusation, appearance before authorities and diligent determination that he testified falsely.

Deuteronomy 19:20
And those who remain shall hear and fear, and shall no more do such an evil thing among you.
  Deuteronomy 17:13 — Repeated hearing-and-fear deterrent formula: Both state that those hearing the judgment will fear and cease the wrongdoing, while the underlying cases remain false testimony and refusal of judicial authority respectively.

Deuteronomy 19:21
Your eye shall not spare him: life for life, eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand, foot for foot.
  Exodus 21:23-25 — Matching retaliatory formula in different cases: Both state life for life, eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand and foot for foot; Exodus places the formula in the pregnant-woman injury case and adds further injuries.
  Leviticus 24:19-20 — Corresponding injury-for-injury language: Both give eye-for-eye and tooth-for-tooth measures; Leviticus applies its statement to inflicted bodily injury and adds fracture, while Deuteronomy concludes the false-witness instruction.

Deuteronomy 20:1
And when you go forth unto war against your enemies, and see horse and rider and a people more numerous than you, you shall not fear them; for the Lord your God is with you, who brought you up out of the land of Egypt.
  Deuteronomy 7:17-19 — Numerical inferiority answered by remembering Egyptian deliverance: Both counter fear of more numerous enemies by recalling the Lord's deeds in Egypt, connecting the battle assurance with the earlier command to remember witnessed signs and deliverance.

Deuteronomy 20:2
And it shall be, when you draw near unto battle, that the priest shall draw near and speak unto the people,
  Deuteronomy 20:3-4 — Same-chapter priestly speech assignment and its contents: The priest's approach to speak before battle is developed by the specified words against fear and the assurance that the Lord goes with Israel to fight and save.

Deuteronomy 20:3
and shall say unto them: Hear, O Israel; you draw near this day unto battle against your enemies. Let not your heart faint; fear not, neither be broken, nor turn aside from before them.
  Deuteronomy 1:29-30 — Battle courage grounded in the Lord's fighting: Both tell Israel not to fear the opponents; the following assurances identify the Lord as the one who fights for them, preserving the different discourse settings.

Deuteronomy 20:4
For the Lord your God goes before you and with you, to fight with you against your enemies, to save you.
  Exodus 14:13-14 — Assurance of divine fighting and deliverance: The battle speech promises that the Lord goes with Israel to fight and save; the sea-deliverance speech likewise announces salvation and that the Lord will fight for the people.

Deuteronomy 20:5
And the scribes shall speak unto the people, saying: What man has built a new house, and has not dedicated it? Let him go and return unto his house, lest he die in the battle, and another man dedicate it.
  Deuteronomy 28:30 — New house lost to another in exemption and curse settings: The exemption guards against dying before dedicating a new house and another dedicating it; the later curse describes building a house without dwelling in it, preserving the different outcomes and contexts.

Deuteronomy 20:6
And what man has planted a vineyard, and has not enjoyed its fruit? Let him go and return unto his house, lest he die in the battle, and another man enjoy its fruit.
  Deuteronomy 28:30 — Vineyard enjoyment threatened in exemption and curse settings: The exemption concerns a newly planted vineyard whose fruit the planter has not enjoyed; the curse likewise names planting a vineyard without gathering its fruit, in a different legal setting.

Deuteronomy 20:7
And what man has betrothed a wife, and has not taken her? Let him go and return unto his house, lest he die in the battle, and another man take her.
  Deuteronomy 24:5 — Marriage-related military exemptions at different stages: This exemption concerns a betrothed man who has not taken his wife; chapter24 exempts the newly married man for one year, preserving the different stages and stated terms.

Deuteronomy 20:8
And the scribes shall speak further unto the people, and say: What man is fearful and faint of heart? Let him go and return unto his house, lest he make the heart of his brother faint, as his own.
  Judges 7:3 — Fearful men permitted to leave the force: Both allow the fearful to turn back; Deuteronomy explains the risk of discouraging brothers, while Judges records Gideon's force receiving the call and many departing.

Deuteronomy 20:9
And it shall be, when the scribes have finished speaking unto the people, that they shall appoint captains of the host to lead the people.
  Deuteronomy 20:5-8 — Same-chapter completed announcements precede appointment of captains: The appointment of captains follows completion of the scribes' particular house, vineyard, betrothal and fear announcements, keeping those preceding exemptions and the later leadership appointment distinct.

Deuteronomy 20:10
And when you draw near unto a city to fight against it, you shall call its people forth with an offer of peace.
  Deuteronomy 2:26 — Peace message before the narrated conflict: The city-war instruction begins with a peace offer; Moses' earlier account likewise records sending Sihon words of peace, while its following request specifically concerns passage and provisions.

Deuteronomy 20:11
And if they answer you peaceably, and open unto you, then all the people found therein shall pay you tribute, and be subject unto you.
  Deuteronomy 20:10 — Same-chapter peace offer and accepting city's status: The condition of a peaceable answer and opening the city responds to the preceding peace offer, with tribute and subjection stated as the resulting terms.

Deuteronomy 20:12
But if they will not hearken unto you, and make war against you, you shall besiege it.
  Deuteronomy 20:10-11 — Same-chapter refusal contrasted with peaceable acceptance: The instruction to besiege follows refusal and war, contrasted with the preceding offer of peace and the tribute arrangement when a city answers peaceably.

Deuteronomy 20:13
And the Lord your God shall deliver it into your hands, and you shall slay every male therein with the sword,
  Deuteronomy 20:15 — Same-chapter stated scope of the city-war judgment: The killing of the city's males belongs to the instruction later expressly assigned to very distant cities, distinguished there from the cities of the named nations.

Deuteronomy 20:14
save the women and the dependent households. And you shall take as spoil unto yourself all the cattle, and all that is in the city, and all its goods; and you shall eat all the spoil of your enemies which the Lord your God gives you.
  Deuteronomy 20:13 — Same-chapter retained persons and spoil contrasted with male judgment: The provision for women, dependent households, cattle and goods follows the command concerning the city's males, specifying the contrasting treatment within this city-war case.

Deuteronomy 20:15
Thus you shall do unto all the cities which are very far from you, which are not of the cities of these nations.
  Deuteronomy 20:16 — Same-chapter distant and inherited cities expressly distinguished: The stated application to very distant cities is followed by a different rule for the cities whose land Israel inherits, rather than applying one undifferentiated policy to both.

Deuteronomy 20:16
But behold, of the cities of these nations, whose land the Lord your God gives you to inherit, you shall save alive nothing that breathes.
  Deuteronomy 7:1-2 — Devotion of the inherited nations: Both prescribe utter destruction concerning the nations removed before Israel in the inherited land; the surrounding city-war instruction separately distinguishes distant cities.

Deuteronomy 20:17
But you shall utterly devote them to destruction, the Hittites, and the Amorites, and the Canaanites, and the Perizzites, and the Hivites, and the Jebusites, and the Girgashites, as the Lord your God commanded you,
  Deuteronomy 7:1-2 — Matching seven nations within the destruction instruction: Both name Hittites, Amorites, Canaanites, Perizzites, Hivites, Jebusites and Girgashites within the instruction about the inherited nations, preserving their different list orders.

Deuteronomy 20:18
lest they teach you to do all their abominations which they did unto their gods, and you sin before the Lord your God.
  Exodus 23:32-33 — Other-god practices as the stated danger of continued association: Both warn that the nations' religious practices could cause Israel to sin; Exodus connects this with covenants and dwelling, while Deuteronomy explains the preceding destruction instruction.

Deuteronomy 20:19
And if you besiege a city many days, to fight against it and take it, you shall not destroy its trees by laying iron against them; for you may eat of them, and shall not cut them down. Is the tree of the field a man, that it should enter the siege before you?
  Deuteronomy 20:20 — Same-chapter food trees distinguished from siege timber: The prohibition on cutting food-bearing trees during siege is contrasted with permission to cut a tree known not to bear food for constructing siegeworks.

Deuteronomy 20:20
But the tree which you know bears no fruit for food, you may destroy and cut down, and build siegeworks against the city which makes war with you, until it is delivered up.
  Deuteronomy 20:19 — Same-chapter permitted nonfood timber and protected food trees: The permission to use a nonfood-bearing tree for siegeworks qualifies the preceding protection of trees from which the besiegers may eat; the explicit distinction is preserved.

Deuteronomy 21:1
And if one is found slain in the land which the Lord your God gives you to inherit, lying in the field, and it is not known who smote him,
  Deuteronomy 21:2-4 — Same-chapter unknown-killer case and specified response: The discovery of a slain person whose killer is unknown triggers measuring nearby cities and the nearest city's heifer procedure, without inventing an identified perpetrator.

Deuteronomy 21:2
your elders and your judges shall go forth, and measure unto the cities round about the slain man.
  Deuteronomy 21:3 — Same-chapter measured proximity determines the responsible elders: The elders' and judges' measurement toward surrounding cities is followed by the nearest city's elders taking the specified heifer, connecting measurement with the assigned local responsibility.

Deuteronomy 21:3
And it shall be, that the elders of the city nearest unto the slain man shall take a heifer of the herd, which has not been worked, and which has not drawn in the yoke.
  Numbers 19:2 — Unyoked heifers in different ritual instructions: Both specify a heifer on which no yoke has been laid; Numbers additionally requires a red unblemished animal for purification, whereas Deuteronomy also prohibits prior work in its unresolved-killing case.

Deuteronomy 21:4
And the elders of that city shall bring down the heifer unto a rough ravine, which has not been tilled nor sown, and shall cut the sinews of the heifer in the ravine.
  Deuteronomy 21:6-8 — Same-chapter ravine procedure followed by declaration and petition: The ravine and cutting of the heifer's sinews are followed by handwashing over that heifer and the elders' denial and mercy petition, retaining the source's particular action.

Deuteronomy 21:5
And the Levitical priests shall draw near, for the Lord your God has chosen them to stand before Him, and to bless in His name; and by their mouth shall every dispute and every injury be decided.
  Deuteronomy 18:5 — Chosen priestly standing and blessing ministry: Both ground the priests' standing before the Lord and blessing in His name in divine choice; chapter21 additionally names deciding disputes and injuries.
  Deuteronomy 17:9 — Priestly role in deciding disputed matters: The assignment of disputes and injuries to priestly decision corresponds to approaching the Levitical priests and judge for a difficult case, without conflating their complete procedural settings.

Deuteronomy 21:6
And all the elders of that city nearest unto the slain man shall wash their hands over the head of the heifer whose sinews were cut in the ravine,
  Matthew 27:24 — Handwashing accompanying an asserted innocence of blood: The elders' handwashing precedes their innocence declaration; Pilate likewise washes his hands and declares innocence of blood before the crowd, without identifying the two cases or endorsing his claim.

Deuteronomy 21:7
and shall answer and say: Our hands have not shed this blood, neither have our eyes seen it.
  Deuteronomy 21:6 — Same-chapter spoken innocence declaration accompanies handwashing: The elders' denial that their hands shed blood or eyes saw it supplies the words accompanying the preceding handwashing over the heifer whose sinews were cut.

Deuteronomy 21:8
Be merciful unto Thy people Israel, whom Thou hast redeemed, O Lord, out of the land of Egypt, that there be no guilt of innocent blood among Thy people Israel. And atonement shall be made for the blood on their behalf.
  Numbers 35:33 — Blood-guilt expiation in different killing cases: The petition seeks mercy and atonement where the killer is unknown; Numbers states expiation through the murderer's blood, preserving the differing cases and prescribed means.

Deuteronomy 21:9
And you shall remove the guilt of innocent blood from among yourselves, if you do that which is good and pleasing before the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 19:13 — Removal of innocent blood guilt in different cases: Both require removing innocent blood guilt from Israel; chapter19 concerns the known intentional killer, whereas chapter21 concludes the unknown-killer heifer procedure.

Deuteronomy 21:10
And when you go forth unto war against your enemies, and the Lord your God delivers them into your hands, and you take their spoil,
  Deuteronomy 20:14 — War spoil and the following captive-woman case: The opening of the captive-woman instruction describes enemy defeat and spoil, corresponding to the earlier city-war provision retaining women and dependent households among the spoils.

Deuteronomy 21:11
and see among the spoil a woman fair of countenance, and set your heart upon her, and would take her unto yourself for a wife,
  Deuteronomy 21:12-14 — Same-chapter desired captive marriage and required conditions: The wish to marry a beautiful captive is followed by the prescribed household preparation, mourning interval and marriage, together with restrictions if she is later rejected.

Deuteronomy 21:12
then you shall bring her into your house, and shave her head, and pare her nails.
  Deuteronomy 21:13 — Same-chapter household preparation and mourning before marriage: Bringing the captive into the house, shaving her head and paring her nails precede removal of captivity clothing and a full month of mourning before the stated marriage.

Deuteronomy 21:13
And you shall take from her the garments of her captivity; and she shall dwell in your house, and mourn her father and her mother a full month. And afterward you shall go in unto her, and dwell with her as husband, and she shall be your wife.
  Deuteronomy 21:11-12 — Same-chapter marriage after the preceding desire and preparation: The permitted marriage follows the prior desire to take the captive as wife and household preparation, with this verse adding the full mourning month before that union.

Deuteronomy 21:14
And it shall be, if you do not desire her, that you shall send her forth free; and she shall not be sold for silver. You shall not treat her with contempt, because you have humbled her.
  Exodus 21:8 — Restrictions on selling a rejected woman in distinct cases: Deuteronomy requires freeing the rejected captive wife without sale; Exodus restricts selling a pledged female servant to a foreign nation, preserving the different situations and remedies.

Deuteronomy 21:15
And if a man has two wives, one loved and one hated, and both the loved and the hated bear him children, and the firstborn son is hers that is hated,
  Genesis 29:30-32 — Loved and hated wives with a son born to the hated wife: The legal case distinguishes loved and hated wives and the hated wife's firstborn son; Genesis describes Jacob loving Rachel more, Leah being hated and Leah bearing Reuben.

Deuteronomy 21:16
then it shall be, in the day when he gives his sons an inheritance of his possessions, that he may not make the son of the loved wife the firstborn, overlooking the firstborn son of the hated wife.
  Deuteronomy 21:17 — Same-chapter prohibited displacement and the firstborn's entitlement: The prohibition on preferring the loved wife's son is answered by acknowledging the hated wife's firstborn and giving his double portion, grounded in his birth priority.

Deuteronomy 21:17
But he shall acknowledge the firstborn, the son of the hated wife, by giving him a double portion of all that he has; for he is the beginning of his children, and the right of the firstborn belongs unto him.
  Genesis 49:3 — Firstborn described as beginning of the children: Both describe the firstborn as the beginning of the father's children; Deuteronomy additionally prescribes his double portion and forbids displacing the hated wife's firstborn through preference for the loved wife's son.

Deuteronomy 21:18
And if a man has a disobedient and rebellious son, who does not hearken unto the voice of his father or the voice of his mother, and they chasten him, but he does not hearken unto them,
  Exodus 20:12 — Rebellious parental disobedience contrasted with the honor command: The case defines a son persistently refusing both parents despite chastening, contrasting with the command to honor father and mother; the passages have different stated consequences.

Deuteronomy 21:19
then his father and his mother shall lay hold on him, and bring him forth unto the elders of his city, and unto the gate of his place.
  Deuteronomy 21:18 — Same-chapter persistent parental disobedience triggers public referral: The parents bring the son to the city elders and gate after the stated persistent refusal to obey either parent despite chastening, preserving the specified condition before referral.

Deuteronomy 21:20
And they shall say unto the men of their city: This our son is disobedient and rebellious; he does not hearken unto our voice; he carouses and is drunken.
  Deuteronomy 21:18 — Same-chapter parents' public accusation states the preceding case: The parents' accusation repeats the son's disobedience and refusal to hear, adding carousing and drunkenness to the case earlier described as persistent rebellion despite chastening.

Deuteronomy 21:21
And the men of his city shall stone him with stones, and he shall die; and you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves, and those who remain shall hear and fear.
  Deuteronomy 13:11-12 — Stoning and deterrent hearing in distinct offenses: Both prescribe communal stoning and state that others hear and fear; chapter13 concerns enticement to other gods, whereas chapter21 concerns the parents' defined rebellious-son accusation.

Deuteronomy 21:22
And if there is in a man a sin deserving judgment of death, and he dies, and you hang him upon a tree,
  Joshua 10:26-27 — Death followed by hanging and later removal: The law describes death judgment followed by hanging upon a tree; Joshua's narrative explicitly has the five kings killed and then hanged until evening before removal.

Deuteronomy 21:23
his body shall not remain upon the tree overnight, but you shall surely bury him that day; for everyone who hangs upon a tree is cursed by God. And you shall not defile the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession.
  Joshua 10:26-27 — Hanged bodies removed before overnight retention: The law forbids overnight retention and requires same-day burial; Joshua orders removal at sunset and placement in the cave, preserving the particular dispositions described in each passage.
  Galatians 3:13 — Tree-curse clause quoted and applied to Christ: Paul explicitly quotes the curse upon everyone hanging on a tree and applies it to Christ's becoming a curse, distinguishing his theological application from the law's body-disposal instruction.

Deuteronomy 22:1
You shall not see your brother’s calf or his sheep wandering in the way, and disregard them; you shall surely bring them back unto your brother, and restore them unto him.
  Exodus 23:4 — Parallel restoration of straying livestock with different owners: Both require returning wandering livestock to its owner; Deuteronomy speaks of a brother's calf or sheep, while Exodus specifically requires returning an enemy's ox or burden animal.

Deuteronomy 22:2
And if your brother is not near you, and you do not know him, you shall gather them into your house, and they shall be with you until your brother seeks them; and you shall restore them unto him.
  Deuteronomy 22:1 — Same-chapter custody provision completing livestock restoration: Keeping the animal until its absent or unknown owner seeks it supplies the practical continuation of the preceding command to return a brother's straying livestock.

Deuteronomy 22:3
So you shall do with his donkey, and so you shall do with his garment, and so you shall do with every lost thing of your brother, whatsoever he has lost and you have found; you may not disregard it.
  Leviticus 5:22-24 — Found property must be restored, with different case settings: Both require restoration of a found lost thing; Leviticus additionally addresses false dealing and an unjust oath, requiring a fifth added to the principal.

Deuteronomy 22:4
You shall not see your brother’s donkey or his calf fallen in the way, and disregard them; you shall surely raise them up with him.
  Exodus 23:5 — Parallel assistance with a fallen animal: Both require helping raise a fallen animal rather than passing by; Deuteronomy names a brother's donkey or calf, whereas Exodus names an enemy's burden animal under its load.

Deuteronomy 22:5
A woman shall not have a man’s equipment upon her, neither shall a man put on a woman’s robe; for everyone who does these things is an abomination unto the Lord your God.
  1 Corinthians 11:4-6 — Different instructions distinguishing male and female attire: Deuteronomy prohibits exchanging the specified male equipment and female robe; Paul gives different head-covering instructions for men and women praying or prophesying, without stating the same clothing rule.

Deuteronomy 22:6
And if you come upon a bird’s nest before you in the way, in any tree or upon the ground, with young birds or eggs, and the mother sitting upon the young or upon the eggs, you shall not take the mother with the young.
  Deuteronomy 22:7 — Same-chapter bird-nest prohibition and permitted taking: The prohibition on taking a mother with her young is developed by the requirement to release the mother while allowing the young to be taken.

Deuteronomy 22:7
You shall surely let the mother go, but the young you may take unto yourself, that it may be well with you, and that your days may be long.
  Leviticus 22:28 — Related restrictions on taking mother and young together: Deuteronomy releases the nesting mother while allowing the young to be taken; Leviticus forbids slaughtering a cow or sheep with her young on one day, preserving the different animals and actions.

Deuteronomy 22:8
And when you build a new house, you shall make a parapet for your roof, that you do not cause bloodshed in your house, if anyone falls from it.
  Deuteronomy 19:10 — Different preventive provisions against bloodguilt: The roof parapet prevents a fatal fall from bringing bloodshed upon a house; refuge-city provision likewise explicitly aims to prevent innocent bloodshed and bloodguilt in the land.

Deuteronomy 22:9
You shall not sow your vineyard with a different crop, lest the produce and the seed which you sow, together with the produce of your vineyard, become consecrated.
  Leviticus 19:19 — Parallel vineyard mixed-sowing prohibition: Both forbid sowing a vineyard with another kind of crop; Deuteronomy adds the consequence concerning consecration of the vineyard produce and sown seed.

Deuteronomy 22:10
You shall not plough with a calf and a donkey together.
  Leviticus 19:19 — Different restrictions on unlike animal pairing: Deuteronomy forbids ploughing with a calf and donkey together; Leviticus prohibits mating livestock of different stock, presenting distinct restrictions rather than the same agricultural act.

Deuteronomy 22:11
You shall not wear a mingled fabric, wool and linen together.
  Leviticus 19:19 — Parallel mixed-material garment prohibitions: Both forbid wearing a garment woven from mixed materials; Deuteronomy specifies wool and linen, while Leviticus describes a garment of two materials without naming them here.

Deuteronomy 22:12
You shall make twisted tassels upon the four edges of your outer garments wherewith you cover yourself.
  Numbers 15:38-40 — Garment-corner tassels and their stated reminder purpose: Both require garment-corner fringes or tassels; Numbers additionally specifies a blue thread and explains their role in remembering and performing the Lord's commandments.

Deuteronomy 22:13
And if a man takes a wife, and dwells with her as husband, and hates her,
  Deuteronomy 22:14-19 — Same-chapter marital hatred initiating the accusation case: The husband's hatred introduces the following accusation, presentation of evidence before elders and penalties if he has falsely given his wife an evil name.

Deuteronomy 22:14
and brings charges against her as a pretext, and gives her an evil name, and says: I took this woman, and when I came unto her, I did not find the tokens of her virginity,
  Deuteronomy 22:15-17 — Same-chapter accusation and parents' evidentiary response: The charge of absent virginity tokens is answered by the parents bringing the tokens and spreading the garment before the city's elders.

Deuteronomy 22:15
then the father of the young woman and her mother shall take and bring forth the tokens of her virginity unto the elders at the gate.
  Deuteronomy 22:17 — Same-chapter evidence brought and displayed before elders: The parents' bringing the virginity tokens to the elders at the gate is developed by the father's statement and the spreading of the garment before those elders.

Deuteronomy 22:16
And the father of the young woman shall say unto the elders: I gave this my daughter unto this man for a wife, and he has hated her;
  Deuteronomy 22:13-14 — Same-chapter father's account restating the husband's conduct: The father recalls giving his daughter as wife and the husband's hatred, repeating the marriage, hatred and ensuing accusation that introduced this case.

Deuteronomy 22:17
and behold, he brings charges against her as a pretext, saying: I did not find the tokens of your daughter’s virginity. Yet these are the tokens of my daughter’s virginity. And they shall spread the garment before the elders of the city.
  Deuteronomy 22:18-19 — Same-chapter evidence followed by judgment upon the accuser: The displayed garment and denial of the pretextual charge precede chastening, the hundred-shekel fine and the restriction against the husband later divorcing her.

Deuteronomy 22:18
And the elders of that city shall take that man and chasten him.
  Deuteronomy 22:19 — Same-chapter chastening and specified additional consequences: The elders' chastening of the man is followed by his fine payable to the woman's father and the requirement that she remain his wife without later dismissal.

Deuteronomy 22:19
And they shall fine him a hundred shekels, and give them unto the father of the young woman, because he has brought an evil name upon a virgin of Israel; and she shall be his wife, and he may not put her away all his days.
  Deuteronomy 19:18-19 — Different penalties for a proven false accusation: Both place consequences upon a false accuser after judicial inquiry or evidence; Deuteronomy22 specifies a fine and marital restriction, whereas chapter19 requires the intended harm to be returned.

Deuteronomy 22:20
But if this matter is true, and the tokens of the young woman’s virginity are not found,
  Deuteronomy 22:21 — Same-chapter alternative finding and its stated judgment: The alternative that the charge is true and the tokens are absent introduces the following judgment upon the young woman rather than the penalties for a false accuser.

Deuteronomy 22:21
then they shall bring forth the young woman unto the doors of her father’s house, and the men of her city shall stone her with stones, and she shall die; for she has wrought folly among the sons of Israel, bringing harlotry into her father’s house. And you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.
  Deuteronomy 22:20 — Same-chapter judgment linked to the alternative finding: The stated execution and removal of evil follow the preceding case condition that the charge is true and the young woman's virginity tokens are not found.

Deuteronomy 22:22
And if a man is found lying with a woman married unto a husband, you shall put both of them to death, the man who lay with the woman, and the woman; and you shall remove the evil one from Israel.
  Leviticus 20:10 — Parallel death judgment upon both adultery participants: Both prescribe death for the man and the woman when intercourse involves another man's wife, with Deuteronomy also stating the removal-of-evil formula.

Deuteronomy 22:23
And if a young woman, a virgin, is betrothed unto a man, and a man finds her in the city and lies with her,
  Deuteronomy 22:24 — Same-chapter city encounter and subsequent stated judgment: The case of a betrothed virgin found in the city introduces the judgment that follows, with its separately stated reasons concerning her lack of outcry and his violation.

Deuteronomy 22:24
you shall bring both of them forth unto the gate of their city, and they shall be stoned with stones and shall die: the young woman, because she did not cry out in the city, and the man, because he violated his neighbour’s wife. And you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.
  Deuteronomy 22:25-27 — Same-chapter contrasting city and field cases: The city's judgment upon both is contrasted with the forced field encounter, where only the man dies and the woman is expressly free of a death-deserving sin.

Deuteronomy 22:25
But if a man finds the betrothed young woman in the field, and forces her and lies with her, you shall put to death only the man who lay with her.
  Deuteronomy 22:26-27 — Same-chapter forced field encounter and protected woman: The command to put only the assailant to death is explained by the woman's innocence of a capital offense and her cry without anyone there to help.

Deuteronomy 22:26
And unto the young woman you shall do nothing; there is in her no sin deserving death. For as when a man rises against his neighbour and slays him, so is this matter.
  Deuteronomy 22:27 — Same-chapter innocence explained through absence of help: The woman's freedom from a death-deserving sin is followed by the explanation that she cried in the field and had no helper, preserving the text's explicit protective reasoning.

Deuteronomy 22:27
For he found her in the field; the betrothed young woman cried out, and there was none to help her.
  Deuteronomy 22:25-26 — Same-chapter field cry explains the distinct judgment: The outcry without help explains the field case's judgment upon the man alone and the express declaration that nothing is to be done to the woman.

Deuteronomy 22:28
And if a man finds a young woman, a virgin who is not betrothed, and forces her and lies with her, and he is found out,
  Exodus 22:15-16 — Different cases involving an unbetrothed virgin and marriage payment: Deuteronomy describes force and discovery; Exodus describes deception and additionally allows paternal refusal, so the unbetrothed status and payment setting do not make the two cases identical.

Deuteronomy 22:29
the man who lay with her shall give unto the father of the young woman fifty silver didrachmas, and she shall be his wife, because he violated her; he may not put her away all his days.
  Deuteronomy 22:28 — Same-chapter payment and marital restriction following the stated offense: The fifty-silver-didrachma payment and prohibition on later dismissal are attached to the preceding forced encounter with an unbetrothed virgin and its discovery.

Deuteronomy 23:1
A man shall not take his father’s wife or uncover his father’s covering.
  Leviticus 18:8 — Parallel prohibition concerning the father's wife: Both prohibit taking or uncovering the father's wife and describe the violation through the father's covering or nakedness, preserving this edition's placement at Deuteronomy23:1.

Deuteronomy 23:2
He whose testicles are crushed, or whose organ is cut off, shall not enter the assembly of the Lord.
  Isaiah 56:3-5 — Assembly exclusion and a distinct later promise to eunuchs: Deuteronomy excludes men with the specified genital injuries from the assembly; Isaiah promises faithful eunuchs an enduring name in God's house, without equating every physical category or resolving the different settings.

Deuteronomy 23:3
One born of a prostitute shall not enter the assembly of the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 23:4 — Same-chapter distinct classes excluded from the assembly: The exclusion of one born of a prostitute is followed by the Ammonite and Moabite exclusion with its own generational language, preserving the distinct categories and grounds.

Deuteronomy 23:4
An Ammonite or a Moabite shall not enter the assembly of the Lord; even unto the tenth generation he shall not enter the assembly of the Lord, even for ever,
  Nehemiah 13:1-2 — Explicit later reading of the assembly exclusion: Nehemiah reports reading Moses' book and finding the exclusion of Ammanites and Moabites from God's assembly, together with the bread-and-water and Balaam reasons.

Deuteronomy 23:5
because they did not meet you with bread and water in the way, when you came forth out of Egypt, and because they hired against you Balaam son of Beor, from Mesopotamia, to curse you.
  Nehemiah 13:2 — Repeated bread-and-water refusal and hiring of Balaam: Both give failure to meet Israel with bread and water and the hiring of Balaam to curse as reasons for the exclusion; Deuteronomy additionally names his patronymic and origin.

Deuteronomy 23:6
And the Lord your God would not hearken unto Balaam; and the Lord your God turned the curses into a blessing, because the Lord your God loved you.
  Numbers 23:11-12 — Intended curse becoming blessing in Balaam's account: Balak complains that the curse he sought became a blessing, and Balaam invokes God's given words; Deuteronomy summarizes the reversal as the Lord's loving action for Israel.
  Nehemiah 13:2 — Repeated curse-to-blessing statement: Both expressly state that God turned Balaam's intended curse into blessing; Deuteronomy also gives the Lord's love for Israel as the reason.

Deuteronomy 23:7
You shall not speak peace unto them, nor seek their advantage, all your days for ever.
  Deuteronomy 23:4-6 — Same-chapter prohibition tied to the named hostile groups: The direction not to seek their peace or advantage follows the Ammonite-Moabite exclusion and its stated refusal-of-provisions and Balaam episodes, identifying the command's immediate referents.

Deuteronomy 23:8
You shall not abhor an Edomite, for he is your brother; you shall not abhor an Egyptian, for you were a resident alien in his land.
  Deuteronomy 2:4-5 — Edomite brotherhood grounds a different treatment: The prohibition on abhorring an Edomite because he is a brother corresponds to the earlier identification of Esau's sons as Israel's brethren with land Israel must not seize.
  Exodus 22:20 — Egyptian alien residence recalled in treatment commands: Both recall Israel's residence as aliens in Egypt; Exodus uses it to forbid mistreating a resident foreigner, while Deuteronomy specifically forbids abhorring an Egyptian.

Deuteronomy 23:9
The sons born unto them shall enter the assembly of the Lord in the third generation.
  Deuteronomy 23:8 — Same-chapter third-generation admission and its preceding groups: The third-generation admission statement follows the separate instructions concerning Edomites and Egyptians, preserving its local referents rather than applying it to the preceding Ammonite-Moabite prohibition.

Deuteronomy 23:10
And when you go forth to encamp against your enemies, you shall keep yourself from every evil thing.
  Deuteronomy 23:15 — Same-chapter campaign-camp vigilance and holiness rationale: The command to guard against evil when encamping against enemies is grounded in the later statement that the Lord walks in the camp to deliver and give victory.

Deuteronomy 23:11
If there is among you a man who is not clean by reason of a discharge in the night, he shall go forth outside the camp, and shall not enter the camp.
  Numbers 5:2-3 — Camp exclusion associated with discharge and uncleanness: Both place a person with the specified discharge outside the camp; Numbers includes other unclean conditions and grounds exclusion in the Lord's dwelling among the camps.

Deuteronomy 23:12
And it shall be, toward evening, that he shall wash his body with water; and when the sun is set, he shall enter the camp.
  Leviticus 15:16 — Washing and evening limit after discharge: Both specify washing the body with water and an evening limit after the stated discharge; Deuteronomy additionally describes reentry into the camp after sunset.

Deuteronomy 23:13
And you shall have a place outside the camp, whither you shall go forth.
  Deuteronomy 23:14 — Same-chapter outside place and its use: The designated place outside the camp is followed by the instruction to dig with the belt peg and cover excrement there, specifying the activity assigned to that place.

Deuteronomy 23:14
And you shall have a peg upon your belt; and when you squat outside, you shall dig therewith, and turn and cover your excrement with it.
  Deuteronomy 23:15 — Same-chapter waste-covering rule and camp holiness: The command to dig and cover excrement outside the camp is followed by the stated holiness rationale concerning the Lord walking there and turning away from indecency.

Deuteronomy 23:15
For the Lord your God walks in the midst of your camp, to deliver you, and to give your enemy before you; therefore your camp shall be holy, that no indecent thing be seen among you, and He turn away from you.
  Numbers 5:3 — Camp purity grounded in divine presence: Both connect protection against camp defilement with the Lord's presence among Israel; Deuteronomy speaks of His walking and delivering, while Numbers speaks of His dwelling in the camps.

Deuteronomy 23:16
You shall not deliver unto his master a slave who has come unto you from his master.
  Deuteronomy 23:17 — Same-chapter fugitive-slave nonreturn and protected residence: The prohibition on returning the escaped slave to his master is developed by permission to dwell in a pleasing place among the hearers without being afflicted.

Deuteronomy 23:17
He shall dwell with you; he shall dwell among you in any place that pleases him. You shall not afflict him.
  Deuteronomy 23:16 — Same-chapter protected residence for the nonreturned fugitive: The permission to settle and prohibition on affliction apply to the slave just described as coming from his master and not to be returned to him.

Deuteronomy 23:18
There shall be no prostitute among the daughters of Israel, neither shall there be one who practises prostitution among the sons of Israel. There shall be no woman who performs initiation rites among the daughters of Israel, neither shall there be an initiated man among the sons of Israel.
  Leviticus 19:29 — Related prostitution prohibitions with different scope: Leviticus forbids making one's daughter a prostitute; Deuteronomy forbids prostitution among Israel's daughters and sons and additionally names female and male initiation-rite roles.

Deuteronomy 23:19
You shall not bring the hire of a prostitute, nor the price of a dog, into the house of the Lord your God for any vow; for both are an abomination unto the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 23:22-24 — Same-chapter forbidden vow proceeds and obligation to fulfill vows: The prostitute's hire and dog's price are prohibited as vow payments, while the following instructions require fulfillment of freely spoken vows; fulfillment does not authorize the specifically forbidden proceeds.

Deuteronomy 23:20
You shall not charge your brother interest on silver, nor interest on food, nor interest on anything which you lend.
  Leviticus 25:35-37 — Parallel prohibition of interest upon a brother's silver or food: Both prohibit interest or increase upon a brother's silver and food; Leviticus introduces the case of a brother who has become poor and requires supporting him.

Deuteronomy 23:21
Unto a foreigner you may lend at interest, but unto your brother you shall not lend at interest; that the Lord your God may bless you in all your works upon the land whither you go to inherit it.
  Exodus 22:24 — Brother-loan interest prohibition with an additional foreigner distinction: Both prohibit charging the brother interest; Exodus specifies the poor brother and avoiding pressure, while Deuteronomy separately permits interest to a foreigner and names a blessing.

Deuteronomy 23:22
And if you vow a vow unto the Lord your God, you shall not delay to pay it; for the Lord your God shall surely require it of you, and there shall be sin upon you.
  Numbers 30:3 — Vow obligation and spoken commitment: Both require carrying out a vow made to the Lord; Deuteronomy specifically forbids delay and names guilt, whereas Numbers emphasizes not profaning the spoken word.

Deuteronomy 23:23
But if you do not desire to vow, there is no sin upon you.
  Deuteronomy 23:22 — Same-chapter voluntary nonvowing contrasted with obligation after vowing: The absence of sin in not desiring to vow contrasts with the preceding obligation not to delay payment of a vow already made, whose nonfulfillment brings guilt.

Deuteronomy 23:24
That which proceeds through your lips you shall keep and do, as you have vowed a gift unto the Lord your God, which you have spoken with your mouth.
  Numbers 30:3 — Repeated obligation to perform what the mouth has spoken: Both require keeping the vow that comes from one's mouth, with Deuteronomy describing the pledged gift and Numbers also mentioning oaths and self-imposed restrictions.

Deuteronomy 23:25
And if you enter your neighbour’s harvest, you may gather heads of grain with your hands; but you shall not put a sickle unto your neighbour’s harvest.
  Deuteronomy 23:26 — Same-chapter bounded use of a neighbour's produce: Both permit limited use of the neighbour's produce: grain may be hand-gathered without a sickle, and grapes eaten without filling a vessel, preserving the different stated acts.
  Matthew 12:1-2 — Hand-plucked grain in a later Sabbath dispute: Matthew describes hungry disciples plucking and eating grain, corresponding to the hand-gathering permission; its stated objection concerns Sabbath observance rather than Deuteronomy's sickle restriction.

Deuteronomy 23:26
And if you enter your neighbour’s vineyard, you may eat grapes until your soul is filled; but you shall put none in a vessel.
  Deuteronomy 23:25 — Same-chapter counterpart limits on taking another's produce: The vineyard permission allows satisfied eating but no vessel collection, corresponding to hand-gathered grain without a sickle in the preceding harvest instruction.

Deuteronomy 24:1
And if a man takes a wife and dwells with her as husband, and she finds no favour in his sight, because he has found some unseemly thing in her, and he writes her a certificate of divorce, and gives it into her hands, and sends her forth out of his house,
  Matthew 19:7-9 — Divorce-certificate provision discussed in a later controversy: The questioners invoke Moses' divorce certificate, and Jesus distinguishes permission because of hardness of heart; Deuteronomy itself begins a conditional case that culminates in the remarriage restriction.
  Mark 10:3-5 — Divorce certificate recalled with speakers distinguished: The Pharisees describe Moses permitting a written dismissal, and Jesus responds concerning hardness of heart; the Deuteronomy case includes that written certificate before its further conditions.

Deuteronomy 24:2
and she departs and becomes another man’s wife,
  Deuteronomy 24:3-4 — Same-chapter second marriage and eventual former-husband restriction: The woman's becoming another man's wife introduces the second husband's dismissal or death and the rule forbidding the former husband to take her again.

Deuteronomy 24:3
and the latter husband hates her, and writes her a certificate of divorce, and gives it into her hands, and sends her forth out of his house, or if the latter husband who took her to be his wife dies,
  Deuteronomy 24:4 — Same-chapter alternative endings of second marriage and ensuing prohibition: Whether the latter husband dismisses her with a certificate or dies, the following rule bars the former husband from taking her again after that second marriage.

Deuteronomy 24:4
her former husband, who sent her away, may not take her again to be his wife, after she has been defiled; for it is an abomination before the Lord your God. And you shall not defile the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession.
  Deuteronomy 24:1-3 — Same-chapter remarriage prohibition tied to its complete case conditions: The bar on the former husband's taking her again depends upon the preceding dismissal, second marriage and its termination, rather than being a standalone command to initiate divorce.

Deuteronomy 24:5
And if a man has newly taken a wife, he shall not go forth unto war, neither shall any duty be laid upon him. He shall be free in his house one year, and shall gladden his wife whom he has taken.
  Deuteronomy 20:7 — Marriage-related exemption from military service with distinct timing: Both exempt a man from going into battle for a marital reason; chapter20 concerns a betrothed man who has not taken his wife, while chapter24 grants a newlywed a year at home.

Deuteronomy 24:6
You shall not take a mill or its upper stone in pledge, for he who does so takes a life in pledge.
  Deuteronomy 24:12-13 — Same-chapter different protections of essential possessions from pledges: The millstone prohibition identifies a pledge threatening life, while the later instruction returns a poor man's pledged garment for sleeping; the protected objects and required remedies differ.

Deuteronomy 24:7
And if a man is caught stealing one of his brethren, of the sons of Israel, and he subjugates him and sells him, that thief shall die; and you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.
  Exodus 21:17 — Parallel death judgment for abduction and sale: Both prescribe death for stealing an Israelite, overpowering or subjugating him and selling him; the actual Exodus counterpart is21:17 in this edition.

Deuteronomy 24:8
Take heed unto yourself concerning the affliction of leprosy. You shall take diligent heed to do according to all the law which the Levitical priests shall declare unto you; as I commanded you, take heed to do.
  Leviticus 13:2-8 — Priestly skin-affliction instruction and its detailed procedures: Deuteronomy requires diligent performance of what the Levitical priests declare concerning leprosy; Leviticus details examination, isolation and declarations of clean or unclean by Aaron's priestly sons.

Deuteronomy 24:9
Remember what the Lord your God did unto Miriam in the way, when you came forth out of Egypt.
  Numbers 12:9-15 — Explicit remembrance of Miriam's affliction: The command to remember what happened to Miriam points to the narrative of her leprosy, Moses' prayer and seven days outside the camp before her cleansing.

Deuteronomy 24:10
If your neighbour owes a debt, any debt whatsoever, you shall not enter his house to take his pledge.
  Deuteronomy 24:11 — Same-chapter prohibition on entering and required outside procedure: The lender must not enter the debtor's house to seize a pledge; the next verse directs him to remain outside while the debtor brings it out.

Deuteronomy 24:11
You shall stand outside, and the man who owes you the loan shall bring forth the pledge unto you.
  Deuteronomy 24:10 — Same-chapter debtor-controlled presentation of the pledge: The lender's remaining outside and receiving the debtor's brought-out pledge implements the preceding ban on entering the debtor's house to take it.

Deuteronomy 24:12
And if the man is poor, you shall not sleep with his pledge in your keeping.
  Exodus 22:25-26 — Pledged garment must not deprive its owner of sleep covering: Both protect the poor or exposed debtor's night covering by requiring its return; Exodus explains that this garment is the owner's covering for sleep and promises to hear his cry.

Deuteronomy 24:13
You shall surely restore his pledge at the setting of the sun, that he may sleep in his garment and bless you; and it shall be mercy unto you before the Lord your God.
  Exodus 22:25-26 — Sunset return of a pledged garment with mercy language: Both require returning the pledged garment before or at sunset so its owner can sleep in it; Deuteronomy calls this mercy for the lender, while Exodus describes the Lord as merciful.

Deuteronomy 24:14
You shall not withhold the wages of the poor and needy, whether of your brethren or of the sojourners within your cities.
  Leviticus 19:13 — Parallel prohibition on withholding a hired worker's wages: Both prohibit withholding earned wages; Deuteronomy expressly includes needy brethren and resident sojourners, while Leviticus states that wages must not remain overnight until morning.

Deuteronomy 24:15
On the same day you shall pay his wages; the sun shall not go down upon them, for he is poor and sets his hope upon them; lest he cry against you unto the Lord, and there be sin upon you.
  Leviticus 19:13 — Wage-payment deadlines with distinct wording: Deuteronomy requires same-day payment before sunset because the poor worker depends on it; Leviticus forbids retaining the wages overnight until morning, preserving the two stated deadlines.

Deuteronomy 24:16
Fathers shall not die for children, and sons shall not die for fathers. Each shall die for his own sin.
  2 Kings 14:5-6 — Explicit citation restricting punishment to the offender: The king spares the killers' sons, expressly citing Moses' law that fathers and sons must not die for each other and each dies for his own sins.

Deuteronomy 24:17
You shall not pervert the judgment of the sojourner, or of the orphan, or of the widow; neither shall you take a widow’s garment in pledge.
  Deuteronomy 10:18 — Justice for the same three vulnerable groups: The ban on perverting the judgment of sojourner, orphan and widow corresponds to the description of the Lord executing judgment for those same groups, with the pledge restriction additionally stated here.

Deuteronomy 24:18
And you shall remember that you were a household slave in the land of Egypt, and the Lord your God redeemed you thence; therefore I command you to do this thing.
  Deuteronomy 15:15 — Repeated redeemed-slavery remembrance grounding treatment commands: Both recall household slavery in Egypt and the Lord's redemption with the same therefore-I-command formula; chapter15 applies it to generous release, while chapter24 grounds protection of vulnerable people.

Deuteronomy 24:19
And when you reap your harvest in your field, and forget a sheaf in your field, you shall not return to take it. It shall be for the poor, and for the sojourner, and for the orphan, and for the widow; that the Lord your God may bless you in all the works of your hands.
  Leviticus 19:9-10 — Harvest provision left for poor people and resident foreigners: Both reserve harvest produce for poor people and resident foreigners; Deuteronomy specifies a forgotten sheaf and additionally names orphan and widow, while Leviticus forbids complete reaping and repeated vineyard gleaning.

Deuteronomy 24:20
And when you gather your olives, you shall not return to glean what remains behind you. It shall be for the sojourner, and for the orphan, and for the widow. And you shall remember that you were a household slave in the land of Egypt; therefore I command you to do this thing.
  Deuteronomy 24:19 — Same-chapter olive and grain provisions for named recipients: Leaving remaining olives corresponds to leaving the forgotten grain sheaf for the sojourner, orphan and widow; the olive instruction also repeats the memory of Egyptian slavery.

Deuteronomy 24:21
And when you gather the grapes of your vineyard, you shall not glean it afterward; it shall be for the sojourner, and for the orphan, and for the widow.
  Leviticus 19:10 — Parallel restriction on repeated vineyard gleaning: Both prohibit gleaning the vineyard again for the owner's benefit; Leviticus names poor people and resident foreigners, while Deuteronomy names the sojourner, orphan and widow as recipients.

Deuteronomy 24:22
And you shall remember that you were a household slave in the land of Egypt; therefore I command you to do this thing.
  Deuteronomy 24:18 — Same-chapter repeated memory of slavery as command rationale: Both call Israel to remember household slavery in Egypt and use that memory to ground these instructions; verse18 additionally explicitly names the Lord's redemption.

Deuteronomy 25:1
And if there is a dispute between men, and they come unto judgment, and the judges judge them, and justify the righteous, and condemn the ungodly,
  Leviticus 19:15 — Judges required to render righteous rather than partial judgment: Both require righteous judgment; Deuteronomy describes vindicating the righteous and condemning the ungodly, while Leviticus rejects partiality toward either poor or mighty.

Deuteronomy 25:2
then it shall be, if the ungodly man is worthy of stripes, that you shall seat him before the judges, and they shall scourge him before them, by number according to his ungodliness.
  Deuteronomy 25:3 — Same-chapter measured scourging and explicit upper limit: The instruction to number stripes according to guilt is immediately bounded by the forty-stripe maximum and the stated concern against dishonouring the brother through excess.

Deuteronomy 25:3
They shall give him forty stripes, and shall not add thereto; lest, if they add more stripes beyond these, your brother be dishonoured before you.
  2 Corinthians 11:24 — Forty-stripe limit and Paul's different reported count: Deuteronomy caps the punishment at forty stripes, while Paul reports receiving forty less one on five occasions; the comparison preserves the different counts without inventing a reason for the reduction.

Deuteronomy 25:4
You shall not muzzle an ox when it treads out the grain.
  1 Corinthians 9:9-10 — Explicit citation applied to labourers' hoped-for share: Paul explicitly cites Moses' unmuzzled threshing-ox command and applies its principle to ploughing and threshing in hope of sharing, within his discussion of material support.
  1 Timothy 5:17-18 — Explicit citation supporting honour for labouring elders: The letter quotes the command not to muzzle a threshing ox as Scripture within its instruction about honour for elders who labour in word and teaching.

Deuteronomy 25:5
And if brethren dwell together, and one of them dies, and has no seed, the wife of the dead shall not be married outside unto a man who is not near of kin. Her husband’s brother shall go in unto her, and take her unto himself for a wife, and dwell with her as husband.
  Genesis 38:8 — Brother's widow and raising offspring for the deceased: Judah directs Onan to take his brother's wife and raise seed for him, corresponding to the later provision for a brother's childless widow, without identifying every case condition as identical.
  Matthew 22:23-24 — Sadducees invoke Moses' brother-marriage provision: The Sadducees state the rule that a childless man's brother marry his widow and raise offspring, introducing their resurrection question; these words are their appeal, not Jesus' opening statement.

Deuteronomy 25:6
And it shall be, that the child whom she bears shall be established in the name of the dead, and his name shall not be blotted out of Israel.
  Ruth 4:10 — Preserving the deceased's name through marriage and offspring: Both connect the marriage arrangement with preventing the deceased's name being blotted out among Israel or his brothers; Ruth concerns Boaz's kinship transaction rather than asserting an identical brother relationship.

Deuteronomy 25:7
And if the man will not take his brother’s wife, the woman shall go up unto the gate, unto the elders, and say: My husband’s brother will not raise up his brother’s name in Israel; my husband’s brother has refused.
  Deuteronomy 25:8-9 — Same-chapter widow's refusal complaint and elders' response: The widow brings her brother-in-law's refusal before elders at the gate; the following instructions summon him, establish his refusal and prescribe the public sandal-and-spitting response.

Deuteronomy 25:8
And the elders of his city shall call him, and speak unto him; and if he stands and says: I will not take her,
  Deuteronomy 25:7 — Same-chapter elders establish the reported refusal: The elders' summons and the man's stated refusal respond to the widow's complaint that he will not raise his brother's name or marry her.

Deuteronomy 25:9
then his brother’s wife shall draw near before the elders, and loose one sandal from his foot, and spit in his face, and answer and say: Thus shall they do unto the man who will not build up his brother’s house.
  Ruth 4:7-8 — Different sandal-removal transactions before witnesses: Both use sandal removal in a kinship-related transaction; Deuteronomy has the widow remove the refuser's sandal and spit, while Ruth describes the kinsman removing and giving his own sandal without that rebuke.

Deuteronomy 25:10
And his name shall be called in Israel: The House of Him Whose Sandal Was Loosed.
  Deuteronomy 25:9 — Same-chapter household designation explained by public action: The name House of Him Whose Sandal Was Loosed memorializes the preceding sandal-removal and declaration against the man who refuses to build his brother's house.

Deuteronomy 25:11
And if men strive together, a man with his brother, and the wife of one draws near to deliver her husband out of the hand of him that strikes him, and puts forth her hand, and seizes his testicles,
  Deuteronomy 25:12 — Same-chapter specified intervention and its stated penalty: The case describes a wife intervening in a fight by seizing the other man's testicles; the next verse attaches the stated hand penalty and prohibition on sparing.

Deuteronomy 25:12
you shall cut off her hand; your eye shall not spare her.
  Deuteronomy 25:11 — Same-chapter penalty tied to the particular act: The hand penalty follows the specified act of seizing a man's testicles during intervention in her husband's fight, preserving the particular case rather than generalizing the sanction.

Deuteronomy 25:13
You shall not have in your pouch differing weights, a great and a small.
  Leviticus 19:35-36 — Parallel prohibition on unjust weights: Deuteronomy forbids keeping a great and small weight in the pouch, while Leviticus forbids injustice in weights and scales and commands just weights.

Deuteronomy 25:14
You shall not have in your house differing measures, a great and a small.
  Leviticus 19:35-36 — Parallel prohibition on unjust measures: The prohibition on having great and small measures corresponds to Leviticus' ban on injustice in measures and its requirement of a just chous, preserving the named forms.

Deuteronomy 25:15
You shall have a true and just weight, and you shall have a true and just measure, that your days may be long upon the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession.
  Leviticus 19:36 — True weights and measures positively required: Both require just weights and measures; Deuteronomy additionally associates them with prolonged days in the given land, while Leviticus recalls deliverance from Egypt.

Deuteronomy 25:16
For everyone who does these things, everyone who does injustice, is an abomination unto the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 19:35 — Unjust measuring practice condemned: The condemnation of those doing these injustices concludes the differing-weight and measure rules; Leviticus likewise explicitly prohibits injustice in measures, weights and scales.

Deuteronomy 25:17
Remember what Amalek did unto you in the way, when you came forth out of Egypt,
  Exodus 17:8-16 — Amalek remembrance and the earlier battle narrative: Deuteronomy commands remembering Amalek's action after the Exodus; Exodus narrates the battle at Rephidim and the command to write a memorial concerning Amalek.

Deuteronomy 25:18
how he withstood you in the way, and smote your rear, those who were weary behind you, when you were hungry and weary; and he did not fear God.
  1 Samuel 15:2 — Amalek's attack during the Exodus route recalled: Both recall Amalek's encounter with Israel on the way out of Egypt; Deuteronomy specifies the attack upon the weary rear, while Samuel states the later intention to avenge it.

Deuteronomy 25:19
And it shall be, when the Lord your God gives you rest from all your enemies round about you, in the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession to inherit, that you shall blot out the name of Amalek from under heaven. You shall not forget.
  Exodus 17:14 — Repeated blotting-out of Amalek's remembrance: Both describe blotting Amalek's name or remembrance from under heaven; Exodus gives a written memorial to be delivered to Joshua, while Deuteronomy gives a settlement-and-rest condition.

Deuteronomy 26:1
And it shall be, when you enter the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession, and inherit it, and dwell therein,
  Deuteronomy 26:2 — Same-chapter settled-land condition introducing firstfruits instruction: Entering, inheriting and dwelling in the given land provides the condition for taking its first produce in a basket to the Lord's chosen place.

Deuteronomy 26:2
that you shall take of the firstfruits of the produce of your land which the Lord your God gives you, and put it in a basket, and go unto the place which the Lord your God shall choose for His name to be called upon there.
  Exodus 23:19 — Firstfruits taken to the Lord's designated house or place: Both require bringing the land's first produce to the Lord; Deuteronomy specifies a basket and the chosen-name place, while Exodus names the house of the Lord.

Deuteronomy 26:3
And you shall come unto the priest who shall be in those days, and say unto him: I declare this day unto the Lord my God, that I have come into the land which the Lord sware unto our fathers to give us.
  Deuteronomy 26:9-10 — Same-chapter declaration of arrival and firstfruits confession: The declaration to the priest that the worshipper has arrived in the sworn land is developed in the confession naming that land and bringing its firstfruits to the giver.

Deuteronomy 26:4
And the priest shall take the basket out of your hands, and set it before the altar of the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 26:10 — Same-chapter basket presentation with the participants' roles preserved: The priest takes the basket and places it before the altar; the later instruction addresses the giver placing the firstfruits before the Lord and bowing, preserving the distinct stated roles.

Deuteronomy 26:5
And you shall answer and say before the Lord your God: My father forsook Syria, and went down into Egypt, and sojourned there, few in number; and there he became a great nation, and a multitude numerous and great.
  Deuteronomy 10:22 — Few Egyptian sojourners becoming a great multitude: Both contrast few ancestors entering Egypt with the later multitude; chapter10 specifies seventy souls, while this confession says the father forsook Syria and became a great nation in Egypt.

Deuteronomy 26:6
And the Egyptians afflicted us, and humbled us, and laid hard labours upon us.
  Exodus 1:11-14 — Egyptian oppression summarized and narrated: The confession's affliction, humiliation and hard labour correspond to Exodus' overseers, forced clay and brick work and Egyptian oppression of Israel.

Deuteronomy 26:7
And we cried unto the Lord, the God of our fathers; and the Lord heard our voice, and saw our humiliation, and our toil, and our distress.
  Exodus 2:23-25 — Israel's labour cry heard by God: Both describe Israel crying under Egyptian labour and God hearing and seeing their condition; Exodus additionally states remembrance of the covenant with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob.

Deuteronomy 26:8
And the Lord Himself brought us forth out of Egypt with great strength, and with a mighty hand, and with His uplifted arm, and with great visions, and with signs, and with wonders.
  Deuteronomy 4:34 — Repeated deliverance recital of hand, arm, visions and signs: Both recount deliverance from Egypt through mighty hand, uplifted arm, great visions, signs and wonders, preserving the explicit features of the Lord's action named in each recital.

Deuteronomy 26:9
And He brought us unto this place, and gave us this land, a land flowing with milk and honey.
  Deuteronomy 6:23 — Departure from Egypt leading to the promised land: Both connect being brought out from Egypt with being brought into the land given according to the ancestral pledge; chapter26 explicitly calls it flowing with milk and honey.

Deuteronomy 26:10
And now, behold, I have brought the firstfruits of the produce of the land which Thou hast given me, O Lord, a land flowing with milk and honey. And you shall set them before the Lord your God, and bow down there before the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 26:2-4 — Same-chapter firstfruits declaration carrying out the basket instruction: The giver presents firstfruits from the Lord's land and bows, carrying out the earlier basket-and-priest presentation instructions while adding the spoken acknowledgement of the divine gift.

Deuteronomy 26:11
And you shall rejoice in all the good things which the Lord your God has given unto you and unto your house, you, and the Levite, and the sojourner among you.
  Deuteronomy 12:7 — Household rejoicing over the Lord's gifts at the worship place: Both require rejoicing in the good or blessed things given by the Lord with the household; chapter26 explicitly includes Levite and sojourner in this firstfruits setting.

Deuteronomy 26:12
And when you have finished tithing all the tithe of the produce of your land in the third year, you shall give the second tithe unto the Levite, and unto the sojourner, and unto the orphan, and unto the widow; and they shall eat within your cities and be filled.
  Deuteronomy 14:28-29 — Third-year local tithe provision with distinct descriptions: Both direct third-year produce to Levite, sojourner, orphan and widow to eat within the cities; chapter26 names the second tithe, while chapter14 says all the tithe without that designation.

Deuteronomy 26:13
And you shall say before the Lord your God: I have cleared the holy things out of my house, and have given them unto the Levite, and unto the sojourner, and unto the orphan, and unto the widow, according to all Thy commandments which Thou hast commanded me. I have not transgressed Thy commandment, neither have I forgotten it.
  Deuteronomy 26:12 — Same-chapter distribution declared complete after its command: The giver's declaration that the holy things have been cleared from the house and distributed to the four named groups corresponds to the preceding third-year tithe instruction.

Deuteronomy 26:14
And I have not eaten thereof in my mourning, neither have I offered thereof for an unclean use, nor given thereof unto the dead. I have hearkened unto the voice of the Lord my God; I have done according to all that Thou hast commanded me.
  Leviticus 22:3-7 — Different purity safeguards concerning holy food: The tithe declaration denies unclean use and eating in mourning or giving to the dead; Leviticus restricts unclean Aaronic descendants from sacred food until cleansing, preserving the different roles and rules.

Deuteronomy 26:15
Look down from Thy holy house, from heaven, and bless Thy people Israel, and the land which Thou hast given them, as Thou didst swear unto our fathers to give us a land flowing with milk and honey.
  Deuteronomy 26:9 — Same-chapter blessing requested upon the confessed gift of land: The prayer asks blessing on Israel and the milk-and-honey land given under the ancestral oath, returning to the confession that the Lord brought them there and gave that land.

Deuteronomy 26:16
This day the Lord your God has commanded you to do all these ordinances and judgments; and you shall keep and do them with all your heart and with all your soul.
  Deuteronomy 10:12-13 — Wholehearted observance of ordinances and judgments: Both require obedience to the Lord's instruction with wholehearted allegiance; chapter10 combines love and service of heart and soul with keeping commandments and ordinances for Israel's good.

Deuteronomy 26:17
You have chosen God this day to be your God, and to walk in His ways, and to keep His ordinances and His judgments, and to hearken unto His voice.
  Joshua 24:21-24 — Choosing the Lord and pledging to hear His voice: Both describe the people choosing the Lord and committing to obey His voice; Joshua records their spoken pledge and commands removal of foreign gods, while Deuteronomy enumerates walking and keeping ordinances.

Deuteronomy 26:18
And the Lord has chosen you this day to be unto Him a people for His own possession, as He has spoken unto you, to keep all His commandments,
  Deuteronomy 7:6 — Repeated choice of Israel as the Lord's own possession: Both explicitly describe the Lord choosing Israel as a people for His own possession; chapter26 adds the obligation to keep all His commandments.

Deuteronomy 26:19
and to be above all the nations, as He has made you renowned, and a boast, and glorious, that you should be a holy people unto the Lord your God, as He has spoken.
  Exodus 19:5-6 — Holy people and special covenant possession: Both describe a people distinguished among the nations as the Lord's holy people or nation; Exodus conditions treasured possession on hearing and covenant keeping and additionally names a royal priesthood.

Deuteronomy 27:1
And Moses and the elders of Israel commanded, saying: Keep all these commandments which I command you this day.
  Deuteronomy 26:16 — Renewed command following wholehearted-obedience declaration: Moses and the elders command keeping all these commandments, following the declaration that the Lord commanded these ordinances and judgments for wholehearted performance.

Deuteronomy 27:2
And it shall be, in the day when you pass over the Jordan into the land which the Lord your God gives you, that you shall set up great stones for yourself, and plaster them with plaster.
  Deuteronomy 27:4 — Same-chapter stone-and-plaster instruction with specified destination: The command to erect and plaster great stones after crossing Jordan is repeated with Mount Ebal explicitly designated as their location.

Deuteronomy 27:3
And you shall write upon the stones all the words of this law, when you pass over the Jordan, when you enter the land which the Lord, the God of your fathers, gives you, a land flowing with milk and honey, as the Lord, the God of your fathers, has spoken unto you.
  Joshua 9:2c — Stone-inscription command and later narrated writing: Deuteronomy commands writing the law on stones after crossing; the actual Joshua continuation records writing Moses' law on stones before Israel, without repeating the plaster detail.

Deuteronomy 27:4
And it shall be, when you pass over the Jordan, that you shall set up these stones, which I command you this day, upon Mount Ebal; and you shall plaster them with plaster.
  Deuteronomy 27:2 — Same-chapter repeated stone-and-plaster command with named destination: The instruction to set up and plaster these stones on Mount Ebal repeats the earlier stone-and-plaster command for the Jordan crossing, now specifying the mountain destination.

Deuteronomy 27:5
And you shall build there an altar unto the Lord your God, an altar of stones; you shall lay no iron upon them.
  Exodus 20:25 — Parallel altar-stone restrictions: Both restrict working altar stones with a blade or iron; Exodus forbids hewn stones and states defilement, while Deuteronomy expressly says no iron shall be laid upon them.
  Joshua 9:2b — No-iron altar command and explicitly Mosaic construction: Joshua describes an altar of whole stones untouched by iron and expressly invokes Moses' written command, corresponding to the altar restriction here.

Deuteronomy 27:6
You shall build an altar of whole stones unto the Lord your God, and you shall offer thereon whole burnt offerings unto the Lord your God.
  Joshua 9:2b — Whole-stone altar and burnt offerings commanded and narrated: Both specify a whole-stone altar and whole burnt offerings; Joshua's account expressly relates its construction and offerings to Moses' law.

Deuteronomy 27:7
And you shall sacrifice there a sacrifice of deliverance unto the Lord your God, and eat, and be filled, and rejoice before the Lord your God.
  Joshua 9:2b — Related altar sacrifices with preserved offering names: Deuteronomy commands a sacrifice of deliverance with eating and rejoicing; Joshua records the altar's sacrifice of well-being, preserving the differing supplied offering descriptions and additional meal instructions.

Deuteronomy 27:8
And you shall write upon the stones all this law very plainly.
  Joshua 9:2c — Repeated law-inscription command and later written law: The requirement to write all the law plainly on stones corresponds to Joshua's statement that he wrote Moses' law upon stones before Israel.

Deuteronomy 27:9
