And Moses and the Levitical priests spoke unto all Israel, saying: Keep silence, and hear, O Israel; this day you have become a people unto the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 26:18 — Peoplehood declaration and divine choice: Both declare Israel a people belonging to the Lord this day; chapter26 explicitly calls them His chosen possession, while this verse calls for silence and hearing.

Deuteronomy 27:10
And you shall hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God, and do all His commandments and His ordinances which I command you this day.
  Deuteronomy 26:16-17 — Hearing and doing in adjacent covenant declarations: Both combine hearing the Lord's voice with performing commandments, ordinances or judgments, with chapter26 additionally specifying wholeheartedness and choosing God as their God.

Deuteronomy 27:11
And Moses commanded the people in that day, saying:
  Deuteronomy 27:12-14 — Same-chapter introductory command and specified ceremonial roles: The announcement that Moses commanded the people introduces the named tribal mountain assignments and the Levites' instruction to address Israel aloud.

Deuteronomy 27:12
These shall stand on Mount Gerizim to bless the people after crossing the Jordan: Simeon, Levi, Judah, Issachar, Joseph, and Benjamin.
  Deuteronomy 11:29 — Gerizim's blessing role specified by tribes: The earlier command assigns blessing to Mount Gerizim; this verse names the six tribes who are to stand there after crossing Jordan.
  Joshua 9:2d — Mountain assignment and later gathered assembly: Joshua places half of the gathered Israel near Gerizim and half near Ebal, explicitly recalling Moses' command, without repeating this verse's individual tribal list.

Deuteronomy 27:13
And these shall stand on Mount Ebal for the curse: Reuben, Gad, Asher, Zebulun, Dan, and Naphtali.
  Deuteronomy 11:29 — Ebal's curse role specified by tribes: The earlier instruction assigns the curse to Mount Ebal; this verse gives the particular six tribes designated to stand there for that declaration.
  Joshua 9:2d — Ebal assignment and later two-mountain arrangement: Joshua describes half of Israel near Ebal and half near Gerizim with the ark and officials, corresponding to the commanded arrangement without reproducing the tribal names.

Deuteronomy 27:14
And the Levites shall answer and say unto all Israel with a loud voice:
  Deuteronomy 27:15 — Same-chapter Levite announcement and congregational response: The direction for Levites to address all Israel loudly is followed by the first spoken curse and the instruction for all the people to answer So be it.

Deuteronomy 27:15
Cursed is the man who makes a graven and molten image, an abomination unto the Lord, the work of the hands of a craftsman, and sets it in a secret place. And all the people shall answer and say: So be it.
  Exodus 20:4 — Image-making prohibition pronounced as a curse: The curse upon making a graven or molten image corresponds to the prohibition on making idols or likenesses; Deuteronomy additionally specifies craftsmanship and secret placement.
  Leviticus 26:1 — Parallel restrictions on manufactured worship objects: Both prohibit manufactured images and describe prohibited placement; Leviticus names pillars and conspicuous stones for worship, while this curse names a secretly placed crafted image.

Deuteronomy 27:16
Cursed is he who dishonours his father or his mother. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Deuteronomy 5:16 — Curse for dishonour and positive parental command: The curse upon dishonouring father or mother is the negative counterpart of the command to honour both parents, where wellbeing and long land life accompany obedience.

Deuteronomy 27:17
Cursed is he who removes his neighbour’s boundaries. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Deuteronomy 19:14 — Boundary-removal prohibition and corresponding curse: Both address removing a neighbour's boundaries; chapter19 identifies them as established in the allotted inheritance, while this verse pronounces the congregationally affirmed curse.

Deuteronomy 27:18
Cursed is he who leads the blind astray in the way. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Leviticus 19:14 — Related prohibitions on harming blind persons in their path: The curse forbids leading the blind astray on the road; Leviticus forbids putting a stumbling block before the blind, preserving the particular differing actions.

Deuteronomy 27:19
Cursed is he who perverts the judgment of the sojourner, and of the orphan, and of the widow. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Deuteronomy 24:17 — Repeated protection of the same three groups' judgment: Both forbid perverting the judgment of the sojourner, orphan and widow; chapter24 additionally prohibits taking the widow's garment in pledge.

Deuteronomy 27:20
Cursed is he who lies with his father’s wife, for he has uncovered his father’s covering. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Leviticus 18:8 — Father's wife prohibition and paternal-covering explanation: Both prohibit the sexual act involving one's father's wife and explain its relation to the father's own covering or nakedness, preserving the supplied terms.

Deuteronomy 27:21
Cursed is he who lies with any beast. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Leviticus 18:23 — Parallel prohibition of sexual relations with animals: Both prohibit lying with an animal; Leviticus gives further male and female descriptions and defilement language, while Deuteronomy supplies the congregational curse.

Deuteronomy 27:22
Cursed is he who lies with his sister, the daughter of his father or of his mother. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Leviticus 18:9 — Sister prohibition with paternal and maternal scope: Both specify the sister who is a daughter of the father or mother; Leviticus also distinguishes birth at home or outside.

Deuteronomy 27:23
Cursed is he who lies with his mother-in-law. And all the people shall say: So be it. Cursed is he who lies with his wife’s sister. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Leviticus 18:18 — Wife's-sister clause within the combined source unit: The second curse in this source unit concerns lying with the wife's sister; Leviticus prohibits taking a woman alongside her living sister, while the mother-in-law clause remains a separate first curse.

Deuteronomy 27:24
Cursed is he who strikes his neighbour treacherously. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Exodus 21:14 — Treacherous attack and deceitful killing: The curse upon striking a neighbour treacherously corresponds to the law concerning attacking a neighbour to kill by deceit; Exodus additionally denies altar refuge in that case.

Deuteronomy 27:25
Cursed is he who takes a bribe to slay an innocent life. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Exodus 23:7-8 — Innocent life and corrupt payment: The curse joins a bribe with killing an innocent person; Exodus prohibits killing the innocent and receiving gifts that corrupt judgment, without treating all its cases as one transaction.

Deuteronomy 27:26
Cursed is every man who does not continue in all the words of this law, to do them. And all the people shall say: So be it.
  Galatians 3:10 — Explicit citation of the law-observance curse: Galatians expressly cites the curse upon everyone who does not abide by all the written law to do it, using it in the argument about works of law.

Deuteronomy 28:1
And it shall be, when you pass over the Jordan into the land which the Lord your God gives you, if you diligently hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God, to keep and do all His commandments which I command you this day, that the Lord your God shall set you above all the nations of the earth.
  Deuteronomy 26:18-19 — Obedient chosen people placed above the nations: Both combine keeping the Lord's commandments with Israel's special standing above the nations; chapter26 calls them His possession and holy people, while chapter28 states the condition explicitly.

Deuteronomy 28:2
And all these blessings shall come upon you, and find you, if you diligently hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 11:27 — Blessing conditioned on hearing commandments: Both explicitly make blessing conditional upon hearkening to the Lord's instruction, with this verse introducing the blessings that will come upon the obedient people.

Deuteronomy 28:3
Blessed are you in the city, and blessed are you in the field.
  Deuteronomy 28:16 — Same-chapter matched city-and-field blessing and curse: The blessing upon city and field is later reversed by a curse naming those exact two settings, retaining the deliberate positive and negative pairing.

Deuteronomy 28:4
Blessed are the offspring of your womb, and the produce of your land, the herds of your cattle, and the flocks of your sheep.
  Deuteronomy 7:13 — Parallel human, agricultural and livestock fertility blessings: Both promise blessing on human offspring, land produce and livestock increase; chapter7 also explicitly names grain, wine and oil within that provision.
  Deuteronomy 28:18 — Same-chapter reversed blessing of offspring and produce: The later curse repeats this verse's human offspring, land produce, herds and flocks, reversing the blessing across the same enumerated areas.

Deuteronomy 28:5
Blessed are your storehouses and your reserves.
  Deuteronomy 28:17 — Same-chapter storehouse-and-reserve blessing reversed: The same storehouses and reserves blessed here are explicitly cursed later, preserving the matched storage vocabulary rather than substituting a different edition's objects.

Deuteronomy 28:6
Blessed are you when you come in, and blessed are you when you go out.
  Deuteronomy 28:19 — Same-chapter coming-and-going blessing reversed: Both mention coming in and going out, with the later curse reversing the blessing and the order of the two movements.

Deuteronomy 28:7
The Lord your God deliver your enemies who rise against you, crushed before your face. By one way shall they come out against you, and by seven ways shall they flee before you.
  Leviticus 26:7-8 — Enemy-defeat promises with different numerical descriptions: Both promise enemy defeat; Deuteronomy describes one approach route and seven escape routes, whereas Leviticus gives five pursuing a hundred and a hundred pursuing tens of thousands, without equating routes and combatant ratios.
  Deuteronomy 28:25 — Same-chapter exact reversal of one-way and seven-way warfare: Enemies coming by one way and fleeing by seven are reversed when Israel goes out one way and flees seven ways, changing which side suffers defeat.

Deuteronomy 28:8
The Lord send His blessing upon you in your storerooms, and in all that you put your hand unto, upon the land which the Lord your God gives you.
  Deuteronomy 28:20 — Same-chapter opposed results upon the work of the hands: Blessing upon whatever Israel puts its hand to is reversed by want, famine and wasting upon whatever it undertakes after forsaking the Lord.

Deuteronomy 28:9
The Lord your God establish you unto Himself a holy people, as He sware unto your fathers, if you hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God, and walk in His ways.
  Deuteronomy 26:18-19 — Holy peoplehood and covenant command keeping: Both describe Israel established or chosen as the Lord's holy people in connection with keeping His commandments, with the earlier passage also naming renown among nations.

Deuteronomy 28:10
And all the nations of the earth shall see that the name of the Lord is called upon you, and they shall fear you.
  Deuteronomy 11:25 — Promised fear among the peoples: Both promise that other peoples will fear Israel through the Lord's action; this verse specifically connects their response with seeing His name called upon Israel.

Deuteronomy 28:11
And the Lord your God shall multiply you in good things, in the offspring of your womb, and in the produce of your land, and in the offspring of your cattle, upon the land which the Lord sware unto your fathers to give you.
  Deuteronomy 7:13 — Repeated multiplication across womb, field and livestock: Both connect the land sworn to the fathers with blessing or multiplication in human offspring, field produce and livestock offspring, preserving the specific threefold provision.

Deuteronomy 28:12
The Lord open unto you His good treasure, heaven, to give rain unto your land in its season, and to bless all the works of your hands. And you shall lend unto many nations, but you shall not borrow; and you shall rule over many nations, but they shall not rule over you.
  Deuteronomy 11:14 — Seasonal heavenly rain and fruitful work: Both promise rain in its season for the land; chapter11 specifies early and latter rain and named crops, while this verse also speaks of the heavenly treasury and blessed work.
  Deuteronomy 15:6 — Repeated international lending and rule promise: Both state that Israel will lend to many nations without borrowing and rule without being ruled, with this verse additionally giving rain and work as blessings.

Deuteronomy 28:13
The Lord your God make you the head, and not the tail; and you shall be above, and not beneath, if you hearken unto the commandments of the Lord your God which I command you this day to keep and do.
  Deuteronomy 28:44 — Same-chapter head-and-tail positions reversed: The promise that Israel will be head rather than tail is reversed when the sojourner becomes lender and head while Israel becomes tail.

Deuteronomy 28:14
You shall not turn aside from any of the words which I command you this day, to the right hand or to the left, to go after other gods and serve them.
  Deuteronomy 5:32 — Repeated prohibition of turning right or left: Both forbid turning from the commanded words to right or left; this verse specifically identifies following and serving other gods as the deviation in view.

Deuteronomy 28:15
And it shall be, if you do not hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God, to keep and do all His commandments which I command you this day, that all these curses shall come upon you, and overtake you.
  Leviticus 26:14-16 — Disobedience condition introducing covenant judgments: Both introduce adverse covenant consequences through failure to obey and perform the Lord's commands; Leviticus additionally names dissolving the covenant before beginning its particular afflictions.

Deuteronomy 28:16
Cursed are you in the city, and cursed are you in the field.
  Deuteronomy 28:3 — Same-chapter city-and-field curse reversing blessing: The city and field cursed here are the exact two settings blessed at the start of the chapter, giving a particular matched reversal.

Deuteronomy 28:17
Cursed are your storehouses and your reserves.
  Deuteronomy 28:5 — Same-chapter curse upon the previously blessed stores: The curse repeats the storehouses and reserves of the earlier blessing, reversing its outcome for those same named provisions.

Deuteronomy 28:18
Cursed are the offspring of your womb, and the produce of your land, the herds of your cattle, and the flocks of your sheep.
  Deuteronomy 28:4 — Same-chapter offspring-and-produce curse reversing the same list: Human offspring, land produce, cattle herds and sheep flocks recur from the blessing list, now explicitly under the opposite pronouncement.

Deuteronomy 28:19
Cursed are you when you go out, and cursed are you when you come in.
  Deuteronomy 28:6 — Same-chapter daily-movement curse reversing blessing: Going out and coming in receive the curse instead of the earlier blessing upon coming in and going out, preserving both movements and the changed order.

Deuteronomy 28:20
The Lord send upon you want, and famine, and wasting, in all that you put your hand unto, whatsoever you do, until He destroys you, and causes you to perish speedily, because of your evil practices, because you have forsaken Me.
  Deuteronomy 28:8 — Same-chapter frustrated work contrasting with blessed work: Want, famine and wasting strike whatever the hand undertakes after forsaking the Lord, reversing the earlier promise of blessing upon those same undertakings.

Deuteronomy 28:21
The Lord make death cleave unto you, until He consumes you from the land whither you go to inherit it.
  Leviticus 26:25 — Death included among covenant afflictions: Both announce death coming upon the people within covenant judgment; Leviticus places it amid sword, city flight and enemy delivery, while Deuteronomy says death cleaves until the people are consumed from the land.

Deuteronomy 28:22
The Lord smite you with destitution, and fever, and chills, and inflammation, and slaughter, and wind blight, and pallor; and they shall pursue you until they destroy you.
  Leviticus 26:16 — Related illness-and-wasting judgment lists with distinct terms: Both include illness within covenant warnings, but the particular fevers, chills, inflammation and other terms here differ from Leviticus' distress, scab and jaundice.

Deuteronomy 28:23
And the heaven above your head shall be bronze, and the earth beneath you iron.
  Leviticus 26:19 — Metal heaven-and-earth images with exchanged assignments: Both describe heaven and earth with iron and bronze under judgment; Deuteronomy assigns bronze to heaven and iron to earth, while Leviticus reverses those assignments.

Deuteronomy 28:24
The Lord give dust for the rain of your land; and earth shall descend upon you from heaven, until He wears you away, and until He destroys you.
  Deuteronomy 11:17 — Rain withheld in threatened land judgment: The dust replacing rain develops the earlier warning that the Lord will shut heaven so rain and fruit fail, while retaining the distinct imagery in each passage.

Deuteronomy 28:25
The Lord give you over to slaughter before your enemies. You shall go forth by one way against them, and you shall flee by seven ways before them; and you shall be scattered among all the kingdoms of the earth.
  Deuteronomy 28:7 — Same-chapter reversal of the identical battlefield route pattern: Israel's one-way advance and seven-way flight reverse the earlier promise that its enemies would approach one way and flee seven ways.
  Leviticus 26:17 — Parallel warnings of defeat and flight before enemies: Both describe Israel falling before enemies and fleeing; Leviticus adds flight when no one pursues, while Deuteronomy gives the one-and-seven pattern and scattering among kingdoms.

Deuteronomy 28:26
And your dead shall be food for the birds of heaven, and for the beasts of the earth; and there shall be none to drive them away.
  Jeremiah 7:33 — Closely repeated unguarded-corpse judgment: Both describe the people's dead becoming food for heaven's birds and earth's beasts, with no one to drive the creatures away.

Deuteronomy 28:27
The Lord smite you with the Egyptian ulcer upon the buttocks, and with grievous scab, and with itching, so that you cannot be healed.
  Deuteronomy 28:35 — Same-chapter incurable ulcer descriptions with different locations: Both threaten grievous ulceration that cannot be healed; this verse mentions the Egyptian ulcer upon the buttocks, while the later verse names knees, legs and the whole body.

Deuteronomy 28:28
The Lord smite you with madness, and with blindness, and with bewilderment of mind.
  Deuteronomy 28:29 — Same-chapter blindness threat and described groping: The threat of blindness and mental bewilderment is followed by the particular image of groping at noon as the blind grope in darkness.

Deuteronomy 28:29
And you shall grope at noonday, as the blind gropes in darkness, and He shall not prosper your ways; and you shall be wronged and plundered all your days, and there shall be none to help you.
  Isaiah 59:10 — Shared blindness, groping and noonday-darkness imagery: Both describe blind-like groping and noon becoming like darkness or midnight; Isaiah places the image within the community's confession of absent judgment and salvation.

Deuteronomy 28:30
You shall take a wife, and another man shall have her; you shall build a house, and shall not dwell therein; you shall plant a vineyard, and shall not gather its fruit.
  Deuteronomy 20:5-7 — House, vineyard and wife losses across distinct settings: Both enumerate a house, vineyard and wife that another may possess or enjoy; the war exemptions seek to avert such loss, while this curse announces it.

Deuteronomy 28:31
Your calf shall be slaughtered before your eyes, and you shall not eat thereof; your donkey shall be seized from you, and shall not be restored unto you; your sheep shall be given unto your enemies, and there shall be none to help you.
  Deuteronomy 28:51 — Same-chapter livestock loss and the invading consumer: The calf, donkey and sheep losses anticipate the later invader consuming livestock offspring and leaving no cattle or sheep, preserving the distinct animals and actions in each description.

Deuteronomy 28:32
Your sons and your daughters shall be given unto another nation, and your eyes shall look upon them and waste away; and your hand shall have no strength.
  Deuteronomy 28:41 — Same-chapter children transferred to another nation and captivity: Sons and daughters given to another nation correspond to the later explanation that children will not remain with their parents because they go into captivity.

Deuteronomy 28:33
A nation which you do not know shall consume the yield of your land and all your labours; and you shall be wronged and crushed all your days.
  Leviticus 26:16 — Parallel loss of agricultural labour to enemies: Both describe the people's sown produce being consumed by adversaries or an unknown nation, turning their cultivation and labour into provision for others.

Deuteronomy 28:34
And you shall be driven mad by the sights which your eyes shall see.
  Deuteronomy 28:31-33 — Same-chapter madness provoked by specified witnessed losses: Madness from what the eyes see follows cattle seized or slaughtered, children taken away and foreigners consuming the land's yield, specifying the distressing sights in this sequence.

Deuteronomy 28:35
The Lord smite you upon the knees and upon the legs with a grievous ulcer, so that you cannot be healed, from the soles of your feet unto the crown of your head.
  Deuteronomy 28:27 — Same-chapter incurable affliction revisited over different body regions: The unhealable ulcer upon knees, legs and from foot to crown corresponds to the earlier incurable Egyptian ulcer and skin afflictions, while the described locations remain distinct.

Deuteronomy 28:36
The Lord lead you, and your rulers whom you shall set over yourself, unto a nation which neither you nor your fathers have known; and there you shall serve other gods, wood and stone.
  Deuteronomy 4:27-28 — Scattering and service to wooden and stone gods: Both warn of being taken among other nations and serving gods of wood and stone; this verse expressly includes the people and the rulers they appoint.

Deuteronomy 28:37
And there you shall become a riddle, and a parable, and a tale among all the nations whither the Lord shall lead you.
  Deuteronomy 29:21-23 — National suffering made a subject of outsiders' speech: The warning that Israel becomes a riddle, parable and tale among nations corresponds to the later scene of descendants and foreigners observing the devastated land and asking why.

Deuteronomy 28:38
You shall carry much seed into the field, and gather little; for the locust shall devour it.
  Micah 6:15 — Sowing without benefiting from the harvest: Both announce sowing without the expected harvest; Deuteronomy specifies much seed, little gathered and locust consumption, while Micah's shorter statement gives no locust cause.

Deuteronomy 28:39
You shall plant a vineyard and dress it, but shall neither drink wine nor rejoice in it; for the worm shall devour it.
  Micah 6:15 — Wine production without drinking its result: Both deny the producer the enjoyment of wine; Deuteronomy names planted and dressed vineyards lost to worms, while Micah describes wine pressed but not drunk.

Deuteronomy 28:40
You shall have olive trees throughout all your borders, but shall not anoint yourself with oil; for your olives shall fall away.
  Micah 6:15 — Olive labour without anointing oil: Both explicitly describe failure to anoint oneself with oil despite olive provision or labour; Deuteronomy names falling olives and Micah speaks of pressing them.

Deuteronomy 28:41
You shall beget sons and daughters, but they shall not be yours; for they shall go into captivity.
  Deuteronomy 28:32 — Same-chapter captivity explaining loss of children: Children born but not retained because of captivity correspond to the earlier statement that sons and daughters are given to another nation before their powerless parents.

Deuteronomy 28:42
Blight shall consume all your trees and the produce of your land.
  Leviticus 26:20 — Parallel failure of trees and field produce: Both announce loss of the land's agricultural yield and trees' fruit; Deuteronomy attributes the consumption to blight, while Leviticus states that field and tree will not yield.

Deuteronomy 28:43
The sojourner who is among you shall rise above you higher and higher; but you shall descend lower and lower.
  Deuteronomy 28:13 — Same-chapter reversal of relative height and standing: The sojourner rising higher as Israel descends reverses the promised position of Israel above rather than beneath, with the following verse specifying the lender-and-borrower relationship.

Deuteronomy 28:44
He shall lend unto you, but you shall not lend unto him; he shall be the head, and you shall be the tail.
  Deuteronomy 15:6 — Lending and rule blessing reversed in the sojourner relation: The earlier promise makes Israel lender rather than borrower and ruler rather than ruled; here the sojourner lends while Israel does not, becoming head while Israel is tail.

Deuteronomy 28:45
And all these curses shall come upon you, and pursue you, and overtake you, until He destroys you, and until He causes you to perish; for you did not hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God, to keep His commandments and His ordinances which He commanded you.
  Deuteronomy 28:15 — Same-chapter announced curse condition restated as a closing summary: The summary repeats the opening threat of curses coming upon and overtaking those who fail to hear and keep the commands, now adding pursuit and destruction.

Deuteronomy 28:46
And they shall be signs and wonders upon you and upon your seed for ever,
  Deuteronomy 29:21-23 — Afflictions as signs for descendants and outside observers: The curses become signs upon the people and their seed; the later scene describes coming descendants and foreigners observing land-plagues and asking the meaning of that anger.

Deuteronomy 28:47
because you did not serve the Lord your God with gladness and a good heart, for the abundance of all things.
  Deuteronomy 12:7 — Failure of glad service contrasted with commanded rejoicing: The charge of failing to serve gladly amid abundance contrasts with the command to eat before the Lord and rejoice with the household because of His blessing.

Deuteronomy 28:48
And you shall serve your enemies whom the Lord shall send against you, in hunger, and in thirst, and in nakedness, and in want of all things; and He shall put an iron collar upon your neck, until He destroys you.
  Leviticus 26:13 — Enemy servitude and an iron collar reversing liberation imagery: The curse places an iron collar upon Israel in enemy service; Leviticus recalls release from Egyptian slavery through a shattered yoke, preserving their distinct bondage imagery.
  Deuteronomy 28:47 — Same-chapter two contrasting forms of service: Failure to serve the Lord gladly amid abundance is followed by compelled enemy service amid hunger, thirst, nakedness and lack, making the contrast explicit.

Deuteronomy 28:49
The Lord shall bring against you a nation from afar, from the end of the earth, as the swooping of an eagle, a nation whose speech you shall not understand,
  Jeremiah 5:15 — Distant invading nation with unintelligible speech: Both announce a nation brought from far away whose speech Israel will not understand; Deuteronomy additionally compares its coming with an eagle's swoop.

Deuteronomy 28:50
a nation shameless of countenance, which shall not regard the face of the aged, nor have mercy upon the young.
  2 Chronicles 36:17 — Merciless invader description and later invasion narrative: Deuteronomy describes disregard for aged and young; Chronicles recounts young men killed, virgins unspared and elders carried away, retaining the different fates without asserting an exclusive fulfillment.

Deuteronomy 28:51
And it shall consume the offspring of your cattle and the produce of your land, leaving you neither grain, nor wine, nor oil, nor the herds of your cattle, nor the flocks of your sheep, until it destroys you.
  Jeremiah 5:17 — Invader consuming harvest and livestock: Both describe the invading nation's consumption of agricultural produce and livestock, with Jeremiah naming vineyards, figs and olives and Deuteronomy naming grain, wine, oil, cattle and sheep.

Deuteronomy 28:52
And it shall wear you down in all your cities, until your high and fortified walls wherein you trust are cast down throughout all your land; and it shall afflict you in all your cities which the Lord your God has given you.
  Jeremiah 5:17 — Destruction of trusted fortified cities: Both explicitly describe the attacking nation destroying fortified cities in which the people trusted; Deuteronomy expands this into siege throughout the cities and collapsing high walls.

Deuteronomy 28:53
And you shall eat the offspring of your womb, the flesh of your sons and of your daughters whom the Lord your God has given you, in your straitness and in your distress wherewith your enemy shall afflict you.
  Leviticus 26:29 — Parallel threat of eating sons' and daughters' flesh: Both explicitly threaten eating the flesh of one's own sons and daughters; Deuteronomy additionally states the besieging enemy and desperate distress as the circumstances.

Deuteronomy 28:54
The tender and most delicate man among you shall look grudgingly upon his brother, and the wife of his bosom, and the children who remain unto him,
  Deuteronomy 28:55 — Same-chapter delicate man's hostility and the withheld food: The delicate man's grudging attitude toward brother, wife and remaining children is explained by the next verse as withholding the flesh he eats when nothing else remains.

Deuteronomy 28:55
begrudging any of them the flesh of his children which he eats, because nothing is left unto him, in your straitness and in your distress wherewith your enemies shall afflict you in all your cities.
  Leviticus 26:29 — Child-flesh threat developed through siege scarcity: The man's grudging withholding of children's flesh develops the same threatened eating of sons and daughters stated concisely in Leviticus, adding the siege and lack of other food.

Deuteronomy 28:56
And the tender and most delicate woman among you, whose foot has never ventured to tread upon the earth because of her delicacy and tenderness, shall look grudgingly upon the husband of her bosom, and her son, and her daughter,
  Deuteronomy 28:57 — Same-chapter delicate woman's hostility and desperate consumption: The woman's extreme delicacy and grudging attitude to husband and children are followed by the stated secret consumption of afterbirth and her child amid siege deprivation.

Deuteronomy 28:57
and upon her afterbirth which comes forth from between her thighs, and the child whom she bears; for she shall eat them secretly for want of all things, in your straitness and in your distress wherewith your enemy shall afflict you in all your cities.
  Deuteronomy 28:53 — Same-chapter particular maternal case within the siege warning: The detailed case of afterbirth and the child she bears consumed secretly elaborates the earlier general warning that parents will eat their children under an enemy's desperate siege.

Deuteronomy 28:58
If you do not hearken to do all the words of this law written in this book, to fear this honoured and wondrous name, the Lord your God,
  Deuteronomy 28:59 — Same-chapter written-law and divine-name condition followed by plagues: Failure to perform the written law and fear the honoured divine name introduces the next verse's extraordinary, enduring afflictions upon the people and their offspring.

Deuteronomy 28:59
then the Lord shall make your plagues and the plagues of your seed extraordinary, plagues great and astonishing, and diseases grievous and enduring.
  Deuteronomy 28:60-61 — Same-chapter enduring disease announcement expanded by scope: The great, enduring plagues upon parents and offspring are developed by the following references to dreaded Egyptian sufferings and infirmities not written in the law book.

Deuteronomy 28:60
And He shall bring back upon you all the grievous suffering of Egypt which you dreaded; and they shall cleave unto you.
  Deuteronomy 7:15 — Egyptian disease promise reversed: The earlier obedient blessing withholds the grievous diseases of Egypt from Israel; this curse instead brings back the dreaded Egyptian sufferings to cleave to them.

Deuteronomy 28:61
And every infirmity, and every plague which is not written in the book of this law, shall the Lord bring upon you, until He destroys you.
  Deuteronomy 28:58-59 — Same-chapter written law and extension to unlisted plagues: The condition concerns doing the written law and is followed by announced diseases; verse61 extends the threatened infirmities beyond those written in that book.

Deuteronomy 28:62
And you shall be left few in number, whereas you were as the stars of heaven in multitude, because you did not hearken unto the voice of the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 10:22 — Star-like multiplication reversed into diminished numbers: The earlier recital celebrates growth from the Egyptian household into a star-like multitude; this curse explicitly contrasts that same star-like former multitude with being left few.
  Deuteronomy 4:27 — Repeated warning of reduced numbers among nations: Both announce that Israel will be left few, with chapter4 explicitly placing this within scattering among nations and chapter28 naming failure to heed the Lord.

Deuteronomy 28:63
And it shall be, as the Lord rejoiced over you to do you good and to multiply you, so shall the Lord rejoice over you to destroy you; and you shall be removed from the land whither you go to inherit it.
  Deuteronomy 30:9 — Divine rejoicing over prosperity reversed and later renewed: The curse contrasts former rejoicing in good and multiplication with removal and destruction; the restoration promise says the Lord will again rejoice over the people for good.

Deuteronomy 28:64
And the Lord your God shall scatter you among all the nations, from one end of the earth unto the other; and there you shall serve other gods, wood and stone, which neither you nor your fathers have known.
  Deuteronomy 4:27-28 — Repeated dispersion and wooden-and-stone-god service: Both describe scattering among nations and service to other gods made of wood and stone; chapter4 additionally emphasizes those gods' inability to see, hear, eat or smell.

Deuteronomy 28:65
And among those nations He shall give you no rest, neither shall there be a resting place for the sole of your foot; and the Lord shall give you there a faint heart, and failing eyes, and a wasting soul.
  Leviticus 26:36-39 — Fear and wasting in the enemies' land: Both describe faintheartedness, fear and wasting amid foreign peoples or enemies' lands; Leviticus adds terror at a windborne leaf and flight without a pursuer.

Deuteronomy 28:66
And your life shall hang before your eyes, and you shall fear day and night, and shall have no assurance of your life.
  Deuteronomy 28:67 — Same-chapter day-and-night insecurity and its voiced longing: The threatened lack of assurance for life by day and night is developed through wishing each morning for evening and each evening for morning because of fear.

Deuteronomy 28:67
In the morning you shall say: Would that it were evening! And at evening you shall say: Would that it were morning! Because of the fear of your heart wherewith you shall fear, and the sights of your eyes which you shall see.
  Deuteronomy 28:66 — Same-chapter alternating wishes express continual fear: The morning-and-evening wishes express the preceding threat that life will lack assurance and the people will fear continually by day and night.

Deuteronomy 28:68
And the Lord shall bring you back into Egypt in ships, and by the way whereof I said: You shall see it no more. And there shall you be sold unto your enemies for male slaves and female slaves, and there shall be none to buy.
  Deuteronomy 17:16 — Egyptian return contrary to the no-return declaration: Both recall the declaration against returning by the Egyptian way; chapter17 applies it to a ruler's horse policy, while this curse threatens return by ships and enslavement.

Deuteronomy 28:69
These are the words of the covenant which the Lord commanded Moses to establish with the sons of Israel in the land of Moab, besides the covenant which He made with them in Horeb.
  Deuteronomy 5:2-3 — Moab covenant heading distinguished from Horeb covenant: This heading explicitly places the commanded covenant in Moab besides the Horeb covenant; chapter5 recalls the Horeb covenant and addresses its living hearers, without merging the occasions.

Deuteronomy 29:1
And Moses called all the sons of Israel, and said unto them, You have seen all that the Lord did in the land of Egypt before you unto Pharaoh, and unto his servants, and unto all his land:
  Deuteronomy 11:3-4 — Egyptian deeds before the addressed eyewitnesses: Both recall the Lord's deeds against Pharaoh and Egypt before the hearers; chapter11 additionally specifies the pursuing army and the sea's destruction.

Deuteronomy 29:2
the great trials which your eyes have seen, the signs, and those great wonders.
  Deuteronomy 7:19 — Repeated eyewitness description of trials, signs and wonders: Both use great trials, signs and wonders seen by the people's eyes to characterize the Egyptian deliverance, with chapter7 applying their memory against fear of other nations.

Deuteronomy 29:3
And the Lord God has not given you a heart to know, and eyes to see, and ears to hear, unto this day.
  Deuteronomy 29:1-2 — Same-chapter witnessed signs contrasted with ungranted understanding: The statement about not yet receiving heart, eyes and ears to know follows the assertion that the hearers saw the Egyptian signs, preserving the text's deliberate contrast.

Deuteronomy 29:4
And He led you forty years in the wilderness; your garments did not grow old, and your sandals did not wear away from your feet.
  Deuteronomy 8:4 — Forty-year garment preservation with distinct foot descriptions: Both describe garments preserved during forty wilderness years; this verse says sandals did not wear away, while chapter8 says the feet did not become calloused.

Deuteronomy 29:5
You ate no bread, and drank no wine or strong drink, that you might know that He is the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 8:3 — Wilderness food and learning dependence upon God: This verse states that no bread, wine or strong drink was consumed in order to know the Lord; chapter8 describes unfamiliar manna and learning dependence upon God's word, preserving the different food descriptions.

Deuteronomy 29:6
And you came unto this place; and Sihon king of Heshbon and Og king of Bashan came out to meet us in battle, and we smote them.
  Deuteronomy 2:32-33 — Sihon's battle and defeat recalled: Both identify Sihon of Heshbon coming to battle and being struck; chapter2 additionally locates the engagement at Jahaz and attributes delivery to the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 3:1-3 — Og's battle and defeat recalled: Both name Og of Bashan as an opponent defeated by Israel; chapter3 supplies Edrei, the divine assurance and the defeat of his people.

Deuteronomy 29:7
And we took their land, and I gave it as an inheritance unto Reuben, and unto Gad, and unto the half tribe of Manasseh.
  Deuteronomy 3:12-13 — Eastern lands assigned to the same tribes: Both assign the conquered land to Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh; chapter3 gives the particular Gilead, Bashan and Argob distributions.

Deuteronomy 29:8
And you shall take heed to do all the words of this covenant, that you may understand all that you shall do.
  Joshua 1:7-8 — Careful law performance linked with understanding: Both connect keeping and doing the law's words with understanding one's actions; Joshua additionally instructs continual speech and day-and-night meditation upon the law book.

Deuteronomy 29:9
You stand all this day before the Lord your God: your tribal chiefs, and your elders, and your judges, and your recording officers, every man of Israel,
  Deuteronomy 29:10-11 — Same-chapter assembly list extended and its covenant purpose: The chiefs, elders, judges, officers and men standing before the Lord are joined by wives, children and sojourners, with the stated purpose of entering the covenant and curses.

Deuteronomy 29:10
your wives, and your children, and the sojourner who is in the midst of your camp, from your woodcutter unto your water carrier,
  Joshua 9:2f — Whole-community participation including children and resident aliens: Both expressly include women or wives, children and resident aliens in the gathered community; Joshua describes the assembly hearing every commanded word rather than this covenant-entry declaration.

Deuteronomy 29:11
that you may enter into the covenant of the Lord your God, and into His curses, which the Lord your God covenants with you this day;
  Deuteronomy 28:69 — Covenant entry follows the Moab covenant heading: The gathered people enter the Lord's covenant and curses after the heading identifying the commanded Moab covenant as additional to the Horeb covenant.

Deuteronomy 29:12
that He may establish you unto Himself for a people, and He shall be your God, as He said unto you, and as He sware unto your fathers, Abraham, and Isaac, and Jacob.
  Deuteronomy 26:17-19 — Reciprocal divine and communal covenant belonging: Both identify the Lord as Israel's God and Israel as His chosen people; this verse explicitly connects that establishment with the oath to Abraham, Isaac and Jacob.

Deuteronomy 29:13
And not with you alone do I make this covenant and this curse,
  Deuteronomy 29:14 — Same-chapter covenant scope beyond the immediate hearers: The declaration that the covenant and curse concern more than the addressed group is completed by including both those present and those not present this day.

Deuteronomy 29:14
but also with those who are here with us this day before the Lord your God, and with those who are not here with us this day.
  Deuteronomy 5:3 — Different stated descriptions of covenant hearers: Chapter29 includes those present and absent, while chapter5 stresses the living hearers rather than their fathers; the comparison preserves both formulations without identifying every absent party.

Deuteronomy 29:15
For you know how we dwelt in the land of Egypt, and how we passed through the midst of the nations through which you passed.
  Deuteronomy 29:16-17 — Same-chapter travelled nations and observed idolatrous danger: The reminder of Egyptian residence and travel among nations leads into observed idols and the question whether anyone's mind has turned toward serving those nations' gods.

Deuteronomy 29:16
And you saw their abominations and their idols, wood and stone, silver and gold, which are among them.
  Deuteronomy 4:28 — Manufactured idols described by their materials: Both describe gods or idols of wood and stone; chapter29 additionally names silver and gold, while chapter4 states the wooden and stone gods' inability to perceive.

Deuteronomy 29:17
Is there among you a man, or woman, or family, or tribe, whose mind has turned aside from the Lord your God, to go and serve the gods of those nations? Is there among you a root springing up in gall and bitterness?
  Hebrews 12:15 — Root-of-bitterness image reused in communal warning: Deuteronomy warns of a root springing up in gall and bitterness among potential idolaters; Hebrews uses a growing root of bitterness to warn of trouble and widespread defilement.
  Deuteronomy 13:7-9 — Potential other-god defection within intimate community: Both address enticement or turning toward other gods within families and the community; chapter13 gives the particular private-enticer case and commands refusing consent.

Deuteronomy 29:18
And it shall be, if he hears the words of this curse and pronounces a blessing in his heart, saying, May it be well with me, though I walk in the wandering of my heart—so that the sinner may not destroy the guiltless with him—
  Deuteronomy 29:19 — Same-chapter false self-blessing answered by rejected mercy: The person who pronounces wellbeing upon himself while following his wandering heart is answered by the declaration that God will not grant him mercy and covenant curses will cleave.

Deuteronomy 29:19
God will in no wise be willing to be merciful unto him; but then the anger of the Lord and His jealousy shall blaze against that man, and all the curses of this covenant which are written in this book of the law shall cleave unto him, and the Lord shall blot out his name from under heaven.
  Deuteronomy 29:18 — Same-chapter threatened individual judgment answers false assurance: The singular man's threatened erasure and covenant curses answer his preceding self-pronounced blessing, preserving the distinction between his private assurance and the announced response.

Deuteronomy 29:20
And the Lord shall set him apart for evils from all the sons of Israel, according to all the curses of the covenant which are written in this book of the law.
  Deuteronomy 29:19 — Same-chapter particular offender singled out within Israel: The statement that the Lord sets him apart from Israel for evils develops the preceding singular offender's curse and erased name, retaining the individual scope within the communal covenant.

Deuteronomy 29:21
And the generation to come, your sons who shall arise after you, and the foreigner who shall come from a far land, shall speak; and they shall see the plagues of that land and its diseases, which the Lord has sent upon it—
  Deuteronomy 29:22-23 — Same-chapter coming observers and their question about the land: Future descendants and a distant foreigner observe the land's plagues; the following description specifies burned, barren ground and the nations' question about the Lord's anger.

Deuteronomy 29:22
sulfur and salt, burned over; all its land shall neither be sown nor sprout, neither shall any green thing grow upon it, as at the overthrow of Sodom and Gomorrah, Admah and Zeboiim, which the Lord overthrew in wrath and anger—
  Genesis 19:24-25 — Named Sodom-and-Gomorrah overthrow and sulfur judgment: The comparison explicitly names Sodom and Gomorrah; Genesis describes sulfur and fire overthrowing those cities and surrounding growth, while Deuteronomy also names Admah and Zeboiim and describes salt.

Deuteronomy 29:23
and all the nations shall say, Why has the Lord done thus unto this land? What does this great heat of anger mean?
  Deuteronomy 29:24-26 — Same-chapter nations' question and stated covenant explanation: The question why the Lord treated the land this way receives the explicit answer of forsaking His covenant, serving other gods and bringing the written curses upon the land.

Deuteronomy 29:24
And they shall say, Because they forsook the covenant of the Lord, the God of their fathers, which He made with their fathers when He brought them forth out of the land of Egypt;
  Deuteronomy 29:25 — Same-chapter covenant abandonment specified as other-god worship: The declaration that the people forsook their fathers' covenant is immediately developed by their serving and bowing to other gods they did not know.

Deuteronomy 29:25
and they went and served other gods, and bowed down unto them, whom they did not know, and whom He had not allotted unto them.
  Jeremiah 5:19 — Foreign-god service linked with foreign-land servitude: Deuteronomy places other-god service before expulsion from the land; Jeremiah explicitly answers a similar why-question by connecting foreign-god worship at home with serving foreigners abroad.

Deuteronomy 29:26
And the Lord was kindled in wrath against that land, to bring upon it all the curses which are written in this book of the law.
  Deuteronomy 28:15 — Written covenant-curse explanation recalls its earlier announcement: The explanation that the Lord brings this book's curses upon the land corresponds to the earlier announcement that disobedience will cause all these curses to overtake Israel.

Deuteronomy 29:27
And the Lord removed them from their land in wrath, and anger, and exceeding great indignation, and cast them into another land, as it is now.
  Leviticus 26:33 — Parallel threatened removal and scattering from the land: Both describe divine removal or scattering of Israel among foreign lands; Leviticus additionally specifies desolate cities and an advancing sword, while Deuteronomy names wrath and indignation.

Deuteronomy 29:28
The hidden things belong unto the Lord our God; but the things revealed belong unto us and unto our children for ever, that we may do all the words of this law.
  Deuteronomy 30:11-14 — Revealed obligation and nearby performable instruction: The revealed things belong to the people for doing the law; the following chapter describes the command as accessible and the word near enough to perform, without claiming disclosure of hidden things.

Deuteronomy 30:1
And it shall be, when all these things come upon you, the blessing and the curse which I have set before your face, and you receive them into your heart among all the nations whither the Lord has scattered you,
  Deuteronomy 4:27-30 — Return considered within the earlier dispersion warning: Both describe Israel among nations after judgment and a subsequent return toward the Lord; chapter30 expressly calls the blessing and curse to the dispersed people's heart.
  Deuteronomy 11:26-28 — Blessing-and-curse pair recalled at the return instruction: The blessing and curse brought to mind among the nations recall the earlier explicit pairing, where hearing instruction brings blessing and deviation toward other gods brings curse.

Deuteronomy 30:2
and you return unto the Lord your God, and obey His voice according to all that I command you this day, with all your heart and with all your soul,
  Deuteronomy 4:29-30 — Wholehearted turning and renewed hearing in dispersion: Both describe wholehearted seeking or return to the Lord and hearing His voice after distress or dispersion, retaining the distinct seek-and-find and return-and-obey formulations.

Deuteronomy 30:3
then the Lord shall heal your sins, and have mercy upon you, and shall gather you again from all the nations whither the Lord has scattered you.
  Deuteronomy 4:31 — Mercy following the people's return: The promise of mercy and gathering develops the earlier assurance that the merciful Lord will not forsake the people or forget the fathers' covenant after their return.

Deuteronomy 30:4
If your dispersion is from one end of heaven unto the other end of heaven, from thence shall the Lord your God gather you, and from thence shall the Lord your God take you.
  Deuteronomy 28:64 — Gathering reverses widely extended dispersion: The threat scatters Israel from end to end of earth, while the restoration promise gathers even from heaven's farthest extent; their different extent descriptions remain intact.

Deuteronomy 30:5
And the Lord your God shall bring you into the land which your fathers inherited, and you shall inherit it; and He shall do you good, and make you more numerous than your fathers.
  Leviticus 26:42 — Restored ancestral land and remembered patriarchal covenant: Deuteronomy promises renewed inheritance of the fathers' land; Leviticus declares remembrance of the covenants of Jacob, Isaac and Abraham together with the land, without detailing the same return sequence.

Deuteronomy 30:6
And the Lord shall thoroughly cleanse your heart, and the heart of your seed, to love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul, that you may live.
  Deuteronomy 10:16 — Related heart transformation with distinct wording and agency: Chapter10 commands the people to circumcise heart-hardness; chapter30 promises that the Lord will thoroughly cleanse their hearts for wholehearted love and life, preserving both wording and agency.

Deuteronomy 30:7
And the Lord your God shall lay these curses upon your enemies, and upon them that hate you, who pursued you.
  Deuteronomy 7:15 — Related redirection of affliction toward Israel's haters: Both describe affliction laid upon those who hate Israel; chapter7 specifies Egyptian diseases withheld from obedient Israel, while chapter30 transfers the curses within the restoration setting.

Deuteronomy 30:8
And you shall return, and obey the voice of the Lord your God, and do His commandments, all that I command you this day.
  Deuteronomy 30:2 — Same-chapter return and obedient hearing repeated: Both describe returning and obeying the Lord's voice and commandments, with verse2 explicitly naming wholeheartedness and verse8 restating performance of what Moses commands.

Deuteronomy 30:9
And the Lord your God shall care greatly for you in every work of your hands, in the offspring of your womb, and in the produce of your land, and in the offspring of your cattle; for the Lord your God shall again rejoice over you for good, as He rejoiced over your fathers,
  Deuteronomy 7:13 — Repeated human, field and cattle provision: Both promise divine good in human offspring, land produce and cattle offspring, while chapter30 additionally describes renewed rejoicing over the people after return.
  Deuteronomy 28:63 — Renewed divine rejoicing reverses threatened loss: Chapter28 contrasts rejoicing to prosper with rejoicing to destroy and remove; chapter30 explicitly promises that the Lord will again rejoice over the people for good.

Deuteronomy 30:10
if you obey the voice of the Lord your God, to keep and to do all His commandments, and His ordinances, and His judgments which are written in this book of the law; if you return unto the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul.
  Deuteronomy 30:2 — Same-chapter wholehearted return and commanded obedience reiterated: Both combine returning with all heart and soul and obeying the Lord's commands; verse10 expressly identifies the commands, ordinances and judgments as written in this law book.

Deuteronomy 30:11
For this commandment which I command you this day is not excessively burdensome, neither is it far from you.
  Deuteronomy 30:12-14 — Same-chapter accessible-command assertion and explanatory contrasts: The assertion that the command is neither excessively burdensome nor far away is explained through denied heavenly or sea journeys and the word's nearness for performance.

Deuteronomy 30:12
It is not in heaven above, that you should say, Who shall ascend for us into heaven, and fetch it for us, that we may hear it and do it?
  Romans 10:6 — Heavenly-ascent question reused with Christ interpretation: Romans repeats the question of who will ascend into heaven and interprets it as bringing Christ down; Deuteronomy asks about fetching the command so it can be heard and done.

Deuteronomy 30:13
Neither is it beyond the sea, that you should say, Who shall cross for us unto the other side of the sea, and fetch it for us, and make us hear it, that we may do it?
  Deuteronomy 30:12 — Same-chapter parallel rejected journeys to obtain instruction: The proposed sea crossing parallels the heavenly ascent question, each denied as a necessary means of fetching instruction for the people to hear and do.

Deuteronomy 30:14
The word is very near you, in your mouth, and in your heart, and in your hands, that you may do it.
  Romans 10:8 — Near-word statement reused with stated interpretive difference: Romans cites the word near in mouth and heart and calls it the proclaimed word of faith; Deuteronomy also includes hands and expressly relates the nearness to doing it.

Deuteronomy 30:15
Behold, I have set before your face this day life and death, good and evil.
  Deuteronomy 30:19 — Same-chapter life-and-death alternatives restated in the witness appeal: The life-and-death alternatives set before Israel are repeated when heaven and earth are invoked, now accompanied by the explicit appeal to choose life for descendants as well.

Deuteronomy 30:16
If you obey the commandments of the Lord your God which I command you this day, to love the Lord your God, to walk in all His ways, and to keep His ordinances and His judgments, then you shall live and be many; and the Lord your God shall bless you in all the land into which you go to inherit it.
  Deuteronomy 10:12-13 — Love, walking, observance and wellbeing in covenant exhortation: Both combine loving the Lord, walking in His ways and keeping His commands or ordinances for Israel's good; this verse expressly adds multiplication and blessing in the inherited land.

Deuteronomy 30:17
And if your heart turns aside, and you will not hearken, and you are led astray to bow down unto other gods and serve them,
  Deuteronomy 8:19 — Other-god turning and service in the warning condition: Both warn of turning from the Lord toward serving and bowing to other gods, with chapter8 explicitly adding forgetting and the witness against resulting destruction.

Deuteronomy 30:18
I declare unto you this day that you shall surely perish, and you shall in no wise be long-lived upon the land which the Lord your God gives you, into which you are crossing the Jordan to inherit it.
  Deuteronomy 4:26 — Repeated threatened perishing instead of long land life: Both declare perishing and denial of prolonged days upon the land entered across Jordan, retaining the specific contrast between inherited land and shortened tenure.

Deuteronomy 30:19
I call heaven and earth to witness unto you this day: I have set before your face life and death, the blessing and the curse. Choose life, that you and your seed may live,
  Deuteronomy 4:26 — Heaven and earth invoked as witnesses concerning land life: Both call heaven and earth as witnesses concerning Israel's future in the land; chapter30 couples the appeal with choosing life, while chapter4 announces perishing under idolatry.

Deuteronomy 30:20
to love the Lord your God, to obey His voice, and to cleave unto Him; for this is your life and the length of your days, that you may dwell upon the land which the Lord sware unto your fathers, Abraham, and Isaac, and Jacob, to give unto them.
  Deuteronomy 11:21-22 — Love, cleaving and lengthened life in the sworn land: Both connect loving and cleaving to the Lord with prolonged days upon the land sworn to the fathers; chapter11 additionally names sons and walking in His ways.

Deuteronomy 31:1
And Moses finished speaking all these words unto all the sons of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 1:1-3 — Whole-Israel address frames: The notice that Moses finished these words to all Israel corresponds to the opening frame identifying his speech to all Israel and the sons of Israel.

Deuteronomy 31:2
And he said unto them, I am a hundred and twenty years old this day; I shall no longer be able to go in and come out. And the Lord said unto me, You shall not cross this Jordan.
  Deuteronomy 34:7 — Moses' age in final speech and death notice: Both explicitly give Moses' age as one hundred twenty; the death notice additionally describes undimmed eyes and unwasted cheeks, without supplying those details as the reason for this speech's limitation.
  Deuteronomy 3:26-27 — Repeated exclusion from crossing Jordan: Moses reports the Lord's prohibition on crossing Jordan; the earlier answer to his petition likewise refuses crossing while allowing him to look at the land.

Deuteronomy 31:3
The Lord your God, who goes before your face, shall Himself destroy these nations from before your face, and you shall take possession of them; and Joshua shall go before your face, as the Lord has spoken.
  Joshua 1:2-6 — Joshua's promised leadership and subsequent charge: Moses announces Joshua going before Israel into possession; after Moses' death the Lord commands Joshua to cross with the people and apportion the sworn land.

Deuteronomy 31:4
And the Lord shall do unto them as He did unto Sihon and Og, the two kings of the Amorites who were beyond the Jordan, and unto their land, when He destroyed them.
  Deuteronomy 3:21 — The two defeated kings as precedent: Both assure Joshua or Israel that what the Lord did to the two eastern kings will be repeated toward the coming kingdoms; this verse expressly names Sihon and Og.

Deuteronomy 31:5
And the Lord delivered them unto you, and you shall do unto them as I commanded you.
  Deuteronomy 7:1-2 — Delivered nations and previously commanded response: The direction to act toward the delivered nations as commanded points to the earlier instruction describing their delivery and the prescribed response, including the prohibition on covenanting with them.

Deuteronomy 31:6
Be courageous and strong; fear not, neither be fainthearted, neither be terrified before their face; for the Lord your God, who goes before you, is with you and among you. He will in no wise fail you, neither will He forsake you.
  Joshua 1:9 — Courage grounded in divine presence: Both prohibit fear and faintheartedness and require strength and courage because the Lord is with the recipients; Moses addresses Israel, while the later charge addresses Joshua.
  Hebrews 13:5-6 — Non-abandonment assurance applied in a later exhortation: The promise that the Lord will not fail or forsake Israel corresponds to Hebrews' quoted non-abandonment assurance, applied there to contentment and confidence in the Lord's help.

Deuteronomy 31:7
And Moses called Joshua, and said unto him before all Israel, Be courageous and strong; for you shall go before the face of this people into the land which the Lord sware unto our fathers to give unto them, and you shall give it unto them for an inheritance.
  Joshua 1:6 — Joshua's courage and apportioning the ancestral land: Both address Joshua with strength and courage because he will lead the people into the land sworn to the fathers and give it as their inheritance.
  Numbers 27:18-23 — Public charge to Joshua and earlier commissioning: Moses calls Joshua before all Israel for his inheritance role; Numbers narrates the Lord's instruction and Moses' public presentation and laying of hands before Eleazar and the congregation.

Deuteronomy 31:8
And the Lord who goes with you will not fail you, neither will He forsake you; fear not, neither be fainthearted.
  Joshua 1:5 — Assurance to Joshua of continuing divine support: Moses assures Joshua that the Lord will not fail or forsake him; the later divine speech similarly promises not to forsake or disregard him, retaining the different speakers.

Deuteronomy 31:9
And Moses wrote the words of this law in a book, and gave it unto the priests, the sons of Levi, who bear the ark of the covenant of the Lord, and unto the elders of the sons of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 17:18-19 — Written law associated with Levitical priests: Moses writes the law and gives it to the Levitical priests and elders; the royal instruction requires a written second-law scroll from those priests for continual reading.

Deuteronomy 31:10
And Moses commanded them in that day, saying, After seven years, at the appointed time of the year of release, at the Feast of the Pitching of Tents,
  Deuteronomy 15:1-2 — Seven-year release as the public-reading calendar: The reading is appointed after seven years in the year of release; the earlier law specifies that seven-year release and explains the remission of a brother's debt.
  Deuteronomy 16:16 — Tents appearance as the public-reading occasion: The reading appointment names the Feast of the Pitching of Tents; the earlier annual-appearance list includes that feast among the occasions at the chosen place.

Deuteronomy 31:11
when all Israel comes together to appear before the Lord your God in the place which the Lord shall choose, you shall read this law before all Israel in their hearing.
  Joshua 9:2e — Commanded public law reading and later narrated reading: Moses commands reading this law in Israel's hearing; the actual Joshua continuation unit records Joshua reading all its words, blessings and curses, without identifying it as the seven-year observance.

Deuteronomy 31:12
Gather the people, the men, and the women, and the children, and the sojourner who is in your cities, that they may hear, and that they may learn to fear the Lord your God; and they shall hearken to do all the words of this law.
  Psalms 77:5-7 — Communal transmission of law for later generations: The gathered adults, children and sojourner must hear, learn and obey; the psalm similarly describes a testimony and law transmitted to sons so they remember God and seek His commands.

Deuteronomy 31:13
And their sons, who do not know, shall hear and learn to fear the Lord your God all the days that they live upon the land into which you are crossing the Jordan to inherit it.
  Deuteronomy 6:6-7 — Children learning the commanded words: Children who do not yet know must hear and learn fear of the Lord; the earlier command places the words in the parents' heart and soul and requires teaching sons daily.

Deuteronomy 31:14
And the Lord said unto Moses, Behold, the days of your death draw near; call Joshua, and stand beside the doors of the Tent of Testimony, and I will command him. And Moses and Joshua went unto the Tent of Testimony, and stood beside the doors of the Tent of Testimony.
  Numbers 27:15-23 — Approaching succession and Joshua's appointment: The Lord directs Moses to bring Joshua to the Tent as death approaches; Numbers records Moses requesting a successor and the Lord appointing Joshua with a public charge.

Deuteronomy 31:15
And the Lord descended in a cloud, and stood beside the doors of the Tent of Testimony; and the pillar of the cloud stood beside the doors of the Tent.
  Exodus 33:9-10 — Cloud descending and standing at the tent entrance: Both describe the cloud pillar descending or standing at the Tent's entrance in connection with divine speech, preserving the respective narrative occasions and witnesses.

Deuteronomy 31:16
And the Lord said unto Moses, Behold, you shall sleep with your fathers; and this people shall arise and go a-whoring after the foreign gods of the land into which it goes; and they shall forsake Me, and break My covenant which I made with them.
  Exodus 34:14-16 — Foreign-god prostitution warned against and forecast: The forecast describes Israel forsaking the covenant and prostituting itself to the land's gods; Exodus had forbidden other-god worship and warned against those inhabitants' cult and marriage enticements.

Deuteronomy 31:17
And I will be kindled in wrath against them in that day, and will forsake them, and turn My face from them; and the people shall be devoured, and many evils and afflictions shall find it. And it shall say in that day, Because the Lord my God is not within me, these evils have found me.
  Deuteronomy 32:20 — Turning the divine face from an unfaithful people: The announced withdrawal of the Lord's face amid afflictions corresponds to the song's declaration that He will turn His face from faithless sons, preserving each statement's particulars.

Deuteronomy 31:18
But I will surely turn My face from them in that day, because of all the wickedness which they have done, for they have turned unto foreign gods.
  Deuteronomy 31:16-17 — Same-chapter stated cause of divine withdrawal: The repeated face-turning announcement explicitly grounds it in foreign-god wickedness, continuing the forecast of covenant abandonment and ensuing wrath and afflictions.

Deuteronomy 31:19
And now write the words of this song, and teach it unto the sons of Israel, and put it in their mouth, that this song may be a witness for Me among the sons of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 32:44 — Song-writing and teaching command and repeated execution notice: The Lord commands writing the song and teaching it to Israel; the later notice expressly says Moses wrote and taught the song before describing its public delivery.

Deuteronomy 31:20
For I will bring them into the good land which I sware unto their fathers to give unto them, a land flowing with milk and honey; and they shall eat, and be filled, and be sated; and they shall turn unto foreign gods, and serve them, and provoke Me, and break My covenant.
  Deuteronomy 8:12-14 — Prosperity and fullness followed by forgetting the giver: The forecast of eating and becoming sated before turning to other gods develops the earlier warning that full meals and multiplied possessions may lead Israel to forget its deliverer.

Deuteronomy 31:21
And this song shall stand against them, bearing witness before their face; for it shall in no wise be forgotten from their mouth, nor from the mouth of their seed. For I know their wickedness, all that they do here this day, before I bring them into the good land which I sware unto their fathers.
  Deuteronomy 31:19 — Same-chapter song's enduring testimony and its commissioned purpose: The song's standing as a witness before Israel and its descendants develops the earlier instruction to write, teach and put it in their mouths for that purpose.

Deuteronomy 31:22
And Moses wrote this song in that day, and taught it unto the sons of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 32:44 — Repeated notice of writing and teaching the song: Both explicitly record Moses writing the song that day and teaching it to the sons of Israel; the later unit also names Joshua in the public delivery.

Deuteronomy 31:23
And Moses commanded Joshua, and said unto him, Be courageous and strong; for you shall bring the sons of Israel into the land which the Lord sware unto them, and He shall be with you.
  Deuteronomy 31:7-8 — Same-chapter renewed charge to Joshua: Moses again charges Joshua to be strong and courageous for bringing Israel into the sworn land, repeating the earlier public charge and assurance of the Lord's presence.

Deuteronomy 31:24
And when Moses had finished writing all the words of this law in a book, even unto the end,
  Deuteronomy 31:26 — Same-chapter completed book and ordered deposit: The completion of writing the law in a book introduces the instruction to place that book beside the covenant ark as a witness against Israel.

Deuteronomy 31:25
he commanded the Levites who bear the ark of the covenant of the Lord, saying,
  Deuteronomy 31:9 — Same-chapter recipients and ark-bearing custodians: The instruction addresses Levites bearing the covenant ark; the earlier handover names priests who are Levi's sons and bear that ark, while also including elders among the recipients.

Deuteronomy 31:26
Take this book of the law, and lay it beside the ark of the covenant of the Lord your God; and it shall be there for a witness against you.
  Deuteronomy 31:19 — Same-chapter written book and taught song as witnesses: The law book beside the ark and the song taught into Israel's mouths are each explicitly assigned a witness role, without identifying their different forms or deposits as identical.

Deuteronomy 31:27
For I know your provocation and your stiff neck; for while I yet live with you this day, you have been provoking God: how much more after my death!
  Deuteronomy 9:6-7 — Stiff-necked provocation during Moses' lifetime: Moses grounds his forecast in Israel's present stiff neck and provocation; the earlier reminder likewise calls them stiff-necked and recounts continuing wilderness disobedience.

Deuteronomy 31:28
Gather unto me your tribal chiefs, and your elders, and your judges, and your recording officers, that I may speak all these words in their hearing, and call heaven and earth to witness unto them.
  Deuteronomy 30:19 — Heaven and earth invoked as covenant witnesses: Moses gathers tribal officials to hear words witnessed by heaven and earth; the preceding life-and-death choice likewise expressly calls heaven and earth to witness against the hearers.

Deuteronomy 31:29
For I know that after my death you shall deal utterly lawlessly, and turn aside from the way which I commanded you; and evils shall meet you in the latter days, because you shall do that which is evil before the Lord, to provoke Him to anger by the works of your hands.
  Judges 2:10-15 — Forecast apostasy and a later narrated foreign-god turn: Moses forecasts future lawlessness, doing evil and resulting afflictions; Judges narrates a later generation forsaking the Lord for surrounding gods and suffering enemies, without making the death notices simultaneous.

Deuteronomy 31:30
And Moses spoke in the hearing of all the assembly of Israel the words of this song, even unto the end:
  Deuteronomy 32:1-4 — Public-song introduction and opening words: The notice of Moses speaking the complete song before Israel leads into the actual appeal to heaven and earth and its opening proclamation of the Lord's greatness and justice.

Deuteronomy 32:1
Give ear, O heaven, and I will speak; and let the earth hear the words of my mouth.
  Deuteronomy 31:28 — Heaven-and-earth hearing and witness summons: The song asks heaven and earth to hear Moses' words, following his explicit plan to speak before Israel's officials and call heaven and earth as witnesses.

Deuteronomy 32:2
Let my utterance be awaited as the rain, and let my words descend as the dew, as a shower upon the grass, and as snow upon the herbage.
  Isaiah 55:10-11 — Rain and snow as comparisons for effective spoken words: Moses compares his utterance with rain, dew, shower and snow nourishing vegetation; Isaiah compares the Lord's accomplishing word with rain and snow producing growth, retaining the different speakers and claims.

Deuteronomy 32:3
For I have called upon the name of the Lord; ascribe greatness unto our God.
  Psalms 95:7-8 — Divine name and commanded attribution of greatness or glory: Calling upon the Lord's name leads to ascribing greatness to God; the psalm similarly commands the nations' families to bring glory to the Lord and His name.

Deuteronomy 32:4
God—true are His works, and all His ways are judgments; a faithful God, and there is no unrighteousness; righteous and holy is the Lord.
  Psalms 91:16 — Explicit denial of injustice in the Lord: Both proclaim the Lord upright or righteous and explicitly deny unrighteousness or injustice in Him; Deuteronomy additionally describes His true works, faithful character and judgments.

Deuteronomy 32:5
Blemished children, not His, have sinned—a crooked and perverse generation.
  Philippians 2:14-16 — Children, blemish and a crooked generation in contrasting exhortations: The song describes blemished children and a crooked, perverse generation; Paul urges blameless children of God amid a crooked generation, retaining the contrast rather than identifying the audiences.

Deuteronomy 32:6
Do you thus repay the Lord, O foolish people and unwise? Is not He Himself your Father, who acquired you, and made you, and created you?
  Deuteronomy 32:18 — Same-chapter divine parenthood and forgotten nurture: The rebuke asks whether the Lord is the Father who acquired and made the people; the later rebuke says they forsook the God who begat and nourishes them.

Deuteronomy 32:7
Remember the days of old; consider the years of generation upon generation. Ask your father, and he shall declare unto you; your elders, and they shall tell you.
  Psalms 77:3-6 — Ancestors recounting divine deeds to later generations: The song tells hearers to ask fathers and elders about former generations; the psalm describes fathers recounting known deeds and transmitting testimony to children and children yet unborn.

Deuteronomy 32:8
When the Most High divided the nations, when He scattered the sons of Adam, He set the bounds of the nations according to the number of the angels of God.
  Acts 17:26 — Divine ordering of nations and their boundaries: Both describe God determining nations' dwelling boundaries; Deuteronomy additionally specifies the number of God's angels, while Acts speaks of humanity from one and appointed times without that angelic clause.
  Deuteronomy 32:9 — Same-chapter nations' allotment and Jacob's particular portion: The division and bounds of the nations are immediately followed by Jacob becoming the Lord's portion, contrasting the general allotment with this specifically named inheritance.

Deuteronomy 32:9
And His people Jacob became the portion of the Lord; Israel, the measured lot of His inheritance.
  Deuteronomy 4:20 — Israel described as the Lord's inheritance: Jacob or Israel is the Lord's measured inheritance; the earlier recital similarly describes the people brought from Egypt as God's people of inheritance.

Deuteronomy 32:10
He sustained him in a wilderness land, in the thirst of burning heat, in a waterless place; He compassed him about, and instructed him, and kept him as the pupil of an eye.
  Deuteronomy 8:15-16 — Divine sustenance in the thirsty wilderness: Both recall the Lord sustaining Israel in a waterless, thirsty wilderness; chapter8 specifies water from rock and manna while the song describes encircling, instruction and guarding.
  Psalms 16:8 — Protection pictured through the pupil of an eye: The song recalls Israel being guarded as an eye's pupil; the psalm petitions that same particular form of guarding, joined there to shelter beneath divine wings.

Deuteronomy 32:11
As an eagle would shelter its nest, it yearned over its young; spreading its wings, it received them, and took them up upon its back.
  Exodus 19:4 — Eagle imagery for carrying Israel: The eagle spreads its wings and carries its young in the song; the earlier divine recital says the Lord bore Israel as upon eagles' wings and brought them to Himself.

Deuteronomy 32:12
The Lord alone led them, and there was no foreign god with them.
  Exodus 13:21-22 — The Lord's guidance of the delivered people: The song attributes Israel's guidance to the Lord alone without a foreign god; Exodus narrates God leading them by the continuing cloud and fire pillars.

Deuteronomy 32:13
He brought them up upon the strength of the earth, and fed them the produce of the fields; they sucked honey out of the rock, and oil out of the solid rock,
  Psalms 80:17 — Honey from rock and divine feeding: Both describe divine feeding and honey from rock; the psalm pairs it with finest wheat, while the song also describes field produce and oil from solid rock.

Deuteronomy 32:14
butter of cattle and milk of sheep, with the fat of lambs and rams, young bulls and goats, with the fat of the kernels of wheat; and they drank wine, the blood of the grape.
  Genesis 49:11 — Wine called the blood of the grape: Both use the specific blood-of-the-grape expression for wine; the song describes Israel drinking it, while Jacob's Judah oracle describes washing a mantle in it.
  Psalms 80:17 — Rich wheat as divine provision: The song's rich wheat kernels among Israel's provisions correspond to the psalm's finest-wheat feeding, without adding the song's livestock and wine details to the psalm.

Deuteronomy 32:15
And Jacob ate and was filled, and the beloved one kicked; he grew fat, he grew thick, he grew broad; and he forsook God who made him, and departed from God his Saviour.
