  Deuteronomy 8:12-14 — Eating, fullness and forsaking the giver: Jacob's eating, fullness and fatness before forsaking his maker develop the earlier warning against full meals, multiplied possessions and an exalted heart forgetting the delivering Lord.

Deuteronomy 32:16
They provoked Me with foreign gods; with their abominations they embittered Me.
  Deuteronomy 31:20 — Foreign gods provoking God after received provision: The song says foreign gods and abominations provoked the Lord; the preceding forecast likewise joins the people's satisfaction in the good land with foreign-god service and provocation.

Deuteronomy 32:17
They sacrificed unto demons, and not unto God, unto gods whom they did not know; new ones, lately come, whom their fathers did not know.
  1 Corinthians 10:20 — Sacrifice to demons rather than God: Both explicitly say that the sacrifices are to demons and not to God; Paul applies this contrast to participation in sacrificial meals and warns against fellowship with demons.
  Psalms 105:37-39 — Demonic sacrifice and Israel's cultic unfaithfulness: Both describe sacrifice to demons; the psalm specifically names sons and daughters and Canaan's images, details not supplied by this song unit's warning about newly known gods.

Deuteronomy 32:18
You forsook God who begat you, and forgot God who nourishes you.
  Deuteronomy 32:6 — Same-chapter divine parenthood and ungrateful forgetting: Forgetting the God who begat and nourishes Israel answers the earlier rebuke about repaying the Father who acquired, made and created them in this manner.

Deuteronomy 32:19
And the Lord saw, and was jealous, and was provoked by the anger of His sons and daughters.
  Deuteronomy 32:16-17 — Same-chapter jealous response to the specified provocations: The Lord's jealous provocation at His sons and daughters follows the particular foreign-god abominations and demonic sacrifices just named, preserving the causal progression of the song.

Deuteronomy 32:20
And He said, I will turn My face from them, and show what shall befall them at the last; for they are a perverse generation, sons in whom is no faithfulness.
  Deuteronomy 31:17-18 — Repeated withdrawal of the divine face: The song's decision to turn the Lord's face from faithless sons repeats the preceding forecast of face-turning amid foreign-god wickedness and resulting afflictions.

Deuteronomy 32:21
They made Me jealous with that which is no god; they provoked Me to anger with their idols. And I will make them jealous with that which is no nation; with a nation without understanding will I provoke them to anger.
  Romans 10:19 — Moses' jealousy-and-nation words cited by Paul: Paul expressly introduces Moses and repeats the warning about making Israel jealous by what is not a nation and provoking anger through a senseless nation.
  1 Corinthians 10:20-22 — Demonic sacrifice and provocation of divine jealousy: The song connects no-god idols with provoking the Lord's jealousy; Paul's sacrificial-meal warning likewise ends by asking whether the hearers are provoking the Lord to jealousy.

Deuteronomy 32:22
For a fire has been kindled out of My wrath; it shall burn unto Hades beneath, devour the earth and its produce, and set ablaze the foundations of the mountains.
  Psalms 17:8-9 — Divine wrath, fire and mountain foundations: Both portray divine anger with fire and the foundations of mountains; the song describes their burning, while the psalm describes shaking and smoke before God's descent.

Deuteronomy 32:23
I will gather evils against them, and spend My arrows upon them.
  Deuteronomy 32:42 — Same-chapter threatened arrows and their later blood imagery: The threat to spend divine arrows upon the people corresponds to the later image of arrows drunk with blood, within the song's developing judgment and vengeance imagery.

Deuteronomy 32:24
They shall waste away with hunger, and be devoured by birds, and suffer incurable backward-bending spasms; I will send against them the teeth of wild beasts, with the fury of things that crawl upon the ground.
  Leviticus 26:21-26 — Covenant judgment through hunger and wild creatures: Both threaten hunger and destructive wild animals among covenant penalties; the song adds birds and crawling creatures, while Leviticus adds livestock loss, depopulation and weighed bread.

Deuteronomy 32:25
Without, the sword shall bereave them; and from the inner chambers, terror: the young man together with the maiden, the nursing child with the aged elder.
  Leviticus 26:25 — Sword judgment within covenant penalties: Both threaten a sword against the disobedient people; Deuteronomy also names inner terror and victims across ages, while Leviticus describes flight into cities and enemy control.

Deuteronomy 32:26
I said, I will scatter them; I will make their remembrance cease from among men,
  Deuteronomy 28:64 — Scattering as a covenant judgment: The song's threat to scatter the people corresponds to the earlier curse's explicit scattering among nations; the song adds threatened loss of remembrance, while the curse names foreign-god service.

Deuteronomy 32:27
were it not for the wrath of enemies, lest they endure long, and lest the adversaries join in the attack; lest they say, Our hand is exalted, and the Lord has not done all these things.
  Exodus 32:11-14 — Adversaries' possible misreading considered amid judgment: The song considers enemies crediting their own hand rather than the Lord; Moses' intercession similarly invokes what Egypt might say, though the particular hostile interpretation and plea differ.

Deuteronomy 32:28
For it is a nation that has lost counsel, and there is no knowledge in them.
  Deuteronomy 32:29 — Same-chapter lack of counsel and failure of understanding: The statement that the nation lacks counsel and knowledge is developed by its failure to understand these things and the appeal concerning the coming time.

Deuteronomy 32:29
They did not have the wisdom to understand these things; let them receive them for the time to come.
  Deuteronomy 32:28 — Same-chapter wisdom failure and preceding diagnosis: The lack of wisdom to understand follows the diagnosis of lost counsel and knowledge, linking two specified failures of discernment rather than supplying an unstated identity for the nation.

Deuteronomy 32:30
How shall one pursue a thousand, and two put tens of thousands to flight, unless God had sold them, and the Lord had delivered them up?
  Leviticus 26:7-8 — Unequal pursuit numbers with reversed covenant outcome: The song explains extraordinary defeat by God's handing the people over; Leviticus promises Israel extraordinary pursuit success, giving five against a hundred and a hundred against tens of thousands rather than identical ratios.

Deuteronomy 32:31
For their gods are not as our God; and our enemies are without understanding.
  Deuteronomy 32:39 — Same-chapter incomparable God and exclusive divine power: The assertion that the opponents' gods are unlike Israel's God corresponds to the later declaration of no god beside Him and His uncontestable power over death, life, wounds and healing.

Deuteronomy 32:32
For their vine is of the vine of Sodom, and their tendril is of Gomorrah; their grape is a grape of gall, their cluster is bitterness.
  Deuteronomy 32:33 — Same-chapter bitter vine and venomous wine imagery: The Sodom-and-Gomorrah vine with gall grapes and bitter clusters is followed by wine described as serpent and asp venom, developing the particular poisoned-produce imagery without a literal vineyard identification.

Deuteronomy 32:33
Their wine is the venom of serpents, and the incurable venom of asps.
  Deuteronomy 32:32 — Same-chapter venomous wine and its bitter grape description: The wine's serpent and asp venom develops the preceding vine, gall-grape and bitter-cluster image, retaining the stated Sodom and Gomorrah association without identifying a modern substance.

Deuteronomy 32:34
Behold, are not these things gathered with Me, and sealed in My treasuries?
  Deuteronomy 32:35 — Same-chapter reserved things and approaching repayment: The things gathered and sealed in God's treasuries are followed by the announcement of a near destruction and timed vengeance; the link does not supply an unexpressed inventory of those treasuries.

Deuteronomy 32:35
In the day of vengeance I will repay, in the season when their foot shall slip; for the day of their destruction is near, and the things prepared for you are at hand.
  Romans 12:19 — Divine repayment language cited against private revenge: The song announces divine vengeance and repayment; Romans quotes the Lord's repayment claim as its reason for forbidding personal revenge, retaining the different application and surrounding wording.
  Hebrews 10:30 — Vengeance and repayment in a later judgment warning: Hebrews quotes the divine vengeance-and-repayment declaration within its warning about deliberate sin, corresponding to the song's announcement of repayment at the approaching judgment.

Deuteronomy 32:36
For the Lord shall judge His people, and be moved concerning His servants; for He saw them weakened, and failing under assault, and exhausted.
  Hebrews 10:30 — The Lord judging His people in a later citation: Hebrews expressly quotes the declaration that the Lord will judge His people; the song continues with His response to weakened servants, a continuation not repeated in the citation.
  Psalms 134:14 — Judgment of God's people and response concerning His servants: Both combine the Lord judging His people with His being moved or comforted concerning His servants, preserving the different supplied expressions and the song's further description of their exhaustion.

Deuteronomy 32:37
And the Lord said, Where are their gods, in whom they trusted,
  Judges 10:13-14 — Unhelpful chosen gods challenged during affliction: The song asks where the trusted gods are; Judges likewise confronts Israel for abandoning the Lord and tells it to seek deliverance from its chosen gods.

Deuteronomy 32:38
whose sacrifices’ fat you ate, and whose drink offerings’ wine you drank? Let them arise and help you, and become your protectors.
  Judges 10:14 — Challenge that the chosen gods provide rescue: Both challenge the foreign gods to rise or save in affliction; the song additionally refers to eating sacrificial fat and drinking libation wine before asking for their protection.

Deuteronomy 32:39
See, see that I am, and there is no god beside Me. I will put to death, and make alive; I will smite, and I will heal; and there is none that shall deliver out of My hands.
  1 Samuel 2:6 — The Lord's power to put to death and give life: Both explicitly attribute death and life to the Lord; the song additionally names wounding, healing and the impossibility of rescue from His hand, while Hannah's song names descent to Hades and return.

Deuteronomy 32:40
For I will lift My hand unto heaven, and swear by My right hand, and say, I live for ever.
  Numbers 14:28 — Divine living declaration accompanying an oath or judgment: The Lord declares His eternal life while lifting His hand and swearing; Numbers likewise introduces a judgment with the declaration that He lives, without importing the hand gesture there.

Deuteronomy 32:41
For I will sharpen My sword as lightning, and My hand shall lay hold of judgment; and I will repay a penalty unto the enemies, and unto them that hate Me will I repay.
  Deuteronomy 32:35 — Same-chapter divine repayment and sharpened judgment weapon: The sharpening sword and grasp of judgment develop the earlier announcement of divine vengeance and repayment, now explicitly directed toward enemies and those who hate Him.

Deuteronomy 32:42
I will make My arrows drunk with blood, and My sword shall devour flesh, from the blood of the slain and of captives, from the head of the rulers of the enemies.
  Deuteronomy 32:23 — Same-chapter blood-filled arrows and earlier threat: The arrows drunk with blood return to the earlier threat to expend God's arrows, now joined with a flesh-devouring sword and named slain, captives and enemy rulers.

Deuteronomy 32:43
Rejoice, O heavens, together with Him, and let all the sons of God worship Him; rejoice, O nations, with His people, and let all the angels of God give Him strength; for He avenges the blood of His sons, and shall avenge and repay a penalty unto the enemies, and unto them that hate Him shall He repay; and the Lord shall thoroughly cleanse the land of His people.
  Romans 15:10 — Nations rejoicing with God's people in a later citation: Romans explicitly quotes the call for Gentiles to rejoice with God's people, corresponding to the song's call for nations to rejoice with His people within its longer heavenly and vengeance declaration.
  Hebrews 1:6 — Heavenly worship language with different named agents: Hebrews calls all God's angels to worship Him; the song calls God's sons to worship and His angels to give strength, so the related heavenly summons is retained without making the clauses identical.

Deuteronomy 32:44
And Moses wrote this song in that day, and taught it unto the sons of Israel. And Moses came in and spoke all the words of this law in the hearing of the people, he and Joshua the son of Nun.
  Deuteronomy 31:22 — Repeated song-writing and teaching notice: Both say Moses wrote this song that day and taught it to Israel; this later unit further describes Moses and Joshua son of Nun addressing the people.

Deuteronomy 32:45
And Moses finished speaking unto all Israel,
  Deuteronomy 32:46-47 — Same-chapter completion notice and concluding admonition: Moses' finishing his speech introduces the demand to heed these words, command them to children and recognize them as life for Israel in the land.

Deuteronomy 32:46
and said unto them, Give heed in your heart unto all these words which I testify unto you this day, which you shall command your sons to keep and to do, even all the words of this law.
  Deuteronomy 6:6-7 — Words held inwardly and taught to children: Both require the commanded words within the hearers and their transmission to sons; chapter6 specifies heart and soul and daily teaching settings, while this closing command stresses doing the whole law.

Deuteronomy 32:47
For this is no empty word unto you; for this is your life, and by this word you shall prolong your days upon the land into which you are crossing the Jordan to inherit it.
  Deuteronomy 30:19-20 — Words, obedience and life in the inherited land: Both connect the commanded allegiance and words with Israel's life and lengthened days in the land; chapter30 frames this as choosing life and cleaving to the Lord.

Deuteronomy 32:48
And the Lord spoke unto Moses in that day, saying,
  Deuteronomy 32:49-52 — Same-chapter divine speech introduction and actual mountain command: The notice that the Lord spoke that day introduces the specific instruction to ascend Nebo, view the land and die there, with its stated exclusion and reason.

Deuteronomy 32:49
Go up into this mountain of Abarim, Mount Nebo, which is in the land of Moab over against Jericho, and behold the land of Canaan, which I give unto the sons of Israel for a possession.
  Numbers 27:12-14 — Nebo ascent and viewing the land before death: Both command Moses to ascend the mountain identified as Nebo and see Canaan, followed by death and the stated failure to sanctify God at the disputed water.
  Deuteronomy 34:1 — Nebo viewing command and narrated ascent: The command names Nebo in Moab opposite Jericho for viewing the land; the final narrative names that mountain, location and Moses' ascent before God shows the land.

Deuteronomy 32:50
And die in the mountain whither you go up, and be gathered unto your people, as Aaron your brother died in Mount Hor, and was gathered unto his people;
  Numbers 20:25-29 — Aaron's Mount Hor death as the explicit comparison: Moses' commanded death is expressly compared with Aaron's death on Hor; Numbers narrates Aaron ascending Hor, transferring garments to Eleazar and dying on its summit.

Deuteronomy 32:51
because you disobeyed My word among the sons of Israel at the water of dispute at Kadesh in the wilderness of Zin, because you did not sanctify Me among the sons of Israel.
  Numbers 20:12-13 — Exclusion grounded in failure to sanctify at the disputed water: Both connect Moses' exclusion with not sanctifying the Lord before Israel at the Water of Dispute; the song's closing notice additionally names Kadesh and the wilderness of Zin.

Deuteronomy 32:52
For you shall see the land from over against it, but you shall not go in thither.
  Deuteronomy 34:4 — Viewing without entry announced and repeated: The Lord permits seeing the land while prohibiting entry; the concluding narrative repeats both conditions after identifying it as the land promised to the patriarchs' seed.

Deuteronomy 33:1
And this is the blessing wherewith Moses, the man of God, blessed the sons of Israel before his death.
  Genesis 49:28 — Distinct final blessings upon Israel's sons: Moses' pre-death blessing of Israel's sons corresponds structurally to Jacob's individualized blessings of his twelve sons, preserving the different speakers and each oracle's contents.

Deuteronomy 33:2
And he said, The Lord has come from Sinai, and appeared unto us from Seir, and hastened from Mount Paran with myriads of Kadesh; at His right hand were angels with Him.
  Exodus 19:16-20 — Sinai divine appearance with differing accompanying descriptions: The blessing recalls the Lord coming from Sinai among other named places and angels; Exodus narrates His Sinai descent amid cloud, fire and trumpet, without adding the Seir, Paran or Kadesh clauses.

Deuteronomy 33:3
And He spared His people; and all the consecrated ones are under Thy hands; and these are under Thee; and the people received from His words
  Deuteronomy 33:4 — Same-chapter received divine words and the Mosaic law: The people receiving from God's words leads directly into the law commanded by Moses and described as Jacob's assemblies' inheritance; the consecrated-under-hand language is preserved without an added identity.

Deuteronomy 33:4
the law which Moses commanded us, an inheritance for the assemblies of Jacob.
  Deuteronomy 4:44-45 — Mosaic law set before Israel: Both identify the law Moses gave or set before Israel, with chapter4 additionally naming testimonies, ordinances and judgments in the wilderness setting.

Deuteronomy 33:5
And there shall be a ruler in the beloved, when the rulers of the peoples are gathered together with the tribes of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 31:28 — Related tribal leadership gatherings with different functions: The gathering of peoples' rulers and Israel's tribes corresponds to Moses' summons of tribal chiefs and officials to hear covenant testimony; the ruler in this blessing remains unnamed rather than independently identified.

Deuteronomy 33:6
Let Reuben live, and not die; and let him be many in number.
  Genesis 49:3-4 — Corresponding Reuben oracles with different emphases: Both individually address Reuben within blessings of Israel's sons; Moses prays for life and numbers, whereas Jacob recalls firstborn standing and the offense against his bed, without making the evaluations identical.

Deuteronomy 33:7
And this for Judah: Hear, O Lord, the voice of Judah, and let them enter unto his people; his hands shall contend for him, and Thou shalt be a helper against his enemies.
  Genesis 49:8 — Judah's hands and opposition to enemies: Both Judah oracles refer to his hands in conflict with enemies and relate him to his people or brothers; Moses asks the Lord's help rather than repeating Jacob's brothers-bowing clause.

Deuteronomy 33:8
And unto Levi he said, Give unto Levi his manifestations, and his truth unto the holy man, whom they tested in a trial, and reviled at the water of dispute;
  Exodus 28:30 — Manifestation and truth in Levi's blessing and Aaron's vestment: Levi's blessing names manifestations and truth; the vestment instruction places Manifestation and Truth on Aaron's judgment piece, without identifying the blessing's tested holy man beyond the supplied text.

Deuteronomy 33:9
who says unto his father and his mother, I have not seen you; and he did not acknowledge his brethren, and disowned his sons. He kept Thine oracles, and preserved Thy covenant.
  Exodus 32:26-29 — Levitical allegiance despite close family relationships: The blessing describes setting aside kin recognition to keep God's words and covenant; Exodus names Levi's sons obeying Moses' Lord-command despite sons and brothers, preserving the narrative's concrete action.

Deuteronomy 33:10
They shall declare Thine ordinances unto Jacob, and Thy law unto Israel; they shall lay incense in Thy wrath, continually upon Thine altar.
  Leviticus 10:8-11 — Teaching Israel divine ordinances: Levi's blessing assigns declaring the law and ordinances to Israel; the earlier instruction specifically addresses Aaron and his sons and requires their teaching the Lord's statutes given through Moses.
  Exodus 30:7-8 — Incense ministry in blessing and altar instructions: The blessing places incense at the altar in its ministry description; Exodus commands Aaron's morning and evening incense as a continuing observance, without replacing the blessing's wrath wording.

Deuteronomy 33:11
Bless, O Lord, his strength, and accept the works of his hands; break the loin of the enemies who rise against him, and let not them that hate him arise.
  Deuteronomy 33:8-10 — Same-chapter named Levitical ministry and petition for acceptance: The request that the Lord bless his strength, accept his work and defeat opponents follows the named Levi blessing and its particular teaching and incense ministry.

Deuteronomy 33:12
And unto Benjamin he said, He who is beloved of the Lord shall dwell in confidence; and God overshadows him all the days, and between his shoulders He rested.
  Deuteronomy 33:27 — Same-chapter protective bodily imagery in confident dwelling: The Benjamin oracle joins confident dwelling, God's overshadowing and rest between shoulders; the closing shelter beneath everlasting arms supplies a related protective bodily image without identifying a sanctuary site or resolving the shoulder pronoun.

Deuteronomy 33:13
And unto Joseph he said, From the blessing of the Lord is his land, from the seasons of heaven and the dew, and from the springs of the depths beneath,
  Genesis 49:25 — Joseph's blessing from heaven and earth: Both Joseph blessings join divine help or blessing with heaven above and earth or depths below; Moses specifies dew and springs while Jacob also names breasts and womb.

Deuteronomy 33:14
and in the season of the produce of the sun’s turnings, and from the conjunctions of the months,
  Deuteronomy 33:13 — Same-chapter seasons and sources of Joseph's land fertility: The produce associated with sun-turnings and months continues Joseph's land blessing from heaven's seasons, dew and deep springs, without converting the supplied seasonal language into a modern calendar scheme.

Deuteronomy 33:15
and from the summit of the ancient mountains, and from the summit of the everlasting hills,
  Genesis 49:26 — Mountains in corresponding Joseph blessings: Both Joseph blessings name ancient or enduring mountains; Moses also names everlasting hills, whereas Genesis names everlasting dunes, preserving those different landform descriptions.

Deuteronomy 33:16
and in the season of the fullness of the earth. And may the things acceptable unto Him who appeared in the bush come upon the head of Joseph, and upon the crown of him who was glorified among his brethren.
  Exodus 3:2-6 — The one appearing in the bush and the narrated revelation: Joseph's blessing invokes acceptance from the one appearing in the bush; Exodus narrates the angelic appearance and the Lord calling from that bush and identifying the patriarchs' God.
  Genesis 49:26 — Joseph's head and crown in corresponding blessings: Both direct blessing upon Joseph's head and crown and relate him to his brothers; Moses says glorified among them, whereas Genesis describes brothers whom he led.

Deuteronomy 33:17
His beauty is that of a firstborn bull; his horns are the horns of a one-horned animal; with them shall he gore the nations together unto the end of the earth. These are the myriads of Ephraim, and these are the thousands of Manasseh.
  Genesis 48:19-20 — Ephraim and Manasseh in blessings of relative prominence: Moses names Ephraim's myriads and Manasseh's thousands; Jacob likewise places Ephraim before Manasseh and promises the younger greater prominence, without turning either passage into an exact census.

Deuteronomy 33:18
And unto Zebulun he said, Rejoice, Zebulun, in your going forth; and Issachar, in his dwellings.
  Genesis 49:13-15 — Paired Zebulun and Issachar oracles with distinct settings: Moses pairs Zebulun's going out with Issachar's dwellings; Jacob's corresponding blessings name Zebulun's maritime dwelling and Issachar's good rest and agricultural toil, preserving their differing descriptions.

Deuteronomy 33:19
They shall destroy nations; and you shall call upon God there, and sacrifice a sacrifice of righteousness; for the wealth of the sea shall nourish you, and the commerce of them that dwell by the coast.
  Genesis 49:13 — Coastal wealth following the Zebulun and Issachar blessing: The preceding paired blessing develops into sea wealth and coastal commerce; Jacob's Zebulun oracle explicitly names a sea dwelling and ship haven, without making Moses' nations-and-sacrifice clauses part of it.

Deuteronomy 33:20
And unto Gad he said, Blessed is He that enlarges Gad; as a lion he rested, having crushed an arm and a ruler.
  Genesis 49:19 — Corresponding Gad combat blessings with different images: Both Gad oracles use combat language; Moses pictures lion-like rest after crushing an arm and ruler, whereas Jacob describes raiders and Gad's answering raid, without equating their images or opponents.

Deuteronomy 33:21
And he saw his first portion; for there was apportioned the land of rulers gathered together with the leaders of the peoples. The Lord wrought righteousness, and His judgment with Israel.
  Numbers 32:20-22 — Gad's first portion and eastern land with military obligation: The Gad blessing recalls a first land portion with leaders and divine judgment; Numbers connects the eastern recipients' possession with armed service before the Lord, without identifying every phrase of the poetic description.

Deuteronomy 33:22
And unto Dan he said, Dan is a lion’s cub, and shall leap forth from Bashan.
  Genesis 49:16-17 — Corresponding Dan oracles using distinct animal images: Both individually address Dan among Israel's tribes; Moses compares him with a lion's cub leaping from Bashan, while Jacob describes a judging tribe and ambushing serpent, preserving the different images and place wording.

Deuteronomy 33:23
And unto Naphtali he said, Naphtali, a fullness of acceptable things; and let him be filled with blessing from the Lord; he shall inherit the sea and the south.
  Genesis 49:21 — Corresponding Naphtali blessings with different images: Moses blesses Naphtali with fullness and the sea-and-south inheritance; Jacob pictures Naphtali as a spreading, fruit-bearing tree, without identifying a modern boundary or substituting an animal for that tree.

Deuteronomy 33:24
And unto Asher he said, Asher is blessed with children, and shall be acceptable unto his brethren; he shall dip his foot in oil.
  Genesis 49:20 — Asher's prosperity in corresponding tribal blessings: Moses' Asher blessing includes oil and acceptance among brothers; Jacob's Asher oracle names rich bread and rulers' delicacies, preserving the distinct images of provision rather than making their clauses identical.

Deuteronomy 33:25
His sandal shall be iron and bronze; and as your days, so shall your strength be.
  Deuteronomy 33:24 — Same-chapter Asher foot-and-sandal blessing continuation: The iron-and-bronze sandal and strength declaration follow Asher's foot-in-oil blessing, linking the explicit foot and footwear images without rewriting them as a gate or geographical prediction.

Deuteronomy 33:26
There is none like the God of the beloved, who rides upon heaven as your helper, the majestic One of the firmament.
  Psalms 67:34-36 — Heaven-riding God and strength for Israel: Both portray God riding the heavens and giving Israel power or help; the psalm also speaks of His powerful voice and magnificence over Israel, preserving the separate poetic details.

Deuteronomy 33:27
And there is shelter from the God of old, and you are under the strength of everlasting arms; and He shall drive out the enemy from before your face, saying, May you perish.
  Psalms 89:1-2 — Everlasting God as refuge through generations: The blessing describes shelter from the God of old and everlasting arms; the prayer identifies God as refuge across generations and existing from age to age before mountains were formed.

Deuteronomy 33:28
And Israel shall dwell in confidence, alone upon the land of Jacob, with grain and wine; and the heaven for him shall be clouded with dew.
  Leviticus 26:3-5 — Agricultural provision and secure dwelling: Both connect fruitful land and divine moisture provision with dwelling securely; the blessing names grain, wine and dew, while Leviticus' obedience-conditioned promise names seasonal rain and successive harvests.

Deuteronomy 33:29
Blessed are you, O Israel! Who is like unto you, a people saved by the Lord? Your helper shall shield you, and the sword is your boast; and your enemies shall lie unto you, and you shall tread upon their neck.
  Psalms 113:17 — The Lord as Israel's helper and defender: The blessing calls Israel a people saved by the Lord, its shielding helper; the psalm specifically names Israel's house hoping in the Lord as helper and defender.

Deuteronomy 34:1
And Moses went up from Araboth of Moab unto Mount Nebo, unto the summit of Pisgah, which is over against Jericho; and the Lord showed him all the land of Gilead unto Dan,
  Deuteronomy 32:49 — Commanded Nebo ascent and narrated land viewing: Moses ascends Nebo in Moab opposite Jericho and sees the land, corresponding to the earlier command naming that mountain, location and viewing purpose, with Pisgah added in this narrative.

Deuteronomy 34:2
and all the land of Naphtali, and all the land of Ephraim and Manasseh, and all the land of Judah unto the farthest sea,
  Deuteronomy 34:1 — Same-chapter regional continuation of the land panorama: The Naphtali, Ephraim, Manasseh and Judah regions continue the land God began showing Moses after his Nebo ascent, extending the named panorama without a modern visibility calculation.

Deuteronomy 34:3
and the wilderness, and the country round about Jericho, the city of palms, unto Zoar.
  Deuteronomy 34:1 — Same-chapter continuation of the narrated land viewing: The wilderness and Jericho-to-Zoar region continue the panorama introduced as God showing Moses the land after his ascent; no modern sightline or separate site identity is certified.

Deuteronomy 34:4
And the Lord said unto Moses, This is the land which I sware unto Abraham, and Isaac, and Jacob, saying, I will give it unto your seed. And I have showed it unto your eyes, but you shall not go in thither.
  Genesis 12:7 — Land-to-seed promise first addressed to Abram: The final declaration identifies the viewed land as the sworn gift to the patriarchs' seed; Genesis records the Lord telling Abram that He will give this land to his seed.
  Genesis 26:3-4 — Land-to-seed promise renewed to Isaac: The final declaration names Isaac among the fathers whose seed receives the land; the Lord's Isaac speech promises land to him and his seed while reaffirming Abraham's oath.
  Genesis 35:10-12 — Land-to-seed promise renewed to Jacob: The final declaration names Jacob alongside Abraham and Isaac; Genesis expressly gives Jacob the land granted to those two fathers and promises it to his seed after him.

Deuteronomy 34:5
And Moses, the household servant of the Lord, died in the land of Moab by the word of the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 32:50 — Moses' commanded death and concluding death notice: The Lord had commanded Moses to die on the mountain and be gathered to his people; the conclusion records His household servant dying in Moab by the Lord's word.
  Joshua 1:1-2 — Moses' death and Joshua's opening succession notice: The death of Moses as the Lord's servant leads into Joshua's account opening after that death and the Lord explicitly declaring His servant dead before commanding Joshua's crossing.

Deuteronomy 34:6
And they buried him in Gai, in the land of Moab, near the house of Peor; and no man knows his burial place unto this day.
  Jude 1:9 — Moses' body in distinct death-related notices: Deuteronomy records Moses' burial and the unknown grave; Jude separately describes Michael's dispute with the devil about Moses' body, without identifying the grave, burial agents or circumstances beyond either text.

Deuteronomy 34:7
And Moses was a hundred and twenty years old when he died; his eyes were not dimmed, neither were his cheeks wasted away.
  Deuteronomy 31:2 — Repeated age with distinct final-condition descriptions: Both give Moses' age as one hundred twenty; the earlier speech says he can no longer go out and in, while the death notice states undimmed eyes and unwasted cheeks without harmonizing the descriptions.

Deuteronomy 34:8
And the sons of Israel wept for Moses in Araboth of Moab, at the Jordan over against Jericho, thirty days; and the days of mourning and weeping for Moses were ended.
  Numbers 20:29 — Thirty-day communal mourning for Moses and Aaron: Both describe Israel weeping thirty days for a named departing leader; Deuteronomy locates Moses' mourning in Moab by Jordan, while Numbers names Aaron and the whole house of Israel.

Deuteronomy 34:9
And Joshua the son of Nun was filled with a spirit of understanding; for Moses had laid his hands upon him; and the sons of Israel hearkened unto him, and did as the Lord commanded Moses.
  Numbers 27:18-23 — Joshua's spirit and Moses' laying on of hands: The conclusion connects Joshua's spirit of understanding with Moses' hands upon him; Numbers narrates the command and public laying on of hands, describing him there as a man with spirit.
  Joshua 1:16-17 — Israel's heeding Joshua and its subsequent response: The death conclusion says Israel heeded Joshua; the later narrative records the eastern tribes answering his commands with promised obedience, retaining their particular audience rather than equating them with all Israel.

Deuteronomy 34:10
And there arose no more a prophet in Israel like unto Moses, whom the Lord knew face to face,
  Exodus 33:11 — Moses' face-to-face relation with the Lord: Both expressly describe the Lord's face-to-face relation with Moses; Exodus additionally compares speaking with a friend and names Joshua's continuing presence at the tent.
  Numbers 12:6-8 — Moses distinguished from other prophetic experience: The conclusion says no comparable prophet arose and names face-to-face knowledge; Numbers distinguishes Moses' mouth-to-mouth speech from prophetic dreams, visions and riddles, preserving the different expressions.

Deuteronomy 34:11
in all the signs and wonders which the Lord sent him to do in the land of Egypt unto Pharaoh, and unto his servants, and unto all his land,
  Exodus 7:3-5 — Egyptian signs and wonders in commission and final recollection: The conclusion recalls signs and wonders sent against Pharaoh and Egypt; Exodus announces their multiplication and the delivering mighty action, preserving the speakers and servants detail of the conclusion.

Deuteronomy 34:12
the great wonders and the mighty hand which Moses wrought before all Israel.
  Deuteronomy 4:34 — Mighty hand and wonders before Israel's eyes: Both recall great wonders and a mighty hand witnessed by Israel, with chapter4 expressly identifying the Egyptian deliverance and adding trials, war and an uplifted arm.
  Exodus 14:30-31 — Concluding mighty-hand recollection and sea-deliverance witness: The conclusion recalls mighty action before Israel; the sea account specifically says Israel saw the Lord's great hand, was rescued and trusted God and Moses, without making Moses the ultimate source of power.
